- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 3 / 1 / 1. Confidence: 5%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Filings are cheap and defensive (companies file dozens of names they never use), launch timing routinely slips past the quarter, and a launch is not a success; the signal is probably a weak proxy for marketing activity that is already in guidance.
- Red team (reviewer): Trademark intensity is a published annual mispricing factor that loads on growth and marketing-spend characteristics, not an event-quarter nowcast; intent-to-use filings are cheap and defensive, launches slip across quarters and a launch is not a success; and certificate-transparency subdomain spikes mostly reflect IT infrastructure, so the combined signal is a noisy proxy for firm growth that is already in guidance.

## S29 Statuspage incident-hours and developer-forum complaints for infrastructure SaaS

- Category: Digital. Sector focus: Usage-based infrastructure and communications SaaS, hosting and CDN, collaboration SaaS, restaurant and commerce platforms. Target: margin + guidance (reliability spend, credits) (nowcast).
- Seed item: Status-page outage frequency and support-forum complaint volume for SaaS
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: The mechanism yields an immaterial quarterly effect: SLA credits are small contra-revenue, churn from outages shows up in later quarters, and reliability investment is rarely quantified in guidance. Large outages are priced on the day they occur and discussed on the call, so the pre-earnings window carries no incremental information. Incident counts measure each company's disclosure policy and component granularity more than reliability, and that policy itself changes over time, which contaminates the within-company comparison. Aggregated outage history already exists cheaply (IsDown, StatusGator) for anyone who wanted it, and no study or vendor links it to earnings. The data are free and lawful, but there is nothing worth the effort; at most, incident-hours could be appended as a free secondary feature to S27 if that survives.
- Mechanism: Public status pages (Statuspage.io pattern) log every incident with start and resolve timestamps and affected components. For usage-based platforms, a quarter with materially more incident-hours implies SLA credits (contra-revenue), elevated churn risk and higher infrastructure spend (margin), and sustained incident clusters precede customer-loss disclosures; Hacker News and Reddit complaint volume adds the customer reaction. The effect on a single quarter's revenue is small in most cases, so the realistic target is margin and guidance commentary on reliability investment. REASONED; no literature.
- Industry context: Infrastructure SaaS sells uptime; customers receive contractual credits when it fails and shop elsewhere when it fails repeatedly. Status pages are public commitments, so reliability is observable at incident level.
- Measurement window: Fiscal quarter start to T-1: count of incidents, total incident-hours weighted by impact level, and share affecting core components from the company's status page (50 most recent per call; older via /history, TESTED HERE), plus HN story and comment counts mentioning outages. History: Statuspage keeps multi-year incident history per page (REASONED); HN full corpus.. Lead time: 1 days.
- Universe coverage: ~6% (about 80 names) have a public Statuspage-style page, but incident-hours are material to a single quarter's revenue or margin for almost none of them.
- Hypothesis: An increase of at least 2x in impact-weighted incident-hours on a company's public status page over the fiscal quarter versus its trailing 4-quarter average, measured 1 trading day before earnings, predicts a negative operating-margin surprise and cautious reliability-related guidance, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Outages are news; large ones are priced immediately and discussed on calls. Nobody is known to sell incident-hours as a cross-sectional earnings signal (UNKNOWN). Sell-side does not model it.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): None known (UNKNOWN); commercial synthetic-monitoring vendors sell reliability data to IT buyers, not to funds.. Why ours differs: Free and unsold; the differentiation is irrelevant if the signal is too weak, which is the main risk.
- Vendors found by reviewer: IsDown, aggregated and normalized outage data as a service (3,550+ official status pages, historical outages, API; sold to IT/ops, not funds) - https://isdown.app/status-page-aggregator-as-a-service; StatusGator competitive-intelligence monitoring of competitors' status pages (IT/ops product) - https://statusgator.com/use-cases/competitive-intelligence; No fund-facing vendor and no paper linking status-page incident-hours to earnings or returns (UNKNOWN)
- Literature found by reviewer: No published paper found on status-page outage frequency and earnings or stock returns (UNKNOWN)
- Data sources:
  - Statuspage.io public API pattern (per company) [DATA] https://status.twilio.com/api/v2/incidents.json (update: live; history: 50 most recent per call; older via /history (TESTED HERE))
  - GitHub Status (pattern example) [DATA] https://www.githubstatus.com/api/v2/incidents.json (update: live; history: )
  - Statuspage.io index page [DATA] https://www.statuspage.io/ (update: ; history: )
  - Hacker News Algolia search API [DATA] https://hn.algolia.com/api/v1/search?query=fastly%20outage&tags=story (update: live; history: full corpus)
  - Reddit Data API [KEY] https://www.reddit.com/api/v1/access_token (update: live; registered script app, terms flag; history: )

Would push legal to 3 if kep…
- Cost: $0: Statuspage APIs are official and keyless; HN free; Reddit free with a registered app.
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 4 / 1 / 1. Confidence: 4%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Incident counts reflect disclosure policy more than reliability (some companies post everything, others nothing), the quarterly revenue effect of credits is immaterial, and big outages are already priced on the day.
- Red team (reviewer): Incident counts reflect disclosure policy and component granularity, not reliability; the quarterly revenue effect of SLA credits is immaterial; large outages are priced the same day and discussed on the call; and the within-company change is confounded by changes in status-page practice, so any residual correlation is noise.

## S30 Website diffs: removed products, softened claims, deleted customer logos

- Category: Digital. Sector focus: B2B software and services with customer-logo pages; consumer brands with product catalogs; med-tech and industrial firms with product pages. Target: revenue (churn or discontinuation); secondary distress (nowcast).
- Seed item: Wayback Machine diffs: removed products, softened claims, deleted customer logos
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: There is no backtestable history: Wayback content is blocked and Common Crawl captures a given page irregularly at roughly monthly resolution, so a deletion cannot be placed inside a quarter relative to the earnings date, which is the whole hypothesis. A forward-only daily crawl of ~1,300 sites would need two or more years before any test, with a material share of sites behind Cloudflare challenges (seen in this pass) and per-site terms to check. The event rate (a few dozen per quarter) is too low and too idiosyncratic to separate from analyst revisions and size, and most edits are redesigns, legal housekeeping and logo-permission lapses. Lower signal_noise to 1.
- Mechanism: Companies edit their websites before they disclose: a logo removed from the customers page often means a lost account (churn and a revenue miss for B2B software), a product page removed means a discontinued line (revenue and inventory write-down), and softened performance or regulatory claims can precede legal or regulatory trouble. Diffing daily snapshots of a fixed set of pages per company (customers, products, investor FAQ, careers) against the prior snapshot and flagging deletions converts these edits into dated events ahead of the earnings release. The Wayback Machine is blocked from the container, so history must come from Common Crawl (monthly-ish) and our own daily crawl going forward. REASONED; no literature.
- Industry context: Marketing teams update public pages as soon as a customer or product relationship ends, long before finance quantifies it in a filing. A removed logo is a small, public, dated fact about churn.
- Measurement window: Own crawl: daily diffs from quarter start to T-1; events = count of logo deletions, product-page removals and claim softening (keyword level) in the quarter. Backfill: Common Crawl WARC fetches at roughly monthly resolution from 2008 (TESTED HERE for the index and the byte-range fetch pattern). The Wayback availability API works (TESTED HERE) but snapshot content on web.archive.org is blocked.. Lead time: 1 days.
- Universe coverage: ~50% of the universe has a logo or catalog page, but datable material deletions are a few dozen per quarter across the universe, too few for a cross-sectional test.
- Hypothesis: The deletion of at least one named customer logo (or a disclosed product line) from a company's website during the fiscal quarter, measured 1 trading day before earnings, predicts a negative revenue surprise and a negative T-1 to T+1 sector-adjusted abnormal return relative to companies with no deletions, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Some hedge funds run website-change monitoring, and Thinknum has sold website-derived datasets (REASONED, unverified for logo tracking). Sell-side does not. Customer losses are usually disclosed on the call, so the edge is the pre-call window, which is real but the event rate is low.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): None confirmed for logo and claim diffs (UNKNOWN); general web-change monitoring tools exist for IT, not for funds.. Why ours differs: Free, daily, company-specific page sets with explicit deletion events rather than aggregate web metrics; the Common Crawl backfill is slower than Wayback but lawful and usable from the container.
- Vendors found by reviewer: Thinknum Alternative Data (web-scraped datasets including online inventory, job listings and product pricing; 300+ fund clients per 2021 report; nothing seen on customer-logo or claim diffs) - https://www.nasdaq.com/articles/thinknums-simple-plan-use-alternative-data-change-world-2016-05-05; Thinknum profile and client base (Integrity Research) - https://www.integrity-research.com/thinknum-raises-11-6-million/; No vendor seen selling logo-deletion or claim-softening events (UNKNOWN)
- Literature found by reviewer: No published paper found on website deletions (logos, product pages, softened claims) and earnings surprises (UNKNOWN)
- Data sources:
  - Common Crawl index collinfo [DATA] https://index.commoncrawl.org/collinfo.json (update: monthly-ish crawls; history: 2008 to Oct 2026 (TESTED HERE))
  - Common Crawl WARC and CDX data [DATA] https://data.commoncrawl.org/crawl-data/CC-MAIN-2026-43/warc.paths.gz (update: per crawl; history: per-crawl indexes and WARCs back to 2008 (TESTED HERE))
  - Wayback availability API (snapshot existence only; content blocked) [DATA] https://archive.org/wayback/available?url=allbirds.com&timestamp=20240601 (update: live; history: )
  - Shopify storefront products.json (product removals) [DATA] https://www.allbirds.com/products.json?limit=2 (update: live snapshot; history: )
  - Own daily crawl of company pages (robots.txt-respecting, per site) [UNPROBED] https://<company>.com/customers (update: ; history: )

- Cost: $0 plus storage; Common Crawl is free; own crawling must respect robots.txt and site terms (legal 2-3 depending on site).
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 4 / 1 / 2. Confidence: 5%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Website edits are mostly redesigns and legal housekeeping; logo removals reflect permission lapses rather than churn; and the handful of true events per quarter is too few and too idiosyncratic to backtest with any power.
- Red team (reviewer): Website edits are overwhelmingly redesigns, legal housekeeping and logo-permission lapses rather than churn; without Wayback there is no datable history, so the backtest cannot exist for years; and the handful of true events per quarter is too few and too idiosyncratic to distinguish from the known factors.

## S31 Unscheduled executive departures (8-K Item 5.02) before earnings; lawful LinkedIn substitute

- Category: Digital. Sector focus: All sectors; strongest for small and mid-caps where the CFO/controller function is thin. Target: EPS surprise + guidance; secondary distress (both).
- Seed item: LinkedIn departure clusters concentrated in one department
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: 1) Remove LinkedIn entirely (ToS-prohibited and Cloudflare-challenged). 2) Detect 5.02 events from the data.sec.gov submissions 'items' field and the daily index rather than the blocked efts full-text search, then fetch and classify the 8-K text (departure vs appointment, officer role, successor named, effective date, 'pursue other opportunities' language). 3) Add CEO departures to the event set and require 'unscheduled' plus 'no named permanent successor' as hard filters, because the vendors (Audit Analytics, Revelio) and sell-side already price plain departures; crowdedness lowered to 2. 4) Demote the ATS backfill-postings leg to a live-only secondary feature since job boards have no history and cannot enter the backtest. 5) Control for consensus revisions dated after the 8-K, because the earnings-day effect may just be the delayed analyst-revision channel; if the effect disappears with that control, the signal is a revision proxy and should be dropped. 6) Run the 2005-2025 backtest (several thousand events, $0) before any live use; this is the one candidate in the set with full lawful history available today.
- Mechanism: LinkedIn departure clustering is ToS-prohibited and blocked, so the lawful substitute is regulatory exhaust: Form 8-K Item 5.02 (departure of principal officers) filed within four business days, plus Form 4 insider-sale clustering and ATS postings for backfill roles (Controller, VP Finance, Head of Sales). An unscheduled CFO, chief accounting officer or chief revenue officer departure in the 90 days before an earnings release, especially without a named successor, signals internal disagreement over the numbers or a sales miss and predicts a negative EPS surprise, guidance cuts and later restatements; a cluster of backfill postings for one department is the public-ATS analogue of a LinkedIn departure cluster. REASONED; LITERATURE on executive turnover around restatements (Hennes, Leone and Miller, The Accounting Review, 2008) is background rather than a direct test of next-quarter surprise.
- Industry context: Finance and sales chiefs are the people who know whether the quarter is on track, and they leave abruptly when they disagree with how it will be reported or see the miss coming. The SEC forces disclosure within days, creating a dated public event.
- Measurement window: Rolling 90 days ending T-1: 8-K 5.02 departure events classified (departure versus appointment, officer role, successor named, 'to pursue other opportunities' language), Form 4 net selling by officers, and count of senior backfill postings per department on the company's ATS. History: 8-K index back to 2004 via the EDGAR daily index (REASONED); Form 4 bulk from 2006q1 (TESTED HERE for the file list).. Lead time: 1 days.
- Universe coverage: ~100% of the universe files 8-Ks; unscheduled CFO/CAO/CRO/CEO departures are roughly 3-5% of firm-quarters (REASONED), giving on the order of 40-60 events per quarter live and several thousand events in a 2005-2025 backtest.
- Hypothesis: An 8-K Item 5.02 reporting the departure of the CFO, chief accounting officer, chief revenue officer or CEO, classified as unscheduled (no prior announcement, no named permanent successor, effective within 30 days), filed within 90 days before an earnings release, predicts a negative EPS surprise and a negative T-1 to T+2 sector-adjusted abnormal return versus sector- and size-matched firms, controlling for consensus revisions made after the filing date, sector, size and momentum; the hypothesis is backtested on 2005-2025 EDGAR history before any trade.
- Who already knows: CFO departures are headline events priced on the filing day; sell-side notes them. Revelio Labs and similar sell profile-derived headcount and departure series to funds (REASONED), so department-level attrition is partly priced for large caps. The gap is in the conditional read (departure plus no successor plus insider selling plus backfill postings) in mid-caps few analysts follow.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Revelio Labs (workforce dynamics from professional-profile data; REASONED), LinkUp and Thinknum for postings.. Why ours differs: Lawful regulatory exhaust rather than LinkedIn scraping; faster (8-K within 4 business days, atom feed in real time) and cheaper; narrower (officers only) so it cannot see rank-and-file clusters, which is the honest limitation.
- Vendors found by reviewer: Revelio Labs, workforce alternative data for investors (profile-derived headcount, seniority mix, key departures; hedge-fund clients) - https://www.reveliolabs.com/solutions/investors; Audit Analytics, 'CEO and CFO Departures: 2016-2018' (practitioner data vendor; notes over half of 8-Ks give no reason for departure) - https://blog.auditanalytics.com/ceo-and-cfo-departures-2016-2018/; MixRank hedge-fund data (profile and firmographic data) - https://mixrank.com/solutions/hedge-funds-data/
- Literature found by reviewer: Hennes, Leone and Miller 2008, 'The Importance of Distinguishing Errors from Irregularities in Restatement Research: The Case of Restatements and CEO/CFO Turnover', The Accounting Review 83(6) (verified; background on turnover and irregularities, not next-quarter surprise) - https://www.kellogg.northwestern.edu/faculty/research/detail/2008/the-importance-of-distinguishing-errors-from-irregularities-in-restatement; the-cfo.io 2023, 'CFO exit can result in a share drop of over 3%' (secondary write-up: -1% next day, -2% over 30 days, recovered by 180 days; underlying paper not identified) - https://the-cfo.io/2023/12/19/cfo-exit-can-result-in-a-share-drop-of-over-3-research/; University of Washington Foster summary of McVay research: sudden CFO or CEO death is followed by an earnings decline the next year - https://foster.uw.edu/?p=102614; Academic listing: forced CFO turnover announcements have negative and significant cumulative abnormal returns - https://scholars.utoledo.edu/display/n197584; No study found that directly tests CFO/CRO departure against the next earnings-announcement surprise or reaction (UNKNOWN)
- Data sources:
  - EDGAR submissions API (8-K list with item codes) [DATA (Phase 0)] https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000320193.json (update: live; history: recent window plus paginated older filings)
  - EDGAR latest 8-K atom feed [DATA (Phase 0)] https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=8-K&count=100&output=atom (update: real time; history: rolling latest window)
  - EDGAR daily index (backfill) [DATA (Phase 0)] https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/daily-index/2025/QTR4/ (update: daily; history: 2004 onward for 8-K item-based filings (REASONED))
  - SEC Form 3/4/5 insider transactions bulk [DATA (Phase 0)] https://www.sec.gov/files/structureddata/data/insider-transactions-data-sets/2025q2_form345.zip (update: quarterly; history: 2006q1 through 2026q3)
  - Greenhouse / Lever / Ashby job boards (live-only secondary) [DATA (Phase 0)] https://boards-api.greenhouse.io/v1/boards/duolingo/jobs?content=false (update: live; history: none)
- Reviewer source checks: https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=8-K&count=100&o… -> DATA (Phase 0) Real-time latest filings; needs declared User-Agent.; https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/daily-index/2025/QTR4/ -> DATA (Phase 0) Backfill by year and quarter; form.idx lists 8-Ks but not item codes.; https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000320193.json -> DATA (Phase 0) Per-company filing list includes an 'items' field per 8-K (e.g. 5.02), which re…; https://www.sec.gov/files/structureddata/data/insider-transactions-data-sets/20… -> DATA (Phase 0) Form 4 bulk 2006q1 through 2026q3.; https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=4&count=10&outp… -> DATA (Phase 0) Real-time Form 4 feed.; https://boards-api.greenhouse.io/v1/boards/duolingo/jobs?content=false -> DATA (Phase 0) Current postings only, no history; the backfill-postings leg is forward-only an…; https://www.linkedin.com/company/allbirds -> 403 Cloudflare challenge (TESTED HERE) Homepage returns 200 but company pages are challenged, and the LinkedIn User Ag…
- Cost: $0: SEC data is free (declared User-Agent, under 10 req/s). Vendor workforce data (Revelio Labs) is well above $100/month.
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 15%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): The departure is priced on the filing day, the subsequent earnings reaction is noise, and most departures are benign (retirement, promotion elsewhere); a small event count makes the backtest fragile.
- Red team (reviewer): The departure is priced on the filing day and any subsequent earnings-day drift may simply be the analyst-revision channel (post-filing estimate cuts) or a distress and size effect; most departures are benign (retirement, promotion elsewhere) and the text classification of 'unscheduled, no successor' is noisy; and Audit Analytics and Revelio already sell departure data, so large-cap pricing is efficient and the residual, if any, sits in thinly covered mid-caps with a small event count.

## S32 Prior-call Q&A evasiveness and hedging language

- Category: Digital. Sector focus: All sectors with scripted calls; most informative where guidance is specific (industrials, consumer, software). Target: EPS surprise + guidance (forward).
- Seed item: Earnings-call language: hedging words, evasiveness in Q&A, tone shifts quarter over quarter
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: Fully published (Hollander et al. 2010; Chen, Rui and Whinston 2025 find the exact next-quarter earnings effect) and widely sold (AlphaSense, S&P Global, Aiera, Quartr), so crowdedness is 1 and the residual in mid-caps is speculative. The reading is 85-95 days stale by the event, violating the spirit of requirement (b). The only $0 bulk transcript source (Motley Fool) carries an explicit terms conflict for bulk scraping (AMBER, legal 3), API Ninjas' free tier cannot cover ~1,300 names, and the lawful EDGAR substitutes contain no Q&A, so the hypothesis cannot be tested lawfully at scale within budget. Including it 'as a control variable' does not justify building it.
- Mechanism: The only call available before this quarter's announcement is last quarter's, so the measurable quantity is management's linguistic behaviour 85-95 days earlier: non-answers in Q&A (question-to-answer length ratio, deflection phrases), hedging and uncertainty word density, and the quarter-over-quarter change in tone between prepared remarks and Q&A. LITERATURE: Larcker and Zakolyukina (Journal of Accounting Research, 2012) find linguistic features of executives' call answers predict subsequent restatements; Hollander, Pronk and Roelofsen (JAR, 2010) find that withholding answers on calls is associated with negative subsequent information; Loughran and McDonald (Journal of Finance, 2011) provide the financial-domain word lists. REASONED that these features carry some information about the next quarter's surprise, but they are stale by the event and widely harvested by sell-side NLP. This is the second permitted sentiment-type idea.
- Industry context: Managers who know the next quarter is at risk answer analysts' forward-looking questions less directly, and the pattern is measurable because calls are transcribed verbatim. The literature finds the effect is small and decays as it becomes known.
- Measurement window: The prior quarter's call transcript (available within a day of that call, roughly 85-95 days before the next release): Q&A evasiveness score, hedging density, and QoQ change versus the company's own prior four calls. Transcripts: Motley Fool pages are readable but bulk scraping is a ToS question (TESTED HERE flag); API Ninjas has a free tier with redistribution rights unclear.. Lead time: 85 days.
- Universe coverage: ~90% of the universe holds calls, but a lawful $0 bulk transcript source covering mid-caps does not exist, so effective coverage for a cross-sectional test is near zero.
- Hypothesis: A one-standard-deviation increase in Q&A evasiveness (deflection share and hedging density) on the prior quarter's call relative to the company's own trailing four calls, measured 85 days before the next earnings release, predicts a negative EPS surprise and a negative T-1 to T+1 sector-adjusted abnormal return, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Sell-side quant desks and many vendors run NLP on transcripts; the hedging and evasiveness factor has been published for over a decade, so it is widely replicated and largely priced in large caps. Residual edge, if any, is in mid-caps and in the within-company QoQ change rather than levels.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Widely sold by sell-side quant research and transcript-NLP vendors (specific vendor names not asserted; UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: It does not differ in kind; it is free and could focus on within-company QoQ change in Q&A evasiveness for mid-caps. Given crowdedness and the 85-day staleness, this is a low-priority candidate to include mainly as a control variable.
- Vendors found by reviewer: AlphaSense Sentiment Indices and transcript sentiment scores (sold to hedge funds; -100 to +100 scoring) - https://www.alpha-sense.com/press/alphasense-launches-sentiment-indices-to-identify-early-signals-of-market-confidence-and-risk/; S&P Global Textual Data Suite / NLP research on earnings-call transparency and sentiment for stock selection (hedge-fund case study) - https://www.casestudies.com/company/sp-global/case-study/searching-for-alpha-with-textual-data; S&P Global, 'NLP III' earnings-call NLP research note - https://spglobal.com/content/dam/spglobal/mi/en/documents/general/NLP-III-Final-013020-10a.pdf; Aiera and Quartr transcript data feeds (per 2026 vendor comparison) - https://allmind.ai/research/earnings-call-sentiment-tracking-platforms
- Literature found by reviewer: Hollander, Pronk and Roelofsen 2010, 'Does Silence Speak? An Empirical Analysis of Disclosure Choices During Conference Calls', Journal of Accounting Research 48(3), 531-563 (verified) - https://ideas.repec.org/a/bla/joares/v48y2010i3p531-563.html; Chen, Rui and Whinston, Information Systems Research 36(1) 2025: machine-learned evasiveness and incoherence in S&P 500 Q&A 2006-2018 predict worse next-quarter earnings - https://ideas.repec.org/a/inm/orisre/v36y2025i1p440-455.html; Kurucak, AEA/AFA 2026 program paper: managerial response time on calls does not predict earnings surprises, though analysts revise down after hesitation - https://swlb1.aeaweb.org/conference/2026/program/paper/ttKSsQ7Z; EvasionBench (arXiv 2601.09142, 2026): large-scale benchmark for detecting evasive answers on earnings calls - https://arxiv.org/html/2601.09142v2; Larcker and Zakolyukina 2012 (cited by the candidate) was not surfaced in this pass; not verified here

- Red team (reviewer): The feature has been published since 2010, replicated in 2025 with the precise next-quarter earnings effect, is sold by AlphaSense and S&P Global to quant desks and is three months stale by the event, so it is fully priced; residual language features proxy for size, volatility and analyst coverage; and the only $0 bulk transcript source carries a terms-of-use conflict (AMBER).

## S33 Supplier-first disclosures: early-reporting suppliers flagging cuts from a major customer

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: Customers of small listed suppliers: industrial OEMs, auto and truck OEMs, consumer durables, telecom and cable equipment buyers, med-tech, semis and electronics. Target: revenue + guidance (nowcast).
- Seed item: Small supplier earnings calls that mention a cut from their largest customer
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: (1) Replace bulk Motley Fool scraping (ToS conflict) with the 8-K Item 2.02 press-release text from EDGAR as the primary event source, plus API Ninjas free-tier transcripts (needs a free API Ninjas key) for the few flagged calls. (2) EDGAR full-text search worked from the container today (200, 4,883 hits) despite the Phase 0 block; use it as the keyword screen but keep the Notes datasets as fallback since availability is intermittent. (3) Tighten the event definition: customer must be named at >=10% in the supplier's latest 10-K, the statement must attribute the change to that customer (a bare 'a large customer' counts only if the 10-K lists exactly one >10% customer), and events citing share loss, pricing or design-outs are excluded. (4) Add an 'already diffused' filter: drop events where the customer's consensus revenue moved >1% between the supplier's filing and T-1 (snapshot Nasdaq/Alpha Vantage consensus daily from the supplier's filing date). (5) Restrict to customers with thin coverage (<=10 analysts), since mega-cap cases (Apple, Facebook, Ciena) are priced the same day per the press record. (6) Signal_noise held at 3 (mechanical when it fires, but small n and text ambiguity); legal risk raised to 2 for transcript terms; confidence cut to 20 because the only literature on the link (customer momentum) shows post-publication decay and the reverse direction is unstudied. (7) Expect 10-30 events/quarter; treat the first 8 quarters as validation, not trading.
- Mechanism: Small component makers, contract manufacturers and packaging firms often report 1-4 weeks before their large customers and must disclose material demand changes from any customer above 10% of revenue (ASC 280 major-customer disclosure, often named in the 10-K and inferable in the 10-Q). When an early-reporting supplier says its largest customer cut orders, pushed out a program or is destocking, the customer's own revenue, inventory and guidance for the same quarter are mechanically affected, and the customer's consensus usually has not moved because the supplier is thinly covered. The map from 10-K major-customer notes (next row) identifies which supplier statements matter for which in-universe customer. LITERATURE: Cohen and Frazzini (Journal of Finance, 2008) document slow diffusion of information along customer-supplier links (customer news to supplier price); the reverse direction used here is REASONED and less studied.
- Industry context: Suppliers see customer demand in their order books weeks before the customer reports, and small suppliers must disclose dependence on big customers. Because earnings seasons are staggered, that information is often public before the customer's own release.
- Measurement window: The window between the supplier's earnings release (8-K Item 2.02 plus transcript) and the customer's release, typically 5-25 days; the reading is a classified text event (cut, push-out or destock versus increase) tied to a named or inferred customer, finalized the day the supplier reports. History: 8-K index from 2004 (REASONED), XBRL concentration data from about 2009 (TESTED HERE for frames).. Lead time: 10 days.
- Universe coverage: ~10% (REASONED, down from 15%): in-universe customers that are named at >=10% in at least one listed supplier's 10-K AND have thin coverage (<=10 analysts); perhaps 120-150 names, with 10-30 classifiable events per quarter.
- Hypothesis: A listed supplier's 8-K Item 2.02 or call disclosure, filed 3-25 calendar days before an in-universe customer's earnings release, that explicitly attributes an order cut, program push-out or destocking to a customer named at >=10% of the supplier's revenue in its latest 10-K, predicts a negative revenue surprise and a negative T-1 to T+2 sector-adjusted abnormal return for the customer, among events where the customer's consensus revenue estimate has moved less than 1% between the supplier's filing date and T-1, controlling for the customer's prior consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum; events citing supplier share loss, pricing or design-outs are excluded.
- Who already knows: Sell-side does this for mega-cap supply chains (Apple, Tesla suppliers), and FactSet and Bloomberg sell relationship maps, but supplier commentary about mid-cap customers is rarely parsed quickly; the customer's analysts often do not cover the supplier. REASONED that a meaningful fraction of these events are not in the customer's consensus by its report date.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): FactSet Supply Chain Relationships and Bloomberg SPLC (relationship maps, not the event classification).. Why ours differs: Faster and more specific than relationship maps: we trade the dated supplier statement during the gap before the customer reports, using free SEC structured data to know which statements matter; vendors sell the map, not the event.
- Vendors found by reviewer: FactSet Supply Chain Relationships (relationship map, revenue-share from filings or FactSet estimates) - https://insight.factset.com/tariff-uncertainty-unveiling-hidden-revenue-and-supply-chain-risks; Bloomberg SPLC (described in Emerald chapter on supply-chain databases) - https://emerald.com/insight/content/doi/10.1108/S1479-838720200000012017; allmind.ai supply-chain data viewer (vendor docs; AI-parsed filings) - https://complete-docs-x17s80.allmind.ai/docs/data-viewer/supply-chain
- Literature found by reviewer: Cohen & Frazzini (2008), Economic Links and Predictable Returns, Journal of Finance 63(4) - https://ideas.repec.org/a/bla/jfinan/v63y2008i4p1977-2011.html; arXiv 2301.11394: customer momentum post-2004 is smaller and loses significance; value-weighted long-short not different from zero - https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2301.11394; McLean & Pontiff (2016), Does Academic Research Destroy Stock Return Predictability? (58% post-publication decline across 97 predictors) - https://Www.Gwern.net/doc/economics/2016-mclean.pdf; EFMA 2013 paper: supplier CARs around linked customers' earnings announcements relate to customers' unexpected earnings news - https://www.efmaefm.org/0EFMAMEETINGS/EFMA%20ANNUAL%20MEETINGS/2013-Reading/papers/EFMA2013_0548_fullpaper.pdf; Journalistic cases of supplier-first disclosure priced same day: Lumentum/Apple 2018 (Apple -4% that day) - https://www.businesslive.co.za/bd/companies/telecoms-and-technology/2018-11-12-apple-shares-fall-as-supplier-lumentum-revises-outlook-on-order-cuts/ ; Arista 'cloud titan' 2019 - https://datacenterknowledge.com/investing/arista-says-cloud-titan-cut-orders-causing-big-revenue-miss ; Lumentum/Ciena 2023 - https://www.lightreading.com/opticalip-networks/lumentums-warnings-drag-at-ciena/d/d-id/784247

U…; https://api.api-ninjas.com/v1/earningstranscript?ticker=MSFT&year=2024&quarter=2 -> KEY (working-sources file) Free API Ninjas key needed; redistribution rights unclear, internal use only.; https://api.nasdaq.com/api/calendar/earnings?date=2026-11-12 -> DATA with robots flag (working-sources file) 
- Cost: $0 for SEC data and calendars; transcripts for the handful of relevant supplier calls can be read manually or via the API Ninjas free tier. Full-text search is blocked, so the supplier set must be built from the structured notes data instead.
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 3 / 3 / 2. Confidence: 20%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Supplier commentary may reflect share loss to another supplier rather than customer weakness, the customer's analysts may already have adjusted, and the number of clean, dated, mappable events per quarter is small; Cohen-Frazzini-type effects have weakened as they became known.
- Red team (reviewer): The supplier's disclosure is itself a public press release, and the customer's stock often reprices the same day (Lumentum to Apple, 2018); what is left for the earnings window is the Cohen-Frazzini residual that arXiv 2301.11394 finds statistically insignificant after 2004, and the residual that survives is confounded with supplier-specific share loss or inventory correction that says nothing about the customer's end demand or its revenue surprise.

## S34 Customer-concentration map: customer-first reporting cascade

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: In-universe suppliers whose >10% customers report earlier: auto, truck and aero suppliers, electronics and semicap suppliers, packaging, consumer-product vendors to big-box retailers, telecom equipment. Target: revenue + EPS surprise (nowcast).
- Seed item: Customer-concentration maps built from 10-K major-customer disclosures
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: This is the textbook customer-momentum anomaly: the map is sold at scale by FactSet and Bloomberg, the effect is published (2008) and the only post-sample evidence found (arXiv 2301.11394) shows it shrinks to insignificance after 2004, consistent with McLean-Pontiff's 58% post-publication decay. The named customers (Walmart, Home Depot, Apple, Boeing, GM) report to front-page coverage, and every auto-parts or housing-vendor analyst revises within days, so the supplier's consensus has moved by T-1 and the gap this program trades is gone. The free XBRL map is cheaper but not faster or more granular than the vendor product: ConcentrationRiskPercentage1 members usually do not carry the customer's name, so the point-in-time advantage claimed is not real without the same 10-K text parsing vendors do. Finally, a customer's revenue surprise maps poorly to one supplier's sell-in (retailer sales up can coincide with vendor destocking; content-per-vehicle and platform mix dominate auto suppliers). The map-building code is not wasted: S33 reuses it, and the dependence-weighted customer surprise belongs in S33's control set, not as a standalone trade.
- Mechanism: Build a point-in-time map from each in-universe company's XBRL ConcentrationRiskPercentage disclosures and 10-K major-customer notes to the named customer (Walmart, Home Depot, Lowe's, Apple, Boeing, GM, AT&T, Amazon, Costco, Target and similar mega-caps that report early). When the customer reports results or guidance before the supplier, its segment revenue, inventory commentary and capex plans propagate mechanically to the supplier's quarter in proportion to the disclosed revenue share. The signal is the customer's revenue surprise and inventory/guidance tone weighted by the supplier's dependence, read on the customer's report date. LITERATURE: Cohen and Frazzini (JF, 2008) find supplier prices under-react to customer news; REASONED that the effect is weaker now but may persist for mid-cap suppliers in the days before their own release.
- Industry context: Many mid-cap manufacturers sell a quarter or more of their output to one giant customer and must say so in their 10-K. Giants report first, so their numbers are a partial preview of the supplier's quarter that the market historically absorbs slowly.
- Measurement window: From the customer's release to the supplier's (typically 5-25 days): dependence-weighted customer revenue surprise and guidance change, plus the customer's inventory-days change. The map is refreshed from each 10-K (annual) and 10-Q where disclosed; the surprise reading is final on the customer's report date.. Lead time: 10 days.
- Universe coverage: ~30% (REASONED): roughly 350-400 in-universe names disclose at least one >10% customer; the tradable subset where the customer reports earlier and is large enough to matter is perhaps ~150 (auto suppliers to GM/Ford, housing and consumer vendors to Home Depot/Lowe's/Walmart, electronics suppliers to Apple, aero suppliers to Boeing). Example candidates to verify: Gentherm, Visteon, Dorman, Central Garden & Pet, Spectrum Brands, Energizer, Simply Good Foods, Hayward, Trex, Acuity.
- Hypothesis: A dependence-weighted customer revenue surprise of at least 3% in absolute value (customer surprise times disclosed revenue share), measured on the customer's report date at least 5 trading days before the supplier's earnings release, predicts the sign of the supplier's revenue surprise and its T-1 to T+1 sector-adjusted abnormal return, controlling for the supplier's consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: FactSet Supply Chain Relationships and Bloomberg SPLC sell the map to most institutions, and the Cohen-Frazzini effect is a textbook anomaly, so for large suppliers the cascade is priced within a day of the customer's release. The remaining gap is for mid-cap suppliers whose analysts update slowly, and in the precision of dependence weights (XBRL percentages rather than vendor estimates).
- Vendors selling similar (generator): FactSet Supply Chain Relationships, Bloomberg SPLC (customer-supplier relationship datasets).. Why ours differs: Free and point-in-time (we know what dependence was disclosed when), with calendar sequencing built in; narrower than vendor maps (only disclosed >10% customers) but those are the links that matter for a one-quarter cascade.
- Vendors found by reviewer: FactSet Supply Chain Relationships (direct and multi-tier customer/supplier networks with revenue share from filings or estimates) - https://insight.factset.com/tariff-uncertainty-unveiling-hidden-revenue-and-supply-chain-risks; Bloomberg SPLC (percentage of costs and revenues attributed to suppliers and customers) - https://emerald.com/insight/content/doi/10.1108/S1479-838720200000012017; allmind.ai supply-chain data viewer (AI-parsed 10-K relationships) - https://complete-docs-x17s80.allmind.ai/docs/data-viewer/supply-chain
- Literature found by reviewer: Cohen & Frazzini (2008), Economic Links and Predictable Returns, JF 63(4), 1.55%/month customer momentum on 1981-2004 data - https://ideas.repec.org/a/bla/jfinan/v63y2008i4p1977-2011.html; arXiv 2301.11394: customer momentum post-2004 smaller and insignificant, consistent with post-publication exploitation - https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2301.11394; McLean & Pontiff (2016), returns 26% lower out-of-sample and 58% lower post-publication - https://Www.Gwern.net/doc/economics/2016-mclean.pdf; JFQA replication supplement confirming the Cohen-Frazzini key results - https://static.cambridge.org/content/id/urn:cambridge.org:id:article:S0022109020000885/resource/name/S0022109020000885sup001.pdf; Using supply chain databases in academic research: a methodological critique (10% rule; disclosures incomplete and non-standard) - https://law-journals-books.vlex.com/vid/using-supply-chain-databases-academic-1042472828
- Data sources:
  - SEC XBRL frames ConcentrationRiskPercentage1 [DATA] https://data.sec.gov/api/xbrl/frames/us-gaap/ConcentrationRiskPercentage1/pure/CY2024.json (update: per period; history: frames back to ~2009 (TESTED HERE))
  - SEC XBRL companyfacts [DATA] https://data.sec.gov/api/xbrl/companyfacts/CIK0000320193.json (update: live; history: XBRL era, ~2009 onward (TESTED HERE))
  - SEC Financial Statement and Notes data sets [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/files/dera/data/financial-statement-and-notes-data-sets/2025q3_notes.zip (update: monthly/quarterly; history: 2009q1 onward (TESTED HERE))
  - SEC Financial Statement Data Sets (quarterly zip) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/files/dera/data/financial-statement-data-sets/2025q3.zip (update: quarterly; ~130 MB each; history: )
  - Nasdaq earnings calendar (sequencing) [DATA] https://api.nasdaq.com/api/calendar/earnings?date=2026-11-12 (update: daily; history: )

- Cost: $0: all SEC; a free Alpha Vantage key for surprise history (25 requests per day on the free tier).
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 1 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 8%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): The anomaly is two decades old and sold by every major data vendor, so the supplier's price moves on the customer's report day and nothing is left for the earnings window; customer-level surprises also rarely map cleanly to one supplier's product line.
- Red team (reviewer): By the supplier's T-1 the customer's result is weeks old, universally covered and already reflected in the supplier's consensus and price; any remaining predictability is the decayed Cohen-Frazzini residual, which is a proxy for sector momentum and analyst revisions rather than a nowcast.

## S35 Index rebalance and forced-flow overlay around earnings dates

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: All sectors; most relevant to names near S&P 400/600 and Russell 1000/2000 boundaries and to names with heavy ETF ownership. Target: distress (forced-flow conditioning of the abnormal return; not revenue or EPS) (both).
- Seed item: Index addition and deletion mechanics; forced sellers such as distressed funds and ETF rebalances
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: (1) Reclassified from signal to exit/sizing rule; the stated hypothesis (more negative T+1..T+5 drift after a deletion) is contradicted by Chang-Hong-Liskovich (no permanent deletion effect) and Greenwood-Sammon (effect concentrated at announcement and shrinking), so the test is rewritten around realized volatility and exit slippage. (2) Removed the 'distressed-holder forced selling' leg (FINRA short interest, 13D/13F, Form 4): not observable in time and UNKNOWN as a signal. (3) Replaced the bot-blocked spglobal.com/spdji page (403) with press.spglobal.com releases (200) plus the Wikipedia change table. (4) Corrected timing: S&P quarterly-rebalance changes are announced about two weeks before the effective date (June 5 to June 22, 2026; March 6 to March 23, 2026), not 5 business days; ad-hoc replacements are shorter notice. Russell is now semi-annual: the December 2026 reconstitution is effective after the close on Dec 11, 2026 with preliminary lists on Nov 13 and weekly updates Nov 20, Nov 27, Dec 4 (lseg.com, confirmed). (5) Legal risk 2 because the SSGA xlsx carries an internal-use redistribution clause (acceptable for internal use). (6) Confidence 10: the filter is cheap and harmless but adds no alpha.
- Mechanism: This is not a fundamental nowcast; it conditions the event trade. Scheduled index events (S&P quarterly rebalances on the third Friday of March, June, September and December; Russell reconstitution in late June, with FTSE Russell's announced move to semi-annual reconstitution from 2026 - REASONED, verify on lseg.com) and ad-hoc S&P additions and deletions create forced buying or selling by index funds that can swamp or amplify the post-earnings move when an earnings date falls inside the rebalance window. Distressed-holder forced selling is observable only indirectly (FINRA short interest, 13D/13F changes, Form 4) and is UNKNOWN as a reliable signal. The practical use is a filter: avoid or size down event trades whose exit window overlaps a known forced-flow date, and note announced deletions where passive selling into the effective date coincides with an earnings release. LITERATURE: index-inclusion price effects (Shleifer 1986; Harris and Gurel 1986; Chen, Noronha and Singal 2004) are well documented and have shrunk over time.
- Industry context: Index funds must trade constituent changes at the effective close regardless of price, and those dates are public weeks ahead. When they collide with an earnings release, the price path reflects mechanical flows as much as the fundamental news.
- Measurement window: S&P announces changes roughly 5 business days before the effective date (REASONED); Russell publishes preliminary add/delete lists several weeks before reconstitution (TESTED HERE: 2026 add/delete PDFs are public). Reading = flag for any in-universe name with an announced add/delete or a rebalance effective date inside the T-5 to T+5 window around its earnings date, plus daily archived SPDR holdings to measure realized passive-weight changes. History: S&P 400 constituent change log on Wikipedia (TESTED HERE), FTSE Russell PDFs by year, SSGA holdings current day only (must archive).. Lead time: 5 days.
- Universe coverage: ~100% as a calendar filter; 5-10% of names have an index event within T-5..T+5 of a given earnings date (REASONED).
- Hypothesis: Among in-universe earnings events, those with an announced S&P or Russell add/delete or a rebalance effective date inside T+1..T+5 show higher realized volatility and volume and worse exit fills than matched events, with no predictable sign of the T+1..T+5 abnormal return after controlling for the surprise, sector, size and momentum; the rule's value is measured by exit slippage and realized variance, not by return.
- Who already knows: Index arbitrage desks and every systematic fund model these flows; predicted add/delete lists are published by sell-side index strategists. Crowdedness 1. The overlay is defensive (avoid bad exits), not a source of alpha.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Sell-side index-event research (certain to exist); specific data vendors not asserted (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: No claim of differentiation; this row is retained as a risk filter and sizing rule for the event book rather than a signal.
- Vendors found by reviewer: S&P Dow Jones Indices press releases on index changes (press.spglobal.com; e.g., June 5, 2026 release for June 22 MidCap 400 changes) - https://press.spglobal.com/2026-06-05-Marvell-Technology-and-Flex-Set-to-Join-S-P-500-Others-to-Join-S-P-MidCap-400-and-S-P-SmallCap-600; FTSE Russell: Russell US Indexes move to semi-annual reconstitution (Jan 16, 2025 release) - https://lseg.com/en/media-centre/press-releases/ftse-russell/2025/russell-us-indexes-move-to-semi-annual-reconstitution; FTSE Russell: December 2026 Russell US Indexes reconstitution schedule (effective after close Dec 11, 2026; preliminary lists Nov 13; updates Nov 20, 27, Dec 4; lockdown from Nov 30) - https://www.lseg.com/en/media-centre/press-releases/ftse-russell/2026/ftse-russell-announces-december-2026-russell-us-indexes-reconstitution-schedule; CXO Advisory summary of index-rebalancing strategies (practitioner) - https://www.cxoadvisory.com/miscellaneous/strategies-for-exploiting-index-rebalancing/
- Literature found by reviewer: Greenwood & Sammon, The Disappearing Index Effect (NBER w30748; S&P 500 addition return not distinguishable from zero in the last decade) - https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w30748/w30748.pdf; Chang, Hong & Liskovich, Regression discontinuity and the price effects of stock market indexing (NBER w19290; deletions show no permanent negative effect) - https://www.nber.org/papers/w19290.pdf; Vijh & Wang, pure additions vs migrations and deletions - https://www.biz.uiowa.edu/faculty/avijh/SP_1.pdf; Huij & Kyosev, do index changes carry earnings information (deletions not associated with changed earnings expectations) - https://repub.eur.nl/pub/105154
- Data sources:
  - S&P Global press room: index-change releases (effective-date tables) [DATA (200 TESTED HERE; replaces blocked spglobal.com/spdji page)] https://press.spglobal.com/index.php?s=2429 (update: per announcement (quarterly rebalance ~2 weeks before effective date; ad-hoc changes a few business days before); history: releases back to at least 2020 seen in search results)

- Cost: $0: all public; the SSGA xlsx carries a redistribution clause (internal use only).
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 1 / 2 / 2. Confidence: 10%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Index-event price effects are among the most arbitraged phenomena in equities and now largely occur at announcement rather than at the effective date, so the overlay adds little; it may also discard good trades for no benefit.
- Red team (reviewer): Index-event effects are among the most arbitraged phenomena in equities and now occur at announcement, not at the effective date, so overlapping an earnings exit with a rebalance mostly adds volume (better fills), and the filter may discard good trades for no measurable benefit; any 'post-earnings deletion drift' found in a backtest is a proxy for the size and momentum factors that drive index migration in the first place.

## S36 Trade-show footprint changes (booth size, exhibitor presence, event-staff postings)

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: Consumer electronics (CES), retail technology (NRF), auto aftermarket (SEMA/AAPEX), medical imaging and devices (RSNA, HIMSS), machine tools (IMTS), firearms and outdoor (SHOT Show), construction equipment (CONEXPO). Target: guidance + margin (SG&A) (forward).
- Seed item: Trade-show footprint changes: shrinking booths, executives skipping keynotes, fewer event-staff postings
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: No history and no power: directories exist for the current show only, Common Crawl cannot backfill JS-rendered directories, and several named shows are unusable (SEMA 404, RSNA page is a booth-sales page, IMTS and CES are SPAs whose ajax/API paths robots.txt disallows), so the first usable YoY comparison for most shows is a year away and then yields one observation per company per year. The mechanism does not meet the program's timing test: booth contracts are signed 9-12 months ahead on a marketing budget cycle, so they do not inform the quarter being reported, and the 'forward guidance' link is REASONED with no literature (the only academic work found is marketing-effectiveness research). The only cheaper path is paid lead-list vendors (ExhibitorsData, Expolista) that sell snapshots, not footprint time series. Scraping SPA directories against robots.txt is a terms conflict (legal 3).
- Mechanism: For companies whose selling season is anchored to an annual trade show, the booth size listed in the exhibitor directory, presence versus absence, and the count of event and field-marketing roles posted on the company's ATS in the run-up are a budgeted, forward-looking read of marketing spend and product pipeline. A shrinking booth or a skipped show signals SG&A cuts (margin positive) and a thin pipeline (guidance negative); a larger booth with many new-product listings signals launch-driven guidance. The reading is final when the directory is published, typically 1-2 months before the show, which is often weeks before the earnings release that follows the show. REASONED throughout; no literature.
- Industry context: In show-anchored industries the annual show is where the year's orders are written and new products are revealed, so the budget a company commits to it reflects its own expectation for the year. Exhibitor directories publish that commitment months in advance.
- Measurement window: Per show-year: booth square footage (where listed) and presence flag from the exhibitor directory, compared with the prior year, read when the directory is published (CES around November-December for a January show; NRF around December; others vary), plus ATS postings for events, trade-show and field-marketing roles in the 120 days before the show. History: only the current show's directory is online (TESTED HERE for CES and NRF); past years must come from our own archive or Common Crawl.. Lead time: 30 days.
- Universe coverage: ~8% (REASONED): about 100 names where one show anchors the sales year: consumer electronics and accessories (Sonos, Logitech, Corsair, Arlo), auto aftermarket (Dorman, Fox Factory, Holley), firearms and outdoor (Sturm Ruger; Smith & Wesson is near the cap floor), med-tech imaging and devices (Masimo, iRhythm), machine tools and welding (Kennametal, Lincoln Electric), construction equipment (Terex, Astec). Verify caps.
- Hypothesis: A year-over-year reduction of at least 25% in a company's listed booth area (or a skipped anchor show) at its primary trade show, measured at least 30 days before the first earnings release after the show, predicts lowered forward guidance and a negative T-1 to T+2 sector-adjusted abnormal return, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Sell-side analysts attend the shows and write notes on launches but do not quantify booth size; no vendor is known to sell exhibitor-footprint series (UNKNOWN). Trade press reports exhibitors skipping shows, so the presence flag can be partly public knowledge.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): None known (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: Unsold, but only useful once a multi-year archive exists; the first usable YoY comparison is a year away for most shows.
- Vendors found by reviewer: ExhibitorsData (per-show exhibitor lists sold as lead lists, e.g., AHR list $450) - https://exhibitorsdata.com/; Expolista (collects and enriches exhibitor data from major tradeshows; lead-generation) - https://expolista.com/about; ExpoCaptive (exhibitor lists with confirmed participation) - https://www.expocaptive.com/?p=14313; AWS Marketplace exhibitor/attendee dataset with booth numbers and event participation history - https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/pp/prodview-pv4q3h7xeza7i
- Literature found by reviewer: Wharton-hosted Journal of Marketing 1997 paper on booth size, staffing and pre-show promotion as drivers of trade-show effectiveness (marketing, not finance) - https://faculty.wharton.upenn.edu/wp-content/uploads/2012/04/JM1997.pdf; Trade Show Executive / CEIR: median exhibitor booth square footage roughly constant over time - https://tradeshowexecutive.com/?p=31248; EVOLIO 2023 (Explori survey): 31% of exhibitors reducing booth size to cut costs, 49% reducing staff - https://cdn.asp.events/CLIENT_Lippman__27AF112E_5056_B733_4905CC1658AA5A58/sites/lippman-connects-2023/media/libraries/resources-1/2023-ESR-September---EVOLIO.pdf
- Data sources:
  - CES exhibitor directory [DATA] https://exhibitors.ces.tech/ (update: annual; browser automation needed, robots.txt disallows ajax endpoints (TESTED HERE); history: current show only)
  - NRF Big Show exhibitors [DATA] https://nrfbigshow.nrf.com/exhibitors (update: annual; robots disallows /api/ (TESTED HERE); history: current show only)
  - SEMA Show exhibitor list [UNPROBED] https://www.semashow.com/exhibitor-list (update: ; history: )
  - RSNA exhibitor list [UNPROBED] https://www.rsna.org/annual-meeting/exhibitors (update: ; history: )
  - IMTS exhibitor directory [UNPROBED] https://www.imts.com/show/exhibitor-directory (update: ; history: )
  - SHOT Show exhibitor list [UNPROBED] https://shotshow.org/exhibitors/ (update: ; history: )
  - Greenhouse/Lever/Ashby job boards (event-staff postings) [DATA] https://boards-api.greenhouse.io/v1/boards/duolingo/jobs?content=false (update: live; history: )
  - Common Crawl (prior-year directory backfill) [DATA] https://index.commoncrawl.org/collinfo.json (update: monthly-ish; history: 2008 onward (TESTED HERE))
- Reviewer source checks: https://exhibitors.ces.tech/ -> DATA with robots flag (working-sources file) Browser automation required; ajax endpoints disallowed by robots.txt.; https://nrfbigshow.nrf.com/exhibitors -> DATA with robots flag (working-sources file) robots disallows /api/.; https://www.semashow.com/exhibitor-list -> 404 Redirects to semashow.com/exhibitor-list which is 404; URL is wrong or page rem…; https://www.rsna.org/annual-meeting/exhibitors -> 301 -> 200 Final URL is /annual-meeting/exhibitors-and-sponsors/exhibit-spaces, a booth-sa…; https://www.imts.com/show/exhibitor-directory -> 302 -> 200 Final URL directory.imts.com/8_0/#/ is a JavaScript single-page app; no static …; https://shotshow.org/exhibitors/ -> 200 ; https://boards-api.greenhouse.io/v1/boards/duolingo/jobs?content=false -> DATA (working-sources file) ; https://index.commoncrawl.org/collinfo.json -> DATA (working-sources file) Common Crawl does not execute JavaScript, so SPA directories (CES, IMTS) are un…
- Cost: $0 but labour-intensive: directories require a browser or manual pull once per show (robots.txt disallows the ajax endpoints at CES and NRF, so no bulk scraping); several shows are UNPROBED.
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 5 / 1 / 3. Confidence: 3%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Booth decisions are made a year ahead on a marketing budget cycle unrelated to the current quarter, many companies have shifted to private events regardless of outlook, and with one observation per company per year there is no statistical power for a decade.
- Red team (reviewer): Booth area is a lagged marketing-budget decision made before the fiscal year starts and is driven by product-launch cadence, so any correlation with later guidance is a proxy for the prior year's margin trend that consensus already has; with n of one per company-year, nothing can be estimated for a decade.

## N01 Texas mixed-beverage gross receipts by location as a same-store alcohol-sales nowcast for restaurant and eatertainment chains

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Casual dining, sports bars, eatertainment (Topgolf, Dave & Buster's, Bowlero/Lucky Strike), sushi/steak chains with Texas footprints. Target: revenue (same-store sales), EPS surprise (nowcast).
- Merged duplicates: Texas mixed-beverage tax receipts per location as a same-store-sales nowcast for casual dining and eatertainment
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: (1) Both Socrata endpoints probed 200 and the chain mapping verified by query; match on location_name and address rather than taxpayer_name (Texas Roadhouse: 88 locations by name but 6 by taxpayer). (2) Join the TABC license file to keep only mixed-beverage-class permits and company-operated units; drop Cracker Barrel (beer/wine permit, 1 row), Portillo's (10 units, too thin) and any chain under 20 matched units; verify Kura (sake/beer) permit class before inclusion. Coverage falls to ~12-20 names. (3) Add a Texas-wide matched-location MB receipts control to strip the regional/oil-economy component. (4) Use only filing-complete months (>=90% of prior-year locations filed) so month 3 is excluded for early reporters; lead time stays ~7 days. (5) Signal_noise cut from 4 to 3: alcohol-only, Texas-only, receipts include the pass-through mixed-beverage sales tax (consistent YoY but a level distortion), and amended/late returns revise history, so backtests need as-of snapshots that do not exist yet (look-ahead risk; start archiving weekly). (6) Crowdedness stays 3: the dataset is public and used by local press for a decade, but no institutional vendor packages it; confidence 30.
- Mechanism: Every Texas establishment holding a mixed-beverage permit must file a monthly gross-receipts tax return (Tax Code ch. 183) itemizing liquor, wine, beer and cover-charge receipts; the Comptroller publishes these location-level dollar amounts on data.texas.gov. For a chain whose alcohol is 10-35% of sales and whose Texas units are 10-25% of the system, same-location YoY alcohol receipts move with traffic and check, so a matched-location index for the quarter's reported months is a cash-basis read on same-store sales before the company reports. Applies to alcohol-heavy restaurant and entertainment chains with company-operated (not franchised) Texas units, where the taxpayer_name field maps cleanly to the parent.
- Industry context: Restaurant stocks move on same-store-sales surprises, and alcohol is the highest-margin, most traffic-sensitive line in casual dining and eatertainment. Texas is a top-three state for most of these chains and the only large state that publishes location-level alcohol receipts.
- Measurement window: Monthly location receipts. TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: September 2026 had 22,677 rows filed (complete) while October had 1,900 (~8%), so a month is essentially complete ~40 days after month-end; dataset rowsUpdatedAt 2026-11-07. Months 1-2 of a quarter are complete well before any announcement; month 3 is complete only when the announcement falls >=42 days after quarter-end (Texas Roadhouse, BJ's, Cheesecake, Bloomin' typically yes; Brinker's late-January report gets 2 of 3 months with ~25 days lead).. Lead time: 7 days.
- Universe coverage: ~1-1.5%: 12-20 names with >=20 matched company-operated mixed-beverage-permitted Texas units (TESTED HERE for Sept 2026: Chili's/Brinker 226 locations, Texas Roadhouse 88, Outback/Bloomin' 52, Cheesecake 20, Kura 16, Dave & Buster's 14, Topgolf 13); Cracker Barrel (1 location) and Portillo's (10) drop out.
- Hypothesis: A matched-location YoY change of at least +/-3 percentage points in Texas mixed-beverage receipts (locations matched on location_name and location_address, restricted to company-operated MB/RM-class permits, using only months where >=90% of prior-year locations have filed), relative to the Texas-wide matched-location MB receipts change, measured 5 trading days before the earnings release, predicts the sign of the same-store-sales surprise versus consensus and the T-1 to T+1 abnormal return versus XLY, controlling for 30-day consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: REASONED: some hedge funds, alt-data boutiques and financial journalists have used Texas mixed-beverage receipts for single-name work (it is a known public dataset); sell-side restaurant analysts rely on Placer.ai traffic, card panels and management commentary rather than this filing. Consensus likely reflects general Texas trends but not a matched-location receipts index built per chain; partial pricing assumed.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): UNKNOWN whether any vendor packages this dataset specifically. Placer.ai sells foot traffic and card panels (Consumer Edge, Earnest, M Science) sell spend for the same chains at scale, which is the crowded benchmark.. Why ours differs: Tax-filed dollar receipts at the individual location (not device-sampled visits or a card panel with coverage bias), free, with full company-operated coverage inside Texas; alcohol receipts also carry a mix signal (bar vs. dining occasion) that traffic data cannot see.
- Vendors found by reviewer: The Texas Bar Tool (small commercial venue-analytics site on a test subdomain; unverified, not an institutional vendor) - https://test.bardata.23.94.4.122.sslip.io/; data.gov catalog entry for Texas Mixed Beverage Gross Receipts (public dataset) - https://catalog.data.gov/dataset/mixed-beverage-gross-receipts; Texas Comptroller mixed beverage receipts page (official source, Tax Code ch. 183) - https://comptroller.texas.gov/taxes/mixed-beverage/receipts.php
- Literature found by reviewer: No academic or practitioner finance study found. Journalistic use is long-standing: CultureMap, Secrets of the TABC reports (2014) - https://houston.culturemap.com/news/restaurants-bars/07-08-14-secrets-of-the-tabc-reports-golf-strip-club-and-restaurant-shockers-plus-how-boobs-drive-alcohol-alcohol-sales; Dallas Morning News city-level alcohol sales rankings from the Comptroller data (2021) - https://dallasnews.com/news/2021/08/05/frisco-alcohol-sales-are-up-60-since-texas-reopened-heres-where-people-spend-the-most; Houston First: Houston alcoholic beverage sales totaled $1.5B in 2025 (flat YoY) - https://www.houstonfirst.com/news/houston-alcoholic-beverage-sales-totaled-15b-in-2025; Dallas Observer: top-selling Dallas bars and restaurants of 2025 - https://www.dallasobserver.com/food-drink/top-selling-dallas-bars-and-restaurants-of-2025-40631002/
- Data sources:
  - Texas Comptroller Mixed Beverage Gross Receipts (Socrata naix-2893) [DATA (200 TESTED HERE; optional free Socrata app token for rate limits)] https://data.texas.gov/resource/naix-2893.json?$limit=5&$order=obligation_end_date_yyyymmdd%20DESC (update: rows added as returns post; month ~complete 40 days after month-end; history: 3.87M location-months; rows from the 2017 era (earliest not queried))
  - TABC License Information (Socrata 7hf9-qc9f) [DATA (200 TESTED HERE)] https://data.texas.gov/resource/7hf9-qc9f.json?$limit=5 (update: daily; history: current register; archive daily)
  - Texas-wide matched-location MB receipts (same dataset, aggregated) as regional control [DATA (same endpoint)] https://data.texas.gov/resource/naix-2893.json?$select=sum(total_receipts)&$group=obligation_end_date_yyyymmdd (update: monthly; history: as above)
- Reviewer source checks: https://data.texas.gov/resource/naix-2893.json?$limit=5&$order=obligation_end_d… -> 200 TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: JSON rows returned; latest obligation_end_date 2026-10-…; https://data.texas.gov/resource/naix-2893.json?$select=count(*)&$where=upper(lo… -> 200 TESTED HERE chain counts for Sept 2026 by location_name: Chili's 226, Texas Roa…; https://data.texas.gov/resource/7hf9-qc9f.json?$limit=5 -> 200 TABC license file; use for permit class (MB/RM/NB) and owner joins.
- Cost: $0 (optional free Socrata app token for higher rate limits)
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 3 / 3 / 1. Confidence: 30%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Texas is a regional sample tied to oil-economy and weather idiosyncrasies; alcohol incidence is secularly declining so alcohol receipts can fall while food sales hold; month 3 is often incomplete at announcement; and because the dataset is known, sophisticated holders may already trade it, leaving little post-announcement drift.
- Red team (reviewer): Sell-side consensus for these chains is already informed by national card-panel and foot-traffic products sold at scale, so the Texas alcohol slice adds a regional, alcohol-only residual that can be wrong-signed (secular decline in alcohol incidence, Texas weather and oil-economy shocks) while the national traffic trend consensus already has is what drives the surprise; the backtest also faces look-ahead because late and amended returns are not timestamped in the public file.

## N02 State air-emission event reports (TCEQ, LDEQ) as a unit-trip detector for refineries, crackers, chlor-alkali, fertilizer and gas plants

- Category: Physical. Sector focus: Energy and materials: refiners, commodity chemicals, fertilizer, gas processing, Permian E&P flaring, Texas EAF steel. Target: revenue (volume), margin (fixed-cost absorption), guidance (nowcast).
- Merged duplicates: State emissions-event reports as an unplanned-downtime meter for Gulf Coast refineries, crackers and ammonia plants
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: (1) Drop refiners: IIR Energy sells planned and unplanned refinery outage data at scale (distributed through LSEG and ICE) and Reuters/Argus desks read the same TCEQ filings, so refinery events are priced; crowdedness lowered to 2 overall. (2) Count only 'emissions event' records (exclude scheduled maintenance/startup/shutdown filings) with a named unit shutdown >24 h, weighted by the site's share of company capacity; raw event counts are dominated by restart flaring. (3) Add an 'already disclosed' exclusion using the company's 8-K/press releases, since large outages are typically pre-announced. (4) Lead time cut from 20 to 15 days and signal_noise from 3 to 2 because final reports can arrive weeks to months late (InsideClimate), per-company samples are thin (Delek 2026: one record), and the public search table shows report date but not a filing timestamp history, so a backtest cannot reconstruct what was visible at T-5 (look-ahead risk; start archiving the search results weekly now). (5) LDEQ EDMS loads (200) but its query interface is unexercised; treat Louisiana coverage as UNPROBED until a working query is demonstrated. (6) Use as a conditioning feature (probability of a volume miss) rather than a standalone trade; confidence 15.
- Mechanism: Texas (TCEQ Air Emission Event Reports) and Louisiana (LDEQ EDMS) require operators to report unplanned emission events within 24 hours, naming the site, the unit (FCCU, cracker, cell line, compressor), start and end time, event type and cause. Counting unplanned event-hours and unit-shutdown events at a company's named sites gives a quarterly unplanned-downtime index per company, which maps to lost throughput for refiners (Delek Tyler/Big Spring, PBF Chalmette, Calumet Shreveport), lost production for chemical makers (Westlake, Olin Freeport, Huntsman Port Neches, LyondellBasell Channelview, Eastman Longview, Celanese Clear Lake, Orion, Cabot, Kronos, Chemours Ingleside), fertilizer (CF Donaldsonville, Mosaic Louisiana), gas processors (Kinetik) and Permian E&P flaring. The gap is between disclosed planned turnarounds and the unplanned downtime visible in filings before the company reports volumes.
- Industry context: Refineries and crackers run at 90-95% utilization, so an unplanned trip of a cracker or FCCU costs days of output plus a restart, and the margin lost per day is large when spreads are wide. Small events such as opacity exceedances are noise; unit shutdowns longer than 24 hours are the signal.
- Measurement window: Event level, full quarter; initial reports within 24 hours and final reports within about two weeks, so the quarter's index is final ~14 days after quarter end; companies report 25-45 days after quarter end.. Lead time: 15 days.
- Universe coverage: ~2%: 20-25 names after dropping refiners; chemicals (WLK, OLN, HUN, LYB if under cap, EMN, CE, CC, KRO, OEC, CBT), fertilizer (CF, MOS), gas processing/midstream (KNTK, WES), steel (STLD Sinton, CMC Seguin), Permian E&Ps (flaring events only).
- Hypothesis: For chemical, fertilizer, gas-processing and steel companies with a Texas or Louisiana site representing >=25% of company capacity, an unplanned emissions-event record (excluding scheduled maintenance/startup/shutdown filings) that names a unit shutdown lasting more than 24 hours, filed at least 5 trading days before the earnings release and not previously disclosed by the company in an 8-K or press release, predicts a negative volume/revenue surprise and a negative T-1 to T+2 abnormal return versus XLB/XLE, controlling for consensus revisions, product-spread moves, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Reuters, Argus and OPIS refinery-outage desks read TCEQ filings for large refineries, and IIR Energy sells a plant-outage database to energy traders (REASONED; vendor existence confident). Chemical, fertilizer, gas-plant and E&P events are much less covered; consensus for small chemical names rarely reflects a two-week cracker outage until the company says so (REASONED). Priced in: headline refinery outages mostly; smaller chemical-plant downtime little.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): IIR Energy (industrial outage database) and Wood Mackenzie/Genscape refinery monitoring (REASONED; not on the named list). None from the named list.. Why ours differs: Free, event-level, 24-hour lag, extended beyond refining to chemicals, fertilizer, gas processing and E&P flaring where no outage desk looks, and measured as company-level unplanned event-hours per quarter rather than headline refinery outages.
- Vendors found by reviewer: IIR Energy (Industrial Info Resources) Offline Event Database and Refinery Capacity Insights: planned and unplanned outage tracking, phone-sourced, sold by subscription - https://www.industrialinfo.com/iirenergy/research-layers/; IIR data distributed via LSEG - https://www.lseg.com/en/data-analytics/financial-data/news/iir-industrial-info-resources; IIR data distributed via ICE - https://developer.ice.com/fixed-income-data-services/catalog/industrial-information-resources-iir; IIR weekly North American refining report (public abstracts) - https://www.industrialinfo.com/news/abstract/industrial-infos-weekly-north-american-refining-report--287360
- Literature found by reviewer: Environmental Integrity Project, Accident Prone (2012 analysis of TCEQ emission event filings 2009-2011 by sector) - https://webarchive.library.unt.edu/dentonfracking/20141030014219/http://www.environmentalintegrity.org/news_reports/documents/20120718AccidentProneFinal.pdf; Environment Texas major malfunction report built from TCEQ EER data - https://environmentamerica.org/texas/wp-content/uploads/2019/01/TX_MajorMal_scrn.pdf; InsideClimate News: some emission event reports from winter storms filed up to four months late - https://insideclimatenews.org/news/26012024/texas-companies-exploit-air-pollution-loopholes/; Texas Tribune on TCEQ emission event reporting (24-hour initial notice, final report two weeks later) - https://www.texastribune.org/?p=125885
- Data sources:
  - TCEQ Air Emission Event Report search (POST by customer name and date range; Excel export) [DATA (200 TESTED HERE; POST search verified by Phase 1 author)] https://www2.tceq.texas.gov/oce/eer/index.cfm (update: initial reports within 24 h; final reports ~2 weeks; some filed months late; history: searchable by date back to early 2000s (REASONED))
  - Louisiana DEQ EDMS (JS app) [UNPROBED functionally (page 200; query API not exercised)] https://edms.deq.louisiana.gov/edmsv2 (update: continuous (REASONED); history: UNKNOWN)
  - EPA ECHO facility REST (site-to-parent mapping) [DATA] https://echodata.epa.gov/echo/echo_rest_services.get_facilities?output=JSON&p_st=TX (update: weekly (REASONED); history: current snapshot)
  - EPA GHGRP facility table (site size weights) [DATA] https://data.epa.gov/efservice/pub_dim_facility/year/2023/state_name/TEXAS/rows/0:2/JSON (update: annual; history: 2010+)
  - EDGAR 8-K feed (to exclude outages the company already disclosed) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=8-K&count=100&output=atom (update: real time; history: latest filings; backfill via daily index)
- Reviewer source checks: https://www2.tceq.texas.gov/oce/eer/index.cfm -> 200 Search page loads; POST search verified by Phase 1 author (Delek 2026: 1 record…; https://edms.deq.louisiana.gov/edmsv2 -> 200 JavaScript app loads; query interface not exercised, so functionally still UNPR…; https://echodata.epa.gov/echo/echo_rest_services.get_facilities?output=JSON&p_s… -> DATA (working-sources file) ; https://data.epa.gov/efservice/pub_dim_facility/year/2023/state_name/TEXAS/rows… -> DATA (working-sources file) ; https://api.weather.gov/points/29.7,-95.0 -> DATA (working-sources file) 
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 15%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Event counts correlate weakly with lost production (many events are flaring during restarts); large outages are pre-announced or picked up by trade press and are already in estimates; site-to-company mapping errors; the Delek 2026 count of one record shows per-company samples can be too thin.
- Red team (reviewer): Event-hours are a poor proxy for lost production (most records are flaring during upsets and restarts, and flare estimates are 'ballpark' per a former TCEQ investigator), the outages large enough to move a quarter are pre-announced by the company or trade press and already in estimates, and for commodity chemicals the quarter is set by observable spreads, so what remains is a thin, late-filed event count whose backtest cannot be made point-in-time.

## N03 EPA NPDES discharge-monitoring-report effluent flow and organic load as a throughput nowcast for single-site process plants

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Materials and energy: single- or few-site chemicals, nitrogen fertilizer, pulp and paper, specialty cellulose, refining. Target: revenue (volumes), margin (utilization) (nowcast).
- Seed item: Municipal water and power usage for large industrial users, and permit renewals that reveal it
- Merged duplicates: EPA ECHO discharge-monitoring (DMR) effluent flow as a monthly production meter for pulp, paperboard, steel and chemical plants
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES.
