- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 1 / 3 / 2. Confidence: 30%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Published for two decades and sold by vendors, so probably priced; the effect is small relative to transaction costs for a sub-$10k account; calendar dates are often vendor estimates rather than company confirmations, adding noise.
- Red team (reviewer): Published since 2002, sold by Wall Street Horizon and FactSet and used by quant funds, so the effect is largely priced in liquid names; what remains is small relative to the costs of a sub-$10k options account, the 'late' feature loads on small, thinly covered firms and on firms with pending bad news already visible in NT 10-Q filings, and free calendar dates are vendor estimates rather than confirmations, so the timing feature is noisier than the literature's.

## N36 Komatsu monthly KOMTRAX operating hours and demand-by-region plus JMTBA monthly machine-tool orders as physical nowcasts for US construction-equipment, rental, aggregates and metalworking mid-caps

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: Industrials: equipment rental, equipment dealers, construction/ag OEMs, aggregates, cutting tools, welding, bearings, motion control. Target: revenue, margin (rental utilization), guidance (nowcast).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: Dropped JMTBA as a primary input (Japanese-built machine tools are a narrow slice of US metalworking capex and track ISM, which is already in consensus); kept it only as an optional cross-check. Dropped ELFA (403 from container). Narrowed the universe to names whose quarter is set by earthmoving and road-building activity rather than aerial rental: aggregates and infrastructure materials (KNF, ACA), road-building and crushing OEMs (ASTE, TEX, MTW), equipment dealers (TITN) and earthmoving-exposed consumables (KMT); moved HRI, CTOS, OSK, LECO, TKR, EPAC, HLIO, CMCO, HY to a secondary list to be tested only if the primary group shows an effect. Feature redefined as the two-month (months 1-2 of the quarter) YoY change in Komatsu North America unit demand and KOMTRAX average operating hours, with FHWA travel-volume and NOAA weather controls. Parsing note: Komatsu PDF links carry rev-hash query strings and must be scraped from the page each month. Lead time kept at 10 days for months 1-2 (month-2 data posts about 5-6 weeks after month-end, i.e. roughly 10 days before late-month reporters). Crowdedness lowered to 2 because the series is reported in trade press monthly; signal_noise lowered to 2 for the two-month partial read and fleet-mix mismatch.
- Mechanism: Komatsu publishes a monthly page 'Monthly demand, orders and Komtrax data' with (1) demand by region in units for its seven main products, (2) a book-to-bill ratio and (3) KOMTRAX machine operating hours by region including North America, a fleet-wide telematics measure of how many hours construction machines actually ran (TESTED HERE: page live, 'Komtrax data (up through Sep 2026)' on 2026-11-11). Operating hours drive rental time-utilization and parts/aftermarket revenue at rental companies and dealers, and the units-demand series is new-equipment retail demand before US OEMs disclose it. JMTBA publishes monthly Japanese machine-tool orders with a foreign/North America breakdown; North American orders of Japanese machine tools are a capex proxy for metalworking end-markets that buy consumables (cutting tools, welding wire, bearings). Applies to rental companies, dealers, OEMs and consumables makers whose quarter is set by hours run and machines bought in North America.
- Industry context: Construction machines are telematics-connected and Komatsu, the number-two global OEM, publishes its fleet's operating hours by region every month; hours run determine rental utilization, parts wear and replacement timing. Japanese machine-tool builders dominate the US metal-cutting market, so their North American order book is an early read on factory capex that drives consumables demand.
- Measurement window: Komatsu KOMTRAX/demand: months 1-2 of the calendar quarter are available at least 10 days before late-month reporters; month 3 arrives with a ~5-6 week lag (TESTED HERE: Sep data visible by Nov 11) so it is only usable for later reporters and FY-offset names (Titan Machinery FQ ends Oct 31, reports early Dec). JMTBA preliminary orders: ~10-12 days after month-end (REASONED), so all three months are available 10-20 days before most releases.. Lead time: 10 days.
- Universe coverage: ~0.6%: about 7 primary names (KNF, ACA, ASTE, TEX, MTW, TITN, KMT); ~0.7% secondary (HRI, CTOS, OSK, LECO, TKR, EPAC, HLIO, CMCO, HY) only if the primary group shows an effect
- Hypothesis: A change of at least 5 percentage points in the year-over-year growth of Komatsu North America unit demand or KOMTRAX average operating hours over the first two months of the quarter, measured at least 10 days before the earnings release, predicts same-sign revenue and margin surprises and close T-1 to close T+2 abnormal returns versus XLI for aggregates producers, road-building and crushing OEMs, equipment dealers and earthmoving-exposed consumables makers, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size, momentum, FHWA travel volume and weather.
- Who already knows: Japan machinery analysts and Caterpillar/Deere analysts cite KOMTRAX and Komatsu demand data (REASONED), so the macro read is partly priced for large OEMs (outside the universe). Rental, dealer and consumables mid-caps are covered by different analysts who rarely use it (REASONED). Rouse Services (owned by RB Global) sells rental utilization and rate analytics to rental companies (REASONED), which is the paid version of the utilization read.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Rouse Services / RB Global (rental utilization and used-value analytics, REASONED); EquipmentWatch (paid equipment data, REASONED). Neither sells the Komatsu/JMTBA mapping to US mid-caps.. Why ours differs: Free, physical (telematics hours, units, orders), monthly, and mapped to the rental/dealer/consumables tickers rather than to the OEMs that everyone already models.
- Vendors found by reviewer: Rouse Services / RB Global Rental Insights (rental rate, physical and dollar utilization benchmarks from 250-400+ participating rental companies; quarterly extracts republished by MHEDA) - https://www.mheda.org/industry-news/latest-market-rental-utilization-data-q1-2025/; Rouse Services site - https://www.rouseservices.com/category/uncategorized/; The Capitol Forum: RB Global's Rouse Services could face antitrust scrutiny over information sharing (notes the product uses three-month-old data) - https://thecapitolforum.com/rb-globals-rouse-services-could-face-antitrust-scrutiny-over-information-sharing/
- Literature found by reviewer: No academic paper found. Trade coverage showing the series is public and followed: Yieh machinery news, Komatsu excavator operating hours by region, Jan 2025 (North America 61.5 hours, +6.4% YoY) - https://machinery.yieh.com/en/komatsus-excavator-operating-hours-decline-in-china-but-rise-in-other-regions-in-jan-2025/; Seisanzai Japan: JMTBA preliminary August 2025 machine-tool orders reported 11 September 2025 (about 11 days after month-end) - https://seisanzai-japan.com/article/p6136; Komatsu IR 'Monthly demand, orders and Komtrax data' page (official source) - https://www.komatsu.jp/en/ir/library/demand-orders
- Data sources:
  - Komatsu IR: Monthly demand, orders and Komtrax data (TESTED HERE 200; 'Komtrax data (up through Sep 2026)'; parse rev-hash PDF links from the page) [DATA] https://www.komatsu.jp/en/ir/library/demand-orders (update: Monthly, about 5-6 weeks after month-end; history: PDFs contain multi-year monthly series (REASONED; not parsed here); archive each revision)
  - JMTBA Monthly Machine Tool Orders Notes PDFs (TESTED HERE 200; latest JMTBA-NOTES-2026-10.pdf; optional cross-check only) [DATA] https://www.jmtba.or.jp/english/category/machine-tool-orders/ (update: Preliminary headline about 11 days after month-end (per Seisanzai report dates); Notes PDF 2-5 weeks; history: Monthly PDFs on site back to at least 2023)
  - FHWA TMAS / Travel Volume Trends (seasonal construction-activity control) [DATA] https://www.fhwa.dot.gov/policyinformation/tables/tmasdata/ (update: Monthly; history: 2010 onward)
  - NOAA NCEI Access Data Service daily summaries (weather control; keyless) [DATA] https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/access/services/data/v1?dataset=daily-summaries&stations=USW00094846&startDate=2025-01-01&endDate=2025-01-31&format=json (update: daily; history: full period of record)
- Reviewer source checks: https://www.komatsu.jp/en/ir/library/demand-orders -> 200 TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: page reads 'Komtrax data (up through Sep 2026)'; links …; https://www.jmtba.or.jp/english/category/machine-tool-orders/ -> 200 TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: latest file JMTBA-NOTES-2026-10.pdf at /wjmtbap/wp-cont…; https://www.elfaonline.org/research/capex-finance-index -> 403 Bot-blocked from container on two attempts; dropped; https://www.fhwa.dot.gov/policyinformation/tables/tmasdata/ -> DATA In working file; seasonal control
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 25%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): KOMTRAX covers only Komatsu's fleet share and is dominated by weather and seasonality; the hours series is a macro proxy already captured by ISM, ABI and housing starts; rental pricing matters more than hours for Herc's margin; and the 5-6 week lag means month-3 is often missing, exactly when quarter-end demand swings occur.
- Red team (reviewer): KOMTRAX measures Komatsu earthmoving machines, while rental fleets (HRI, CTOS) are dominated by aerial work platforms, telehandlers and trucks, so hours on excavators are a weak proxy for rental time utilization and no proxy for rental rate, which drives margin; the series is a public, press-reported macro construction-activity read that moves with ISM, housing starts and weather and is therefore largely absorbed by the sector-ETF benchmark the target is measured against; and month 3 of every quarter arrives after most releases, so the feature is a two-month partial read.

## N37 Off-cycle fiscal-quarter peers as nowcasters: reporters whose quarter overlaps two of the three months of calendar-quarter peers and lands 3-6 weeks earlier

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: Multiple: homebuilding and building products (Lennar/KB Home, Nov FY), freight and logistics (FedEx, May FY), railcars (Greenbrier, Aug FY), industrial distribution (MSC Industrial, Aug FY), coatings and building materials (RPM, May FY), packaged food (General Mills, Conagra, Lamb Weston, May FY; McCormick, Nov FY), restaurants (Darden, May FY), office furniture (Steelcase, Feb FY; MillerKnoll, May FY), steel processing (Commercial Metals, Aug FY; Worthington, May FY), EMS (Jabil, Aug FY), uniforms/services (Cintas, May FY), footwear and apparel (Nike, May FY). Target: revenue, EPS surprise, guidance (both).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: This is the intra-industry information transfer / industry lead-lag effect, published since the 1990s and shown by Chung, Hrazdil and Trottier (2015, 2014) to have decayed to insignificance in recent samples, especially under GICS peer definitions. The first-order transfer is priced on the predictor's day and sell-side read-through notes after Lennar, FedEx, Nike, Darden and General Mills are routine, so the only residual claimed (after controlling for the day-of reaction) is exactly what the overreaction literature measured and later found gone. The free data cannot supply historical revenue consensus for the predictor (Nasdaq forecast is snapshot-only), so the 'surprise vs own consensus' predictor is not backtestable at $0 without proxies. Expected residual is below this account's costs.
- Mechanism: A firm whose fiscal quarter ends one month before the calendar quarter reports results and next-quarter guidance that cover two of the three months its calendar-quarter peers and customers are still accumulating, and it does so 3-6 weeks before they report (e.g., Lennar's Sep-Nov quarter reported mid-December predicts the Oct-Dec quarter that Taylor Morrison, Meritage, M/I Homes and the building-products names report in late January-February; Greenbrier's Sep-Nov quarter reported in early January predicts Trinity and GATX; FedEx's Sep-Nov quarter reported mid-December predicts Forward Air, Hub Group, ArcBest and Saia). The off-cycle reporter's revenue surprise and guidance versus its own consensus is therefore a partial nowcast of the peers' quarter, not just a correlated prior-quarter print. LITERATURE: intra-industry information transfer is well documented (Ramnath 2002, Journal of Accounting Research; Hou 2007, Review of Financial Studies on industry lead-lag), and Thomas and Zhang (2008, Journal of Accounting Research, 'Overreaction to intra-industry information transfers?') report that late announcers' prices overreact to early announcers' news and partially reverse at their own announcement, so the sign must be tested rather than assumed.
- Industry context: Roughly a fifth of US companies use fiscal years that end in months other than December, so within many sub-industries one firm closes its books a month early and reports while its peers are still inside their quarter. Its print is the only audited look at the same demand months before the peers speak.
- Measurement window: Two of the three months of the target's quarter (the overlap), plus the predictor's guidance for the following quarter, which spans the target's third month. Available 14-35 days before the target's release depending on the pair.. Lead time: 21 days.
- Universe coverage: ~15-20%: 200-250 names in sub-industries that contain at least one off-cycle reporter. Example targets (REASONED; verify caps): Taylor Morrison (TMHC), Meritage (MTH), M/I Homes (MHO), Installed Building Products (IBP), Trinity (TRN), GATX (GATX), Forward Air (FWRD), Hub Group (HUBG), ArcBest (ARCB), Texas Roadhouse (TXRH), Brinker (EAT), HNI (HNI), Post (POST), TreeHouse (THS), Olympic Steel (ZEUS), Ryerson (RYI), Plexus (PLXS), Benchmark (BHE).
- Hypothesis: A revenue or guidance surprise of at least two standard deviations (relative to the predictor's own surprise history) by an off-cycle reporter whose fiscal quarter overlaps at least two months of the target's quarter, measured at least 10 days before the target's release, predicts a same-sign close T-1 to close T+2 abnormal return versus the sector ETF and a same-sign revenue surprise for the not-yet-reported peers, controlling for the peer's abnormal return on the predictor's announcement day, 30-day consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum; a significant opposite-sign coefficient would instead confirm the Thomas-Zhang reversal.
- Who already knows: Sell-side publishes explicit read-through notes after Lennar, FedEx, Nike, Darden, General Mills and MSC prints, and the peers' prices move on the predictor's day, so the first-order transfer is priced (LITERATURE, Thomas and Zhang 2008). The exploitable part is the residual for low-coverage small-caps after controlling for the peer's reaction on the predictor's day, and the possibility that the sign is a reversal rather than a continuation.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): No vendor known to sell a fiscal-offset pair map (UNKNOWN); Bloomberg peer/supply-chain functions provide the raw relationships.. Why ours differs: Systematic pair construction from XBRL fiscal-period-end data across the whole universe, with the predictor's guidance quantified against its own consensus, and the target's day-of reaction used as a control to isolate under- or over-reaction.
- Vendors found by reviewer: NONE FOUND in two searches: no vendor selling a fiscal-offset pair map was seen; results were academic and practitioner commentary only
- Literature found by reviewer: Thomas and Zhang 2008, Journal of Accounting Research 46(4) 909-940, 'Overreaction to intra-industry information transfers?' - https://ideas.repec.org/a/bla/joares/v46y2008i4p909-940.html; Chung, Hrazdil and Trottier 2015, Journal of Banking & Finance 60, 'On the efficiency of intra-industry information transfers: The demise of the overreaction anomaly' - https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:eee:jbfina:v:60:y:2015:i:c:p:153-167; Chung, Hrazdil and Trottier 2014, American Journal of Business 29(1), 'Industry classification and the efficiency of intra-industry information transfers' (anomaly disappears in recent years under GICS) - https://ideas.repec.org/a/eme/ajbpps/v29y2014i1p95-111.html; Secondary citing paper, Journal of Risk and Financial Management 18(1):42 (early-announcer news split into current-quarter and future-quarter components) - https://mdpi.com/1911-8074/18/1/42; Practitioner note 'Reading across a supply chain from a single earnings report' (read-across trades are routine) - https://www.bit.com/helpcenter/articles/16553603
- Data sources:
  - Nasdaq earnings calendar (actual and scheduled release dates for predictor and targets) [DATA] https://api.nasdaq.com/api/calendar/earnings?date=2026-11-12 (update: Daily; history: One day per request; build the archive forward and backfill from EDGAR 8-K Item 2.02 dates)
  - SEC XBRL companyfacts (fiscal period end dates via dei/us-gaap context periods; revenue by fiscal quarter) [DATA] https://data.sec.gov/api/xbrl/companyfacts/CIK0000320193.json (update: On filing; history: XBRL era, ~2009 onward)
  - SEC Financial Statement Data Sets (fye and period fields for building the fiscal-offset pair map across the universe) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/files/dera/data/financial-statement-data-sets/2025q3.zip (update: Quarterly; history: 2009 onward)
  - Nasdaq analyst earnings forecast (consensus for predictor and targets; archive daily) [DATA] https://api.nasdaq.com/api/analyst/AAPL/earnings-forecast (update: Daily snapshot; history: Snapshot only)
  - Motley Fool earnings call transcripts (predictor's guidance language; ToS flag noted in the working file, read not bulk-scrape) [DATA] https://www.fool.com/earnings-call-transcripts/ (update: Per call; history: Dated paths, multi-year)
  - Yahoo Finance chart API (target's reaction on the predictor's announcement day, used as a control) [DATA] https://query1.finance.yahoo.com/v8/finance/chart/AAPL?range=5d&interval=1d (update: Daily; history: Decades)
- Reviewer source checks: https://api.nasdaq.com/api/calendar/earnings?date=2026-11-12 -> DATA (phase0 list; not re-probed) Snapshot per day; backfill from EDGAR 8-K 2.02 dates; https://data.sec.gov/api/xbrl/companyfacts/CIK0000320193.json -> DATA (phase0 list) Fiscal period ends available; revenue by quarter; https://www.sec.gov/files/dera/data/financial-statement-data-sets/2025q3.zip -> DATA (phase0 list) fye/period fields for the pair map; https://api.nasdaq.com/api/analyst/AAPL/earnings-forecast -> DATA (phase0 list) Snapshot only: no historical revenue consensus for the predictor's surprise, so…; https://www.fool.com/earnings-call-transcripts/ -> DATA (phase0 list, ToS flag) Read-only; bulk scraping is a ToS question. Use 8-K EX-99.1 press releases from…; https://query1.finance.yahoo.com/v8/finance/chart/AAPL?range=5d&interval=1d -> DATA (phase0 list) Control: target's reaction on the predictor's day
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 1 / 2 / 2. Confidence: 15%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): The literature already documents both the transfer and an overreaction-then-reversal, and read-through notes are a sell-side staple, so any residual is small; the predictor's mix (Lennar's geographies, FedEx's parcel mix) differs from the targets; and what looks like a lead may just be post-earnings-announcement drift of the predictor transmitted through factor exposure.
- Red team (reviewer): What looks like a lead is the predictor's post-earnings-announcement drift transmitted through sector beta and industry momentum (Hou 2007), plus a mix mismatch (Lennar geographies vs small homebuilders, FedEx parcel vs LTL); after the day-of reaction, 30-day revisions, sector and momentum controls the coefficient is an unstable sign test on roughly 200 names, and the 2015 evidence says the market already corrected this.

## N38 REIT supplemental tenant tables as an early store-count and distress feed for retail, restaurant and fitness tenants

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: Consumer discretionary and staples: multi-unit brick-and-mortar retailers, restaurants, fitness and consumer services with 100+ leased locations. Target: revenue (unit growth), guidance (store openings), distress (both).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: Top-tenant tables in REIT supplementals list only the top 20-40 tenants by ABR, which are mostly mega-caps outside the universe (TJX, Ross, Kroger, Dollar Tree, Burlington); a mid-cap tenant appears in a handful of landlords covering perhaps 5-15% of its fleet, so a 3% QoQ change in sampled lease count is dominated by the REIT's own acquisitions, dispositions and redevelopments rather than tenant action. Watch-list and bankruptcy tables appear after the distress is public (dockets, press). Openings are guided and pre-announced, and Coresight, dataplor and Placer already sell openings/closures at scale; the REIT version is neither faster (supplementals land 3-4 weeks after quarter-end) nor more granular. PDF table extraction across 15-20 formats is high effort for a sampled, landlord-driven count.
- Mechanism: Shopping-center, mall and net-lease REITs (Kimco, Regency, Brixmor, Phillips Edison, Kite Realty, Tanger, Macerich, Agree Realty, NNN REIT, Essential Properties, Four Corners, NetSTREIT, Acadia, Urban Edge, InvenTrust, Federal Realty) file quarterly supplemental packages as 8-K EX-99 exhibits listing their top tenants with number of leases/stores and ABR, plus tenant watch-lists, bankruptcy exposure tables and lease-termination fee income. REITs report in late October/January/April/July, before January-fiscal-year retailers (late Nov/Feb/May/Aug) and before calendar-quarter restaurants and fitness chains that report in the second half of the following month. Aggregating one tenant's lease count across 15-20 REIT supplementals quarter over quarter gives a sampled net opening/closure count 3-6 weeks before the tenant discloses unit growth, and a tenant's appearance on a watch-list or in a bankruptcy table is a distress read-through. Applies to tenants whose revenue growth is unit-driven and whose consensus embeds a guided opening count.
- Industry context: Most US retailers and restaurant chains lease their locations, and a handful of public REITs own tens of thousands of those boxes; because landlords report before tenants and must disclose top-tenant exposure, the landlord's filings are an early, independent sample of the tenant's unit base and credit health.
- Measurement window: The REIT's quarter, which coincides with the calendar quarter; for January-fiscal-year retailers the REIT quarter covers two of the retailer's three months, for calendar-quarter restaurants and fitness chains all three. Available 14-35 days before the tenant's release.. Lead time: 21 days.
- Universe coverage: ~6%: about 80 names. Likely fits (REASONED; verify caps): Five Below (FIVE), Ollie's (OLLI), Academy Sports (ASO), Boot Barn (BOOT), Floor and Decor (FND), Planet Fitness (PLNT), Dave and Buster's (PLAY), Sally Beauty (SBH), Shake Shack (SHAK), Cava (CAVA), First Watch (FWRG), Life Time (LTH), Mister Car Wash (MCW), Sweetgreen (SG).
- Hypothesis: A net change of at least 3% quarter over quarter in a tenant's aggregated lease count across at least 10 REIT supplementals, or a first appearance on any landlord's watch-list or bankruptcy table, measured at least 10 days before the tenant's earnings release, predicts a same-sign revenue surprise versus consensus and close T-1 to close T+2 abnormal return versus XLY (negative for watch-list appearances), controlling for guided unit growth, 30-day consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: REIT analysts track tenant exposure from the REIT's side and retail analysts track openings from the retailer's own disclosures and press releases; cross-REIT aggregation per tenant is not a standard product (REASONED). Coresight Research publishes aggregate US store opening/closure tallies (REASONED), and Placer.ai sells foot traffic, which is a different measure. For mid-cap tenants the sampled store-count change is unlikely to be in consensus before the print.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Placer.ai (foot traffic, not unit counts); Coresight Research (store closure tallies, REASONED); Green Street (REIT and tenant analytics, REASONED). None known to sell per-tenant cross-REIT lease-count diffs (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: Free regulatory exhaust, tenant-mapped across landlords, measured as net unit change and watch-list mentions, and timed to the 3-6 week gap between landlord and tenant reporting.
- Vendors found by reviewer: Coresight Research Weekly US Store Openings and Closures Tracker (paywalled, weekly, retailer-level) - https://coresight.com/research_product_type/store-trackers/; Coresight Store Intelligence Platform (infers openings/closures from store-location databases) - https://coresight.com/store-intelligence-platform-overview/; Placer.ai foot traffic (Land & Buildings short case study on a REIT) - https://www.placer.ai/library/land-buildings-enters-public-short-position; dataplor location data (openings, closures, historical status tracking) - https://www.dataplor.com/location-data/; S&P Global Market Intelligence note on short sellers targeting retail REITs via store closures - https://www.spglobal.com/marketintelligence/en/mi/research-analysis/15032017-equities-retail-reits-singled-out-by-short-sellers.html
- Literature found by reviewer: No paper on cross-landlord tenant lease-count aggregation found. Adjacent only: PropertyChronicle 2025 on ML forecasts of single-tenant lease renewals - https://www.propertychronicle.com/?p=26138; Cornell eCommons 2011 Liu 'What's bad for the goose...' (title seen in results, content not verified) - https://ecommons.cornell.edu/bitstream/handle/1813/70895/2011_Liu_Whats_bad_for_goose.pdf?sequence=1
- Data sources:
  - EDGAR submissions API per REIT CIK (8-K rows carry an items field such as '2.02,9.01' and the exhibit list; TESTED HERE 2026-11-11 for Kimco CIK 879101: HTTP 200, items present) [DATA] https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000879101.json (update: On filing; history: Recent window per response; older via the submissions 'files' paging and EDGAR daily index)
  - EDGAR daily index (to enumerate all 8-K filings by day and fetch EX-99 supplemental PDFs/HTML; PDF table extraction needed) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/daily-index/2025/QTR4/ (update: Daily; history: Archive by year/quarter; supplementals as 8-K exhibits since ~2005 (REASONED))
  - REIT investor-relations sites (alternative source for supplementals; Brixmor returned HTTP 403 to plain curl, TESTED HERE, so rely on EDGAR exhibits) [UNPROBED] https://www.brixmor.com/investors (update: Quarterly; history: Varies)
  - Nasdaq earnings calendar (tenant release dates) [DATA] https://api.nasdaq.com/api/calendar/earnings?date=2026-11-12 (update: Daily; history: One day per request)
- Reviewer source checks: https://www.brixmor.com/investors -> PROBED 403 IR site blocks plain curl; rely on EDGAR 8-K EX-99 exhibits only; https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000879101.json -> DATA (phase0 list) 8-K items field present; supplementals are furnished exhibits, typically PDF, w…; https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/daily-index/2025/QTR4/ -> DATA (phase0 list) Enumerate 8-K filings by day; https://api.nasdaq.com/api/calendar/earnings?date=2026-11-12 -> DATA (phase0 list) Tenant release dates
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 10%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): REIT portfolios are a small, non-random sample of a tenant's fleet, and lease counts change when the REIT buys or sells centers rather than when the tenant acts; openings are already guided and pre-announced; unit growth moves revenue modestly compared with comparable-store sales; and lease signing precedes store opening by quarters.
- Red team (reviewer): The measured quantity is landlord portfolio turnover, not tenant unit growth: a Kimco disposition of three centers removes a tenant's leases with no change in the tenant's fleet, and lease signing precedes opening by quarters, so any correlation with the tenant's revenue surprise is spurious or already in guided unit counts.

## N39 Earnings-triggered early lock-up release clauses in 424B4 prospectuses as a forced-supply overhang in the post-earnings window of recent IPOs

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: All sectors; companies within roughly 12 months of IPO with market cap above $500M and listed options. Target: abnormal return in the post-event window (supply overhang), secondary: EPS/revenue surprise reaction asymmetry (forward).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: (1) Reclassify: this is an announcement-window supply overhang, not a nowcast of the quarter, so it fails program criterion (a) and must be used as an overlay (stand aside on longs, or a defined-risk put spread only when the CBOE implied move is not elevated vs size-matched recent IPOs), not as an earnings-surprise predictor. (2) Parse the price condition: many clauses release only if the stock trades X% above the IPO price for N of M days; compute the condition state daily and flag an event only when release is unconditional or the condition is met 5 trading days before the print. (3) Correct the examples: per the sources, Snowflake and DoorDash early releases were time-based (91 days) while Airbnb, Reddit, OneStream and Wish were tied to the second earnings release; verify each 424B4's 'Lock-up Agreements' / 'Shares Eligible for Future Sale' section by regex, and distinguish 'blackout pull-forward' clauses (release BEFORE the pre-earnings blackout, i.e. outside the event window) from post-release clauses. (4) Widen the backtest to 2020-2026 IPOs (not the last 12 months) to reach 150+ events; take releasable shares from the prospectus cap table and ADV from Yahoo. (5) Benchmark against fixed-date expirations of matched IPOs to show the earnings-conditional version is distinct from the generic lock-up effect. (6) Watch for underwriter early-release waivers (Kelly, Lv and Twite) announced by 8-K or press release, which move the event date.
- Mechanism: Since about 2020 many IPO lock-up agreements release a tranche (often 15-35% of locked shares, sometimes all shares if the stock trades a set percentage above the IPO price) a fixed number of trading days after the company's first or second earnings release following the IPO, instead of at a fixed 180-day date (REASONED; widely reported in 2020-2021 prospectuses such as Snowflake, Airbnb and DoorDash; must be verified per 424B4). The earnings announcement is therefore also a mechanical supply event: employees, VCs and pre-IPO holders can sell from T+1 or T+2, which skews the post-announcement price path negative regardless of the surprise and mutes the reaction to a beat. LITERATURE: fixed-date lock-up expirations carry roughly -1% to -3% abnormal returns with volume spikes (Field and Hanka 2001, Journal of Finance; Bradley, Jordan, Roten and Yi 2001, Journal of Financial Research); the earnings-conditional version concentrates that supply inside the 2-10 day event window this program trades. Applies to any recent IPO whose 424B4 lock-up section references an earnings release.
- Industry context: IPO lock-ups stop insiders selling for about six months, and underwriters increasingly tie the release to the first post-IPO earnings report so that selling happens when the market has fresh information. That turns a routine earnings date into a scheduled supply shock for thinly floated new listings.
- Measurement window: Binary clause flag plus releasable shares divided by average daily volume, both known from the 424B4 at IPO; the earnings date is confirmed roughly 30 days ahead. Fully available before the event.. Lead time: 30 days.
- Universe coverage: ~2-4%: 25-50 IPO names per year above $500M with listed options, financials excluded; backtest sample 2020-2026 of roughly 150-250 events
- Hypothesis: Among IPOs within 18 months of listing whose 424B4 ties a lock-up release to an earnings release, where the release is unconditional or its price condition is met 5 trading days before the earnings date, and whose releasable shares exceed 10 times ADV, the close T+1 to close T+5 abnormal return versus the sector ETF is at least 1.5 pp lower than for size-, sector- and age-matched IPOs with fixed-date lock-ups, controlling for the earnings surprise, the pre-event implied move, consensus revisions and momentum; and the T-1 to T+1 reaction to a positive surprise is significantly smaller for the clause group.
- Who already knows: IPO desks, underwriters and IPO research shops track lock-up calendars; Renaissance Capital publishes IPO calendars and lock-up expirations (REASONED). Fixed-date expiries are crowded; the earnings-conditional clauses are tracked less systematically and are not part of EPS consensus (REASONED). Options implied volatility around these events may already embed the overhang, which is testable from the CBOE snapshots.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Renaissance Capital and IPO Boutique (IPO calendars including lock-up dates, REASONED); Bloomberg carries lock-up expiration fields. None known to flag earnings-conditional release clauses with releasable-shares-to-ADV ratios (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: Reads the clause itself from the prospectus, computes releasable float relative to ADV, and trades the overhang inside the 1-5 day post-earnings window with defined-risk puts or by standing aside on longs.
- Vendors found by reviewer: Renaissance Capital IPO Pro (lock-up expiration dates behind subscription; free articles document earnings-based early-release provisions) - https://www.renaissancecapital.com/IPO-Center/News/89910/the-erosion-of-the-ipo-lock-up; Renaissance Capital 'Back so soon? Early lock-up provisions return to the IPO market' - https://renaissancecapital.com/IPO-Center/News/101000/Back-so-soon-Early-lock-up-provisions-return-to-the-IPO-market-with-VC-back; Cooley CapX 'Early lock-up releases: overview and trends' (law-firm practitioner note on earnings-date triggers and blackout pull-forwards) - https://capx.cooley.com/2025/01/20/early-lock-up-releases-overview-and-trends/; lambdafin 'IPO lockup expiration impact' (claims mean -4.8% 10-day return; unverified) - https://www.lambdafin.com/articles/ipo-lockup-expiration-impact
- Literature found by reviewer: Kelly, Lv and Twite 2024, University of Melbourne Finance WP 2024-05, 'Early release from lockup: insider sales during the post-IPO lockup period' - https://fbe.unimelb.edu.au/__data/assets/pdf_file/0006/5175618/UoMFinanceWP_2024_05.pdf; TechCrunch 2022 'The year of the disappearing lock-up' (Snowflake 25% at 91 days, DoorDash 40% at 91 days: time-based, not earnings-based) - https://techcrunch.com/2022/01/04/the-year-of-the-disappearing-lock-up; Cohen Milstein 'Locked out: how changing post-IPO lockup practices threaten Section 11 recovery' (Airbnb, Reddit, OneStream, Wish lock-ups expiring after the second earnings release) - https://www.cohenmilstein.com/locked-out-how-changing-post-ipo-lockup-practices-threaten-section-11-recovery/; Financial Management 2007 36(3) 63-80 (lock-up paper seen in results, title not confirmed) - https://ideas.repec.org/a/bla/finmgt/v36y2007i3p63-80.html; Candidate-cited Field and Hanka 2001 (Journal of Finance) and Bradley et al. 2001 were NOT re-verified in these searches
- Data sources:
  - EDGAR latest filings feed, type=424B4 (same getcurrent endpoint the working file lists for Form 4 and 8-K; TESTED HERE 2026-11-11 with a declared SEC User-Agent: HTTP 200) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=424B4&count=100&output=atom (update: Rolling, poll daily; history: Latest filings only; backfill from the daily index)
  - EDGAR daily form index (enumerate 424B4 filings by day, then fetch each prospectus and regex the lock-up section for 'earnings') [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/daily-index/2025/QTR4/form.20251105.idx (update: Daily; history: Archive by year/quarter back to 1994)
  - SEC Form 3/4/5 insider transactions bulk data (confirm post-release selling ex post) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/data-research/sec-markets-data/insider-transactions-data-sets (update: Quarterly bulk; daily via the Form 4 feed; history: 2006q1 to 2026q3)
  - CBOE delayed option quotes (implied move around the event, to check how much is priced) [DATA] https://cdn.cboe.com/api/global/delayed_quotes/options/AAPL.json (update: Delayed intraday snapshot; archive daily; history: Snapshot only)
  - Nasdaq earnings calendar (release dates) [DATA] https://api.nasdaq.com/api/calendar/earnings?date=2026-11-12 (update: Daily; history: One day per request)
- Reviewer source checks: https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=424B4&count=100… -> PROBED 200 Declared SEC User-Agent; at 05:02 EST returned 'No recent filings' (same-day ro…; https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/daily-index/2025/QTR4/form.20251105.idx -> DATA (phase0 list) Backfill 424B4 filings 2020-2026; https://www.sec.gov/data-research/sec-markets-data/insider-transactions-data-se… -> DATA (phase0 list) Ex-post confirmation of post-release selling; https://cdn.cboe.com/api/global/delayed_quotes/options/AAPL.json -> DATA (phase0 list) Implied move to test whether the overhang is already priced; archive daily; https://api.nasdaq.com/api/calendar/earnings?date=2026-11-12 -> DATA (phase0 list) Release dates
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 3 / 1. Confidence: 30%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): IPO desks know the clauses, so the overhang may be discounted before the event or embedded in option prices, making puts expensive; many clauses are price-conditional (only if the stock is 25-33% above the IPO price), which creates selection toward winners; and 30-50 events a year give little statistical power after costs.
- Red team (reviewer): IPO desks, Renaissance IPO Pro and Bloomberg lock-up fields already carry these dates and recent-IPO option IV already embeds the supply, so puts are expensive; the price-conditional variant selects post-IPO winners (momentum / IPO drift confound); and with 25-50 noisy, high-idiosyncratic-vol events a year the measured effect may be the generic fixed-date lock-up expiration return rather than anything earnings-specific.

## N40 Used-equipment auction values and dealer used-inventory levels (Sandhills monthly market reports, ELFA, RB Global) as a margin and order nowcast for equipment dealers and OEMs

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: Industrials: equipment dealers, truck and trailer OEMs, ag and construction OEMs, equipment rental, truck-lessor fleets. Target: margin (used-inventory write-downs, disposal gains), revenue/orders, guidance (both).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: The used-value cycle is published monthly by Sandhills (free), ACT Research and J.D. Power (paid but syndicated in the trade press the same week) and is cited by sell-side, so it is in consensus; it is macro-cyclical rather than firm-specific, and for truck and trailer OEMs the ACT/FTR order series is the direct nowcast analysts already use. The free Sandhills reports are category-level percentage headlines with no downloadable history, so a backtest requires parsing years of press releases. The dealers where used-inventory write-downs actually bite (Alta, Titan) are at or below the $500M floor, leaving Rush, Ryder, Herc and OEMs for which the index adds little beyond the sector ETF. RB Global's IR site returns 403. Write-downs cluster at fiscal year-end, breaking the quarter alignment.
- Mechanism: Sandhills Global publishes monthly market reports on used construction equipment, farm machinery, heavy-duty trucks and semi-trailers listed on its marketplaces, with inventory levels and asking and auction values, month over month and year over year (TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: news page live with headlines such as 'Used Truck and Trailer Inventory Down in October as Semi-Trailer Auction Values Jump Nearly 20%' and monthly construction-equipment inventory reports). Used values set trade-in economics: when used values fall while used inventory swells, dealers carry aging used stock and take write-downs (Titan Machinery, Rush Enterprises, Alta Equipment), rental fleets' disposal gains shrink (Herc, Custom Truck), and OEM new orders slow because customers cannot trade up (Wabash, Hyster-Yale, AGCO, CNH, Lindsay, Blue Bird, REV). Rising used values with falling inventory signal replacement demand and pricing power. ELFA's monthly CapEx Finance Index (new equipment-finance volume) corroborates the new-equipment side. Applies to dealers and OEMs whose margins depend on used-equipment values and whose new orders depend on trade-in cycles.
- Industry context: New equipment is mostly sold against a trade-in, so the used market sets both the buyer's ability to upgrade and the dealer's inventory risk. Sandhills runs the largest US online marketplaces for used machinery and trucks and publishes monthly inventory and value trends from its own listings.
- Measurement window: Months 1-2 of the quarter are certain 10 days before most releases; month 3 arrives 10-20 days after month-end (REASONED from release headlines), so it is available for dealers and OEMs reporting 30+ days after quarter-end and for FY-offset names (Titan Machinery FQ Oct, reports early December).. Lead time: 10 days.
- Universe coverage: ~3%: about 35 names. Likely fits (REASONED; verify caps): Rush Enterprises (RUSHA), Wabash (WNC), AGCO (AGCO), CNH (CNH), Lindsay (LNN), Alamo (ALG), Terex (TEX), Herc (HRI), Custom Truck One Source (CTOS), Allison Transmission (ALSN), Blue Bird (BLBD), REV Group (REVG), Ryder (R), Miller Industries (MLR); Titan Machinery (TITN) and Hyster-Yale (HY) near the $500M floor.
- Hypothesis: A decline of at least 5% YoY in Sandhills auction values combined with a rise of at least 10% YoY in used inventory for a dealer's or OEM's equipment category, measured 10 days before the earnings release, predicts a negative gross-margin surprise and negative close T-1 to close T+2 abnormal return versus XLI, with the effect scaled by used inventory as a share of total inventory, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Ag and construction sell-side cites Sandhills and auction results, and ACT Research and J.D. Power sell used-truck price data to trucking and OEM analysts (REASONED). The used-value cycle is largely priced for the large OEMs; dealer-level used-inventory write-down risk (Titan, Rush) is covered by few analysts (REASONED).
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Sandhills Global (free headline reports; paid granular data); Rouse Services / RB Global (used-value and rental analytics, REASONED); ACT Research and J.D. Power Valuation Services (used-truck pricing, REASONED).. Why ours differs: Uses the free monthly headline indices, maps them to dealer used-inventory exposure (from the dealer's own balance-sheet disclosure of used inventory) rather than to the OEM cycle, and times the read to the dealer's quarter.
- Vendors found by reviewer: Sandhills Global monthly market reports (free by sign-up on its trade sites, syndicated as press releases; granular data is paid) - https://www.truckpaper.com/blog/how-to-and-tips/2025/12/make-data-driven-decisions-with-sandhills-globals-monthly-market-reports; Sandhills press release on PRNewswire (asking/auction value spread) - https://prnewswire.com/news-releases/asking-auction-value-spread-reaches-historic-highs-in-several-ag-equipment-markets-302191112.html; ACT Research 'State of the Industry: U.S. Classes 3-8 Used Trucks' (paid; monthly figures syndicated in trade press) - https://www.fleetequipmentmag.com/act-research-july-used-truck-prices/; J.D. Power commercial truck used-value monthly report (paid; syndicated) - https://www.truckinginfo.com/news/used-class-8-prices-continue-to-rise-amid-tight-supply; Transport Topics coverage of ACT used Class 8 prices and volumes - https://www.ttnews.com/articles/used-class-8-sales-0626
- Literature found by reviewer: NONE FOUND: no paper or practitioner report linking used-equipment indices to dealer or OEM earnings surprises was seen
- Data sources:
  - Sandhills Global market reports (news page; monthly construction, farm, truck and trailer inventory and value reports). TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: HTTP 200, keyless. [UNPROBED] https://www.sandhills.com/news (update: Monthly, roughly 10-20 days after month-end (REASONED from headline dates); history: Articles on site back several years (REASONED); build a parsed archive forward)
  - ELFA CapEx Finance Index (monthly new business volume of equipment finance). TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: HTTP 200. [UNPROBED] https://www.elfaonline.org/research/capex-finance-index (update: Monthly, ~3-4 weeks after month-end; history: UNKNOWN from this probe (series dates to 2004; REASONED))
  - RB Global investor relations (quarterly GTV and used-equipment commentary; rbauction.com itself returned HTTP 403 to curl, TESTED HERE, and its auction-results pages should not be scraped) [UNPROBED] https://investor.rbglobal.com/ (update: Quarterly; history: Multi-year filings)
  - FMCSA SAFER / QCMobile (carrier counts as a demand-side control for truck and trailer OEMs) [KEY] https://mobile.fmcsa.dot.gov/qc/services/carriers/docket-number/139506?webKey=DEMO (update: Snapshot; history: Current snapshot)
- Reviewer source checks: https://www.sandhills.com/news -> PROBED 200 41.8 KB page; headlines confirmed (e.g. 'Used Truck and Trailer Inventory Down …; https://www.elfaonline.org/research/capex-finance-index -> PROBED 200 152 KB page; monthly index page reachable; https://investor.rbglobal.com/ -> PROBED 403 Blocked to curl; do not scrape; use EDGAR filings for RB Global GTV; https://mobile.fmcsa.dot.gov/qc/services/carriers/docket-number/139506?webKey=D… -> KEY (phase0 list) Free registration; snapshot only
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 1 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 15%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Category indices are coarse and lag OEM dealer-inventory disclosures that analysts already model; used values are a macro-cyclical factor rather than firm-specific information; report timing varies month to month; and dealer write-downs are often taken at fiscal year-end rather than in the quarter the index moves.
- Red team (reviewer): Used-equipment values are a lagging, macro-cyclical factor largely absorbed by the XLI regression and by the freight/ag cycle variables analysts already model; any residual is a proxy for the industrial cycle, not information about the firm's quarter, and the month-3 reading lands after the print for calendar-quarter OEMs.

## N41 Counterparty exhaust: 8-K Item 1.02 terminations and Item 1.01 supply agreements filed by small companies that name a mid-cap customer or supplier

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: All sectors; strongest in industrials, healthcare services and devices, technology hardware, consumer brands with concentrated supply chains. Target: revenue, margin, guidance, capex (both).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: Materiality runs the wrong way: an Item 1.02 is filed because the agreement is material to the small filer, and every example found (Methode/Delphi, Amincor/Aldi, XPEL/entrotech, Wright/Novartis) names a counterparty that is either outside the universe or for whom the contract is a rounding error; anything large enough to move a mid-cap's quarter would be disclosed by the mid-cap itself. Cohen-Frazzini's inattention effect runs from a large customer's return to its small suppliers, not from a small filer's 8-K to its large counterparty. Events are roughly 10-30 per quarter across the universe, dominated by credit, lease and employment 1.02s, and the sign is ambiguous when the larger party terminates in its own interest. Data is free and lawful but the signal is too sparse and too immaterial to justify a standalone build; keep the 8-K items filter only as a shared EDGAR utility.
- Mechanism: When a small public supplier or customer files an 8-K Item 1.02 (termination of a material definitive agreement) or Item 1.01 (entry into one), it must describe the agreement and usually names the counterparty, which is often a larger company for which the change is immaterial or will only be disclosed at earnings. A supplier's termination reveals a customer's program cancellation, insourcing, destocking or capex cut that hits the customer's next quarter (revenue, margin or guidance); a small supplier's new supply agreement reveals a mid-cap customer's capacity expansion or product ramp before the customer guides to it. The SEC submissions API exposes an items field for every 8-K (TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: Kimco rows show '1.01,2.03,3.02,9.01'), and the daily form index lists every 8-K (TESTED HERE: 594 8-Ks on 2025-11-05) whose full-submission header carries 'ITEM INFORMATION' lines (TESTED HERE), so Item 1.01/1.02 filings can be isolated without full-text search; counterparties are tagged by matching the filing text against the universe name list and GLEIF legal names. Applies to any universe company that appears as a named counterparty.
- Industry context: The 8-K rules force a company to disclose within four business days when it enters or terminates a contract material to it, and materiality is judged from the filer's size, so a $300M supplier must disclose a contract that a $10B customer can ignore. The small filer's disclosure is therefore the customer's information, published early.
- Measurement window: Event-driven: filings land 4 business days after the triggering event, typically 20-60 days before the counterparty's next earnings release; the signal is the count and classification of counterparty-naming 1.01/1.02 filings in the 90 days before the release.. Lead time: 20 days.
- Universe coverage: Potentially the whole universe but sparse: perhaps 10-30 universe companies per quarter are named as counterparties (REASONED, ~2% per quarter). Types: mid-cap OEMs named by component suppliers, device makers named by contract manufacturers, retailers named by brand suppliers, pharma-services names named by small drug developers.
- Hypothesis: An 8-K Item 1.02 filed by another company that names a universe member as the counterparty to a supply or customer agreement, filed at least 5 trading days before that member's earnings release, predicts a negative revenue or guidance surprise and negative close T-1 to close T+2 abnormal return versus the sector ETF for the named member, while a counterparty-naming Item 1.01 supply agreement predicts positive capex or revenue guidance, controlling for the member's abnormal return on the filing day, consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Users of EDGAR full-text search and Bloomberg filing alerts see the filer's 8-K, and the filer's own stock reprices immediately; the named counterparty's analysts often do not read small filers' 8-Ks (REASONED), consistent with the customer-supplier inattention documented by Cohen and Frazzini (2008, Journal of Finance, 'Economic Links and Predictable Returns') (LITERATURE). Little of it is in the counterparty's consensus before the print.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Quiver Quantitative and similar sell SEC-filing-derived alert data in general (REASONED); no vendor known to sell counterparty-side mapping of Item 1.01/1.02 filings at scale (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: Reads the filing from the counterparty's side, classifies agreement type (supply/customer versus debt/lease/employment, which dominate Item 1.02 counts), and times the signal to the counterparty's earnings rather than to the filer's.
- Vendors found by reviewer: FactSet supply-chain relationship data (FactSet Insight 'Propagating momentum information through global supply chain networks') - https://insight.factset.com/propagating-momentum-information-through-global-supply-chain-networks?hs_amp=true; AQR research page hosting Cohen and Frazzini - https://www.aqr.com/Insights/Research/Journal-Article/Economic-Links-and-Predictable-Returns; No vendor selling counterparty-side mapping of 8-K Item 1.01/1.02 filings was seen
- Literature found by reviewer: Cohen and Frazzini 2008, Journal of Finance 63(4) 1977-2011, 'Economic Links and Predictable Returns' (customer momentum: large customer's return predicts small supplier, 10%-customer links) - https://ideas.repec.org/a/bla/jfinan/v63y2008i4p1977-2011.html; Example Item 1.02 filings seen: Methode Electronics 2009 (Delphi terminated supply agreement) - https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0000065270/000110465909054273/a09-26104_18k.htm; XPEL 2022 (entrotech supply agreement non-renewal) - https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1767258/000176725822000004/xpel-20220120.htm; Wright Medical 2013 (Novartis non-renewal of supply) - https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0001137861/000100579413000082/wmgi8k122013.htm; Amincor 2012 (Aldi terminated sole-customer relationship) - https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0001167905/000116552712000775/g6160.txt
- Data sources:
  - EDGAR daily form index (all 8-K filings by day; TESTED HERE 2026-11-11 with a declared SEC User-Agent: HTTP 200, 594 8-K rows for 2025-11-05) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/daily-index/2025/QTR4/form.20251105.idx (update: Daily; history: Archive by year/quarter; current 8-K item numbering since August 2004 (REASONED))
  - EDGAR submissions API (items field per 8-K to filter 1.01/1.02; TESTED HERE) [DATA] https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000879101.json (update: On filing; history: Recent window per response plus paged history files)
  - EDGAR full-submission text header (ITEM INFORMATION lines; TESTED HERE 2026-11-11 for 0001865107-25-000073.txt) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1865107/0001865107-25-000073.txt (update: On filing; history: Full archive)
  - EDGAR latest 8-K feed (near-real-time polling) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=8-K&count=100&output=atom (update: Rolling; history: Latest filings only)
  - GLEIF LEI API (legal-name and parent-child matching for counterparty tagging) [DATA] https://api.gleif.org/api/v1/lei-records?filter[entity.legalName]=Apple%20Inc&page[size]=1 (update: Daily; history: Current records)
- Reviewer source checks: https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/daily-index/2025/QTR4/form.20251105.idx -> DATA (phase0 list) All 8-Ks by day; https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000879101.json -> DATA (phase0 list) items field per 8-K; https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1865107/0001865107-25-000073.txt -> DATA (phase0 list) ITEM INFORMATION header lines; https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=8-K&count=100&o… -> DATA (phase0 list) Rolling latest filings; https://api.gleif.org/api/v1/lei-records?filter[entity.legalName]=Apple%20Inc&p… -> DATA (phase0 list) Legal-name matching
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 4 / 1 / 1. Confidence: 10%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Events are rare and most Item 1.02 filings concern credit facilities or leases, so classification errors dominate; the terminating party is often the larger counterparty acting in its own interest, which can be positive for it; the impact on a mid-cap's quarter is usually immaterial; and sophisticated holders of the counterparty may already screen for its name in all filings.
- Red team (reviewer): Materiality asymmetry makes the information set immaterial by construction, so any backtest hit rate would be driven by misclassified credit-facility terminations, sign ambiguity and a handful of lucky events; the named counterparty's analysts do not need to read these filings because the content cannot move its guidance.

## N42 Buyback-intensity blackout exposure from 10-Q monthly repurchase tables: the return of a price-insensitive bid after earnings

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: All sectors; strongest in mid-cap industrials, consumer and technology names with large repurchase programs relative to trading volume. Target: abnormal return in the post-event window (flow-driven); secondary: EPS surprise via lower share count (minor) (forward).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: The one practitioner test found (HedgeFundAlpha) reports no significant return difference around blackouts even for heavy-buyback firms, and the academic literature documents the timing pattern but not a tradeable return effect. The plausible firm-level effect is a few tenths of a percent over T+1 to T+5, below this account's round-trip costs, and the flow (5-10% of ADV) is small relative to post-earnings volume, which runs 3-10x normal. The monthly table is untagged HTML, so building ~350 firms x 8 quarters of parsing is high effort. The month-3-collapse flag is confounded: firms pause when results are weak and 10b5-1 plans run through blackouts. Hong, Wang and Yu (not 'Warner') concerns return variance of unconstrained firms, not announcement drift. The mechanism never produces an EPS or revenue surprise, so it fails program criterion (a).
- Mechanism: Companies that buy back stock in the open market suspend discretionary purchases during the quarterly blackout, usually from about two weeks before quarter-end until one or two trading days after the earnings release, unless they buy through a Rule 10b5-1 plan. Part II Item 2 of each 10-Q and 10-K discloses shares repurchased by month, so for each firm one can measure the repurchase run-rate as a share of average daily volume and whether month-3 purchases collapse relative to months 1-2, which identifies discretionary (non-10b5-1) programs. For firms where buybacks exceed 5-10% of ADV, the pre-earnings window lacks a large steady buyer and the post-earnings window (T+2 to T+5) sees it return, which should support the stock after the print and dampen the downside after a miss. LITERATURE: Hong, Wang and Warner (2008, Journal of Financial Economics, 'Firms as buyers of last resort') show repurchasing firms' prices are less sensitive to negative shocks. Applies to firms with active discretionary programs and float-relative intensity high enough to matter.
- Industry context: Corporate buybacks are one of the largest sources of equity demand, but insider-trading rules push most companies to stop buying in the weeks before results unless they pre-committed through a 10b5-1 plan. The pause and resumption are visible after the fact in the monthly repurchase table every 10-Q must include.
- Measurement window: Trailing two quarters of monthly repurchases from the most recent 10-Q/10-K, available about 40-45 days after the prior quarter-end, hence roughly 45 days before the next earnings release; the month-3-collapse flag classifies discretionary programs.. Lead time: 40 days.
- Universe coverage: ~25-30% have active programs (about 350 names); high-intensity discretionary programs (at least 5% of ADV) perhaps ~8%, about 100 names (REASONED). Types: cash-rich mid-cap industrials, specialty retailers, software and semiconductor names with authorizations above 5% of market cap.
- Hypothesis: For firms whose trailing two-quarter open-market repurchases exceed 5% of average daily volume and whose month-3 purchases are below 30% of the months 1-2 average, measured at least 40 days before the earnings release, the close T+1 to close T+5 abnormal return versus the sector ETF is at least 0.8 percentage points higher than for size-, sector- and surprise-matched non-repurchasers, controlling for the earnings surprise, consensus revisions, size, momentum and the value factor.
- Who already knows: Sell-side and bank buyback desks publish index-level blackout calendars and the share of S&P 500 market cap in blackout (REASONED), so the aggregate effect is widely discussed; firm-level intensity relative to ADV combined with a discretionary-versus-10b5-1 classification from the monthly table is not standard (REASONED). The flow is not in consensus EPS at all; the question is whether the post-print drift is already arbitraged.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): No vendor known to sell firm-level monthly repurchase intensity with blackout classification (UNKNOWN); Bloomberg and S&P carry quarterly buyback totals.. Why ours differs: Firm-level, monthly, free from the 10-Q table, scaled by ADV, and classified by whether purchases stop in month 3, which is the actual marker of the mechanism.
- Vendors found by reviewer: No vendor selling firm-level monthly repurchase intensity with blackout classification was seen; HedgeFundAlpha 'Buyback blackout periods do not negatively impact market performance' (practitioner test: no significant return difference for heavy-buyback firms) - https://hedgefundalpha.com/news/buyback-blackout-periods-do-not-negatively-impact-market-performance%ef%bb%bf/; SeeItMarket 'Stock buybacks, blackout periods, and market returns' (not rigorous; mis-states blackouts as an SEC rule) - https://seeitmarket.com/?p=53384
- Literature found by reviewer: Hong, Wang and Yu 2008, Journal of Financial Economics 88(1) 119-145, 'Firms as buyers of last resort' (candidate mis-cites the third author as 'Warner'; paper is about lower short-horizon return variance of financially unconstrained repurchasers, not a post-earnings drift) - https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/jfinec/v88y2008i1p119-145.html; Tinbergen Institute DP 21-101 'The corporate calendar and the timing of share repurchases and equity-based compensation' (repurchase volume rises ~42% from month 1 to month 2 of the fiscal quarter because of blackouts) - https://papers.tinbergen.nl/21101.pdf; Harvard Law School Forum summary of the same paper - https://corpgov.law.harvard.edu/2022/04/28/the-corporate-calendar-and-the-timing-of-share-repurchases-and-equity-compensation; ECGI WP 'Equity-based compensation and the timing of share repurchases' - https://www.ecgi.global/sites/default/files/2025-06/equity-based-compensation-and-the-timing-of-share-repurchases.pdf; Harvard Law Forum on Busch and Obernberger (RFS 2016) 'Actual share repurchases, price efficiency, and the information content of stock prices' (price support at fundamental value) - https://corpgov.law.harvard.edu/2016/11/16/actual-share-repurchases-price-efficiency-and-the-information-content-of-stock-prices/
- Data sources:
  - EDGAR submissions API (locate each 10-Q/10-K primary document, then parse the 'Issuer Purchases of Equity Securities' monthly table) [DATA] https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000320193.json (update: On filing; history: Recent window plus paged history)
  - SEC XBRL companyfacts (PaymentsForRepurchaseOfCommonStock and StockRepurchasedDuringPeriodShares for quarterly totals and cross-checks) [DATA] https://data.sec.gov/api/xbrl/companyfacts/CIK0000320193.json (update: On filing; history: ~2009 onward)
  - Yahoo Finance chart API (average daily volume for the intensity ratio) [DATA] https://query1.finance.yahoo.com/v8/finance/chart/AAPL?range=5d&interval=1d (update: Daily; history: Decades)
  - SEC Financial Statement and Notes data sets (bulk notes text for repurchase-program disclosures and 10b5-1 mentions) [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/files/dera/data/financial-statement-and-notes-data-sets/2025q3_notes.zip (update: Monthly since 2025; history: 2009q1 onward)
- Reviewer source checks: https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000320193.json -> DATA (phase0 list) Locate 10-Q/10-K; the monthly repurchase table is untagged HTML in Part II Item…; https://data.sec.gov/api/xbrl/companyfacts/CIK0000320193.json -> DATA (phase0 list) Quarterly totals only (PaymentsForRepurchaseOfCommonStock); no monthly breakdown; https://query1.finance.yahoo.com/v8/finance/chart/AAPL?range=5d&interval=1d -> DATA (phase0 list) ADV; https://www.sec.gov/files/dera/data/financial-statement-and-notes-data-sets/202… -> DATA (phase0 list) Notes text for 10b5-1 mentions
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 1 / 1. Confidence: 10%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Many firms now run 10b5-1 plans straight through the blackout, so the flow never stops; the effect is likely a few tenths of a percent, below this account's trading costs; buyback intensity proxies for value and quality factors; and firms pause programs precisely when results are weak, confounding the sign.
- Red team (reviewer): Buyback intensity is a value, quality and low-volatility factor proxy; the 'support' after the print is indistinguishable from the known lower downside variance of cash-rich firms, and the measured drift would be the factor loading, not the flow.

## N43 Securitization-trust 10-D monthly servicer reports as a nowcast of captive-finance credit losses (Harley-Davidson, CarMax)

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Consumer companies with SEC-registered captive-finance ABS: motorcycles (Harley-Davidson Financial Services) and used-auto retail (CarMax Auto Finance). Target: EPS surprise (finance-segment provision), margin (nowcast).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: (1) Harley: the July 2025 KKR/PIMCO deal sold more than $5B of existing receivables and commits about two-thirds of new originations to a forward-flow, so public-trust collateral and HDFS's on-book exposure have diverged structurally; re-map the finance line post-deal, weight HOG lower, and use months 1-2 only because the month-3 10-D files ~27 days after month-end, around the print. (2) Make CarMax the anchor (all three months filed ~10 days before earnings) and add Form ABS-EE EX-102 loan-level XML (public auto ABS since Nov 2016) to compute 30+/60+ delinquency by origination vintage and credit tier, which directly addresses the seasoning and selection critique. (3) Add CNH Industrial via the publicly registered CNH Equipment Trusts (monthly 10-D with EX-99.1 distribution reports), mapping to CNH's financial-services provision; keep a quarterly EDGAR scan for 'Owner Trust', 'Receivables Trust' and 'Equipment Trust' names tied to universe parents. (4) Measure the pool trend in excess of the prior quarter's 10-Q delinquency disclosure and management's provision guidance, so only the un-guided component is tested. (5) Control for one-offs flagged in servicer footnotes (e.g. CarMax's Oct 2021 servicing-system transition). (6) Resolve trust names from CIKs via the submissions API because the company-search atom returns mangled names. Lead time revised to ~7 trading days for the full quarter (months 1-2 available 3-6 weeks ahead).
- Mechanism: Public auto/motorcycle ABS trusts must file a Form 10-D each month with the servicer report: pool balance, 30/60/90+ day delinquencies, net losses and recoveries. HDFS contributes a large share of Harley's operating income and CarMax Auto Finance income (net of its loan-loss provision) is a key swing in CarMax EPS, so pool-balance-weighted delinquency and loss trends across the active trusts give the direction of the provision 2-8 weeks before the parent reports. Applies to consumer discretionary names whose captive lender issues publicly registered ABS (Lithia's LAD trusts appear to be 144A-only: no 10-D found, TESTED HERE).
- Industry context: Harley's and CarMax's captive lenders fund themselves partly through public securitizations, and Reg AB forces monthly disclosure of how those loans are performing. Those same loans are a representative slice of the receivables whose loss provision runs through the parent's income statement.
- Measurement window: Monthly collateral performance. TESTED HERE: Harley-Davidson Motorcycle Trust 2024-B 10-Ds filed 2026-10-28 (Sept), 2026-09-29 (Aug), 2026-08-27 (Jul), i.e. ~27 days after month-end, so for a calendar quarter months 1-2 are in hand ~3-5 weeks before Harley's report and month 3 arrives about the same day. CarMax Auto Owner Trust 10-Ds filed ~15th of the following month (TESTED HERE on trust 2018-2 history), so all three months of CarMax's Feb/May/Aug/Nov quarters are filed ~10 days before its earnings.. Lead time: 7 days.
- Universe coverage: ~0.2-0.3%: KMX (anchor), CNH (via CNH Equipment Trusts), HOG (lower weight after the 2025 HDFS deal); re-scan EDGAR quarterly for new universe parents with public ABS shelves
- Hypothesis: For CarMax and CNH (Harley at lower weight), a rise of at least 25 bp YoY in the pool-balance-weighted 60+ day delinquency rate across the issuer's active public trusts for the quarter's filed months, in excess of the delinquency path implied by the prior quarter's 10-Q disclosure and management's provision guidance, measured 5 trading days before the earnings release, predicts a finance-segment provision above consensus, a negative EPS surprise and a negative T-1 to T+1 abnormal return versus XLY (XLI for CNH), controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: ABS investors and rating-agency surveillance (Moody's, KBRA, S&P) read these monthly; sell-side auto and powersports analysts cite trust data only occasionally. REASONED: the credit trend is semi-public but rarely mapped to the parent's EPS surprise; management typically guides provision direction on the prior call, so part of the trend is in consensus.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Intex and Bloomberg distribute structured-finance collateral performance to fixed-income desks at institutional prices; no vendor known to sell this as an equity-earnings signal for HOG/KMX (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: Free from EDGAR, aggregated across all live trusts and aligned to the parent's fiscal quarter and earnings date, with an explicit mapping to the finance-segment provision line that equity consensus models.
- Vendors found by reviewer: sec-api.io 'Form ABS-EE Files Dataset' (commercial aggregator of the free EDGAR ABS-EE loan-level filings since Nov 2016) - https://sec-api.io/datasets/form-absee-files; Credit Flow Research 'US ABS loan-level data on EDGAR' - https://creditflowresearch.com/us-abs-loan-level-data-on-edgar; MSCI 'What can loan-level data reveal about US auto-loan ABS?' (loan-level fields beat pool-level for risk) - https://www.msci.com/research-and-insights/blog-post/what-can-loan-level-data-reveal-about-us-auto-loan-abs; Candidate-cited Intex and Bloomberg were not seen in results and are not re-asserted here
- Literature found by reviewer: SEC Regulation AB II final rule 2014 (asset-level disclosure via Form ABS-EE for auto loans/leases, RMBS, CMBS) - https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2014/09/24/2014-21375/asset-backed-securities-disclosure-and-registration; CarMax Auto Owner Trust 2020-3 Form 10-D EX-99.1 (delinquency buckets 31-60/61-90/91-120, charge-offs, recoveries, cumulative net losses, delinquency trigger flag; footnotes on the Oct 2021 servicing-system transition) - https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1814294/000181429421000060/a2020-3ex991111521.htm; CNH Equipment Trust 2021-C Form 10-D (monthly distribution report as Exhibit 99.1; depositor CNH Capital Receivables LLC) - https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1881859/000110465922004737/tm222766d1_10d.htm; Harley-Davidson HDFS transaction with KKR and PIMCO, July 2025 (sale of >$5B existing retail loans, forward-flow of ~2/3 of new originations for 5 years, HDFS retains ~1/3 on balance sheet) - https://powersportsbusiness.com/top-stories/2025/07/31/harley-davidson-to-sell-over-5b-of-existing-retail-loans-to-kkr-pimco/; Harley-Davidson Q2 2025 results press release announcing the HDFS transaction - https://seekingalpha.com/pr/20182362-harley-davidson-delivers-second-quarter-financial-results-and-announces-hdfs-transaction-with
- Data sources:
  - EDGAR company search atom for trust CIKs (declared User-Agent required) [PROBED 200 (Harley variant)] https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcompany&company=carmax+auto+owner+trust&type=10-D&output=atom&count=40 (update: Monthly 10-D per trust; history: Per trust from issuance; CarMax and Harley trusts since 2006, CNH Equipment Trusts since at least 2012)
