- Cost: $0.
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 4 / 1 / 1. Confidence: 8%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Export tons are largely contracted and often guided; cloud cover and the 5-day revisit can miss a Panamax that loads in one or two days; pad area to tonnage conversion is crude because pile height is unobserved; revenue surprises depend more on realized met-coal prices than on tons.
- Red team (reviewer): Berth occupancy from a 5-day-revisit optical sensor is undersampled by an order of magnitude relative to loading durations, and the terminals are shared with other shippers, so the measured series is mostly sampling noise plus third-party tons; whatever survives is a proxy for guided export volumes already in consensus. (Vendor/literature web check not run: search budget exhausted this turn.)

## N55 BLS PPI detailed-commodity indexes as full-quarter realized-price nowcasts for single-product chemicals, metals and packaging names

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Chlor-alkali and PVC, TiO2, carbon black, nitrogen and phosphate fertilizers, aluminum and steel products, glass containers, paperboard.. Target: revenue, margin, EPS surprise (nowcast).
- Seed item: [digital] Price-change frequency in B2B and industrial-distributor catalogs
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: (1) Narrow the universe to names where one US-priced product with a validated PPI code is at least half of revenue; the probe shows guessed codes can simply not exist, so the mapping is the real setup cost and must be checked per series. (2) Use first-release values: the API returns the latest revised value, not the vintage, so archive each monthly release on its publication day from now on and, for the backtest, document that revised values were used and bound the revision risk by comparing the archived first releases with later revisions as they accumulate (ALFRED coverage of detailed WPU codes is UNKNOWN). (3) Add a control for the sector ETF's own move on the PPI release day, since the release is a sector-wide event and the tradable part is the idiosyncratic residual. (4) Exclude global-priced products (TiO2 for Tronox, aluminum for Constellium) from the primary test unless the US PPI series is shown to track their realized price. (5) Confidence 30->25; crowdedness kept at 2 because PPI is a mainstream public release even though the quarter-average detailed-code test is not standard. Vendor and literature web check could not be run this turn (search budget exhausted); the ICIS/S&P/Argus/Fastmarkets claims in the spec are unverified here.
- Mechanism: Monthly PPI commodity indexes are built from transaction prices (contract and spot) that producers report for the week of the 13th; the month-3 index is released around the 13th-16th of the following month, before most Q3 materials earnings in late October to early November, so the full-quarter average realized-price proxy is known one to three weeks before results. For single-product producers (chlorine/caustic to Olin, PVC to Westlake, TiO2 to Kronos/Tronox/Chemours, carbon black to Cabot/Orion, nitrogen to CF/CVR Partners/LSB, aluminum sheet to Kaiser/Constellium, glass containers to O-I, paperboard to Graphic Packaging/Sonoco), quarter-average PPI change times guided volume approximates revenue change; the gap versus consensus revenue, which analysts build from spot assessments, is the signal.
- Industry context: Commodity chemical, metal and packaging producers have little quarter-to-quarter pricing power, so realized price times volume is revenue and price drops fall to margin with a lag. Producers themselves report transaction prices to BLS, so the index reflects actual contract resets rather than only spot quotes.
- Measurement window: Full quarter (three monthly PPI readings) as of roughly day 13-16 of the month after quarter end; initial PPI values are revised for four months.. Lead time: 10 days.
- Universe coverage: ~2-3%: 25-40 names where a single US-priced product with a matching 6- to 8-digit PPI code is at least half of revenue (e.g., OLN chlor-alkali, WLK PVC/caustic, KRO/TROX/CC TiO2, CBT/OEC carbon black, CF/UAN/LXU nitrogen, KALU/CSTM/CENX aluminum, OI glass containers, GPK/SON paperboard, CLF/CMC steel, ASIX, HWKN); the ~100-name figure overstates how many are single-product and US-priced (REASONED).
- Hypothesis: For companies with at least 50% of revenue from one US-priced product mapped to a validated PPI commodity code, a deviation of at least 3% between the quarter-average first-release PPI for that product and the price path implied by consensus revenue (consensus quarterly revenue change minus guided or trailing volume change), measured 10 trading days before earnings, predicts the sign of the revenue surprise and the close T-1 to close T+2 abnormal return versus XLB, controlling for consensus revisions, the sector ETF's reaction to the PPI release itself, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: PPI is a mainstream macro release, and chemical and metals analysts prefer ICIS, S&P Global Commodity Insights, Argus and Fastmarkets weekly spot assessments (paid), which lead PPI; the specific test of quarter-average detailed PPI against consensus revenue is not standard (REASONED). Priced-in share is high for spot-driven products and lower for contract-heavy ones (TiO2, caustic, cans, paperboard) where PPI captures contract resets.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): ICIS, S&P Global Commodity Insights (IHS Markit), Argus, Fastmarkets sell price assessments; nobody sells PPI, which is public.. Why ours differs: Transaction-weighted, includes contract pricing, free, full-quarter reading before earnings, and covers about 100 names at once; the measured quantity is the realized-price level, not the price-change frequency of the seed item.
- Data sources:
  - BLS Public Data API v1 (keyless, ~3 years) / v2 (free registration key, 20 years, 500 queries per day); example series WPU10 (metals and metal products); detailed chlor-alkali, TiO2, fertilizer, container and paperboard codes to be looked up in the PPI commodity database (REASONED) [DATA] https://api.bls.gov/publicAPI/v1/timeseries/data/WPU10 (update: monthly, released about day 13-16 of the following month; history: many commodity codes 1947+ or 1980s+ (REASONED); v1 keyless returns ~3 years)
  - BLS PPI commodity data finder (series lookup; not tested) [UNPROBED] https://data.bls.gov/PDQWeb/wp (update: monthly; history: same as API)
- Reviewer source checks: https://api.bls.gov/publicAPI/v1/timeseries/data/ -> 200 TESTED HERE: keyless POST with six detailed PPI codes. WPU0613, WPU0652, WPU101…; https://data.bls.gov/PDQWeb/wp -> 200 PPI commodity data finder loads; usable for the code lookup.; https://api.bls.gov/publicAPI/v1/timeseries/data/WPU10 -> DATA Listed DATA in the Phase 0 file (keyless ~3 years; free v2 key for 20 years).
- Cost: $0 (free BLS v2 key).
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 3 / 1. Confidence: 25%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): PPI is sampled, sometimes imputed and revised for four months, and may lag spot by a month; many 'single-product' names are mixed by product and region (TiO2 is global); consensus already embeds spot moves that lead PPI; and the PPI release moves the whole sector so the idiosyncratic residual may be small.
- Red team (reviewer): Analysts build revenue from weekly spot assessments that lead PPI by a month, so by T-10 the PPI quarter average is largely a lagged, sampled and partly imputed restatement of prices the market already saw; the measured 'gap versus consensus' is dominated by the error in reconstructing analysts' implied price and volume from guidance rather than by new price information, and whatever signal remains is a sector factor rather than an idiosyncratic surprise. (Vendor/literature web check not run: search budget exhausted this turn.)

## N56 FMCSA roadside-inspection count per carrier USDOT number as a monthly fleet-activity and safety-cost nowcast

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Asset-based truckload and LTL carriers, owner-operator networks, dedicated and drayage fleets. Target: revenue (miles, tractor count); margin (out-of-service and violation rates feeding insurance and maintenance) (nowcast).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: (1) Data: all three Socrata files now probed and working (status DATA); note insp_date is text, so monthly grouping needs substring, not date functions. (2) Add a targeting control: deflate by state-month inspection totals and by the carrier's level-1 share, because the Inspection Selection System raises inspections for carriers with worse SMS percentiles; Werner's 2025->2026 rise of ~30% (TESTED HERE) while its reported fleet has been shrinking (REASONED from company filings, not verified here) is the exact pattern that falsifies the plain exposure story and must be resolved against 10-Q tractor counts before any capital is risked. (3) Replace the MCS-150 power-unit denominator (Werner's is a 2024 self-report of 2023 mileage) with reported average tractors from the 10-Q. (4) Archive the file weekly from now to measure the month-3 completeness curve (Oct 2026 read 10-15% low at T+11). (5) crowdedness 5->4 (vetting vendors resell the same data; investors do not package it), signal_noise 3->2, confidence 30->22. Vendor and literature web check could not be run this turn (search budget exhausted).
- Mechanism: State and federal enforcement generate roadside inspections roughly in proportion to a carrier's highway exposure (tractor-miles, weigh-station passes), so the monthly number of inspections tagged to a carrier's USDOT number tracks its active tractor count and miles driven, which is the volume driver of truckload revenue (loaded miles x rate per mile) and LTL linehaul activity. A carrier parking trucks, losing drivers or shrinking its fleet shows fewer inspections in the same month; one growing a dedicated fleet or absorbing an acquisition shows more. Driver and vehicle out-of-service rates from the same records move with driver tenure and deferred maintenance, which feed claims/insurance and maintenance expense, a margin item carriers rarely guide. TESTED HERE: Werner (DOT 53467) ~370-630 inspections/month, Knight (428823) ~240-340, Marten (74432) ~170-340, Saia (29124) ~130-200, Heartland (134697) ~90-170, with data through 2026-11-08 in a file refreshed 2026-11-10.
- Industry context: Truckload carriers earn revenue per loaded mile, so the number of active tractors and miles driven in the quarter is most of the volume story, yet carriers only disclose fleet size quarterly. Every commercial truck on US highways is subject to roadside inspection, and each inspection is logged against the carrier's USDOT number in a public federal database.
- Measurement window: Calendar quarter, daily inspection records. Months 1-2 are complete well before earnings; month 3 is ~70-90% complete at T-14 because states upload within ~21 days (REASONED from SMS methodology), so use a vintage-adjusted month 3 or months 1-2 only.. Lead time: 14 days.
- Universe coverage: ~1.5%: 15-20 asset-based names (WERN, HTLD, MRTN, KNX, SNDR, CVLG, PTSI, ULH, SAIA, ARCB, XPO, LSTR via Landstar Ranger/Inway, HUBG, JBHT, R); backtest depth is only ~12 quarters (file begins 2023-11), so about 200 events.
- Hypothesis: A decline of at least 8% in quarter-to-date roadside inspections for a carrier's USDOT numbers versus the same quarter a year earlier, deflated by the same-period inspection count in the states where the carrier was inspected and adjusted for the carrier's own change in level-1-inspection share (a targeting proxy), using months 1-2 plus vintage-adjusted month 3 and measured 14 trading days before the release, predicts a negative revenue surprise and a negative close T-1 to close T+2 abnormal return versus IYT, controlling for consensus revisions over the prior 30 days, sector, size and momentum; the exposure link is validated first against reported average tractors and loaded miles in the 10-Q.
- Who already knows: Shippers, brokers and insurers use SMS/CSA data for carrier vetting; sell-side trucking analysts discuss CSA scores qualitatively. Nobody we know of publishes per-carrier inspection counts as a miles/fleet nowcast (UNKNOWN). Consensus for TL revenue is built from DAT/Cass rate indices and company guidance, so fleet-activity deviations are largely not priced until the release.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Carrier-vetting vendors (Carrier411, SaferWatch, Highway) resell FMCSA safety data to brokers and shippers, not as an investor activity signal (REASONED). No investor vendor known.. Why ours differs: Uses a free federal file as a per-ticker volume and safety-cost nowcast with ~2-day latency, something vetting vendors do not package for earnings prediction.
- Data sources:
  - FMCSA MCMIS Vehicle Inspection File (Socrata, data.transportation.gov) - TESTED HERE: 8.27M rows, insp_date 2023-11-10 to 2026-11-08, rowsUpdatedAt 2026-11-10; SoQL filter by dot_number works [UNPROBED] https://data.transportation.gov/resource/fx4q-ay7w.json (update: Appears continuous (records within 2 days of inspection) - REASONED from max date vs refresh date; history: Rolling ~3 years (2023-11 onward) in this file)

- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Inspection intensity is targeted: the Inspection Selection System sends more inspections to carriers with worse safety scores, so counts can rise while miles fall (TESTED HERE: Werner's monthly count rose from ~450 in 2025 to ~600 in 2026; whether that is miles or targeting is UNKNOWN until checked against reported tractor counts). Enforcement blitzes (CVSA Roadcheck in May) and state budget changes add noise, and rate per mile, not volume, often dominates the surprise.
- Red team (reviewer): Inspection counts are a product of exposure and enforcement targeting, and targeting moves with the carrier's safety scores, so a count change is as likely to reflect an ISS alert as a change in miles: Werner's inspections rose about 30% from 2025 to 2026 in the file while its fleet was reportedly shrinking, which is the targeting story, not the volume story. Even where exposure dominates, truckload revenue surprises are driven by rate per mile, which this data cannot see. (Vendor/literature web check not run: search budget exhausted this turn.)

## N57 Hawaii daily air-passenger counts (DBEDT) as a destination-level demand nowcast for Alaska Air's Hawaiian network and Matson

- Category: Physical. Sector focus: Airlines with concentrated leisure-destination exposure; Jones Act container shipping to Hawaii. Target: revenue (RASM on Hawaii routes); guidance (nowcast).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: Two-name universe in which the data measure the wrong thing: arrivals aggregate every carrier, so Southwest, United, Delta, American and the Japanese carriers' share shifts dominate what reaches ALK, and Hawaii is a minority of ALK's post-merger revenue (REASONED); load factor is a weak driver of unit revenue relative to fare, which the counts cannot see; ALK issues mid-quarter investor updates that pre-empt the signal. The only daily source is a Tableau Public embed with no sanctioned export (workbook and CSV routes 404, TESTED HERE), so the pipeline would be a brittle vizql scrape; the clean DBEDT monthly xlsx files arrive too late to add lead. Vendor/literature web check not run this turn (search budget exhausted).
- Mechanism: Hawaii publishes daily counts of arriving passengers by airport and origin, derived from the agricultural declaration forms collected on every arriving flight (REASONED), with only days of lag. After the Hawaiian merger a large share of Alaska Air's capacity touches Hawaii and Hawaiian/Alaska carry a large share of Hawaii-bound seats (REASONED), so quarter-to-date arrivals versus published seat growth nowcast load factor and unit revenue on a network mainland-focused analysts model coarsely. Matson's Hawaii container volume correlates more weakly with visitor-driven consumption. TESTED HERE: the DBEDT page loads and embeds a Tableau Public dashboard (Dashboard_DailyPax) that must be scraped; SFO's monthly by-airline traffic summary PDF (Sep 2026 posted Nov 2026) is a usable cross-check for ALK's SFO hub; TSA's throughput pages return 403 from this container.
- Industry context: Leisure routes to an island destination have no substitute mode, so arrivals counted at the airport equal the airlines' traffic on those routes. Hawaii collects a declaration form from every arriving passenger for agricultural inspection, which the state turns into a daily public count.
- Measurement window: Full quarter of daily arrivals by island airport (HNL, OGG, KOA, LIH, ITO), domestic versus international; posting lag ~3-7 days (REASONED).. Lead time: 12 days.
- Universe coverage: <0.2%: 2 names (ALK, MATX), with MATX only weakly linked.
- Hypothesis: A decline of at least 4% year over year in quarter-to-date domestic arrivals into Hawaii airports, net of year-over-year scheduled seat growth, measured 10 days before ALK's earnings release, predicts a negative unit-revenue surprise and a negative T-1 to T+2 abnormal return versus JETS/IYT for ALK, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Hawaii tourism statistics are public and covered by local press; ALK's analysts may cite HTA monthly data but rarely daily arrivals versus seat capacity. Airline investors mostly key off ALK's own mid-quarter investor updates, so a late-quarter Hawaii demand shift is only partly priced.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): No vendor known to sell Hawaii arrival counts; Cirium and OAG sell seat capacity, not arrivals (REASONED).. Why ours differs: Daily destination-level demand count matched against free seat-capacity data for one carrier's largest leisure network; no vendor product needed.

## N58 Gulf Coast unplanned petrochemical outage hours (TCEQ and LDEQ emission-event reports) as a nowcast of inland tank-barge and tank-car demand

- Category: Second-order. Sector focus: Inland tank-barge operators; chemical railcar lessors. Target: revenue and margin (barge utilization, spot rates) for KEX inland marine; guidance (nowcast).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: (1) Re-point the primary use of the data from the second-order Kirby barge-demand mapping (1 real name, loose coupling, term-contract volumes that defer rather than cancel) to the first-order effect on the site owner, where lost production hours map directly to the quarter's volume; keep KEX as a secondary aggregate. (2) Filter events to unit-shutdown/upset causes with duration above a threshold, since most emission-event reports are short flaring or maintenance events. (3) Collection: subscribe to and archive the RSS feed daily (explicitly offered for subscription; TESTED HERE, rolling ~7-day window) and obtain history through a Texas Public Information Act request for an extract of the emission-events database (REASONED to be routine and low-cost) rather than crawling the search form, because www2.tceq.texas.gov robots.txt disallows everything; legal_risk raised 2->3 to flag that the spec's tested path conflicts with robots.txt. (4) Louisiana (LDEQ) left out of the primary test until a query path is verified. (5) crowdedness 4->3 (large outages are reported by paid plant-status services and the press; small facility-level event-hours are not aggregated), signal_noise 3->2, confidence 25->20. Vendor and literature web check could not be run this turn (search budget exhausted); the ICIS/Argus/OPIS claims in the spec are unverified here.
- Mechanism: Kirby's inland fleet moves petrochemicals, refined products and black oil between Texas and Louisiana plants, and management routinely attributes weak quarters to customer plant outages and navigation delays (REASONED from company commentary). Texas rules require operators to report unplanned emission events within 24 hours to TCEQ, whose public database lists facility, start and end time, cause and type; Louisiana's LDEQ holds the equivalent. Summing event-hours at the largest barge-served crackers, refineries and chemical sites, weighted by their liquid-cargo relevance, gives a near-real-time count of lost liftings, which drives barge utilization and spot rates within the quarter; chemical tank-car utilization for GATX and Trinity follows more weakly. TESTED HERE: a POST search returned events through 2026-11-10 including Lubrizol Deer Park, INEOS Chocolate Bayou and LyondellBasell Bayport; the database runs from 2003-01-31; an RSS feed of recent events is offered.
- Industry context: Inland tank barges are the main way Gulf Coast chemical plants and refineries move liquid intermediates between sites, so when a plant trips, liftings stop and barges idle. Texas environmental law forces plants to report every unplanned release publicly within a day, which makes plant upsets observable in near real time.
- Measurement window: Full quarter of event-hours (events posted within 1-2 days of start); KEX reports ~4 weeks after quarter end.. Lead time: 10 days.
- Universe coverage: ~1.5-2%: 15-25 names that own major Texas chemical or refining sites (e.g., OLN, WLK, HUN, CC, TROX, KRO, ASIX, LXU, CBT, OEC, CVI, DK, PBF, PARR, GPK, IOSP; REASONED from public plant locations) as the primary first-order test; KEX retained as a single-name second-order aggregate.
- Hypothesis: A top-quintile quarter-to-date sum of unplanned emission-event hours whose reported cause is a unit shutdown or upset at Texas facilities owned by a universe chemical or refining issuer, relative to that issuer's own trailing eight quarters, measured 10 trading days before its earnings release, predicts a negative volume/revenue surprise and a negative close T-1 to close T+2 abnormal return versus XLB or XLE, controlling for consensus revisions, the quarter's product-price change, size and momentum; as a secondary test, the same event-hours summed over barge-served Gulf Coast sites are tested against KEX's inland-marine surprise versus IYT.
- Who already knows: ICIS, Argus and OPIS report major cracker and refinery outages to chemical and fuel traders on paid services; chemical-sector sell-side tracks large outages. Nobody we know maps outage-hours to barge demand for Kirby (UNKNOWN); Kirby's analysts mostly react to management commentary at the release.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): ICIS, Argus Media and OPIS sell plant-status and outage news for chemicals and fuels (REASONED that they offer such services); none sells a barge-demand nowcast that we know of.. Why ours differs: Free, systematic, facility-weighted event-hours from regulatory filings rather than paid outage news, mapped to one issuer's volume driver.
- Data sources:
  - TCEQ Air Emission Event Report database search (POST form, fields event_start_beg_dt/event_start_end_dt) - TESTED HERE: HTTP 200 with current events; note www2.tceq.texas.gov robots.txt is Disallow: / so keep volume minimal and prefer the RSS feed [UNPROBED] https://www2.tceq.texas.gov/oce/eer/index.cfm?fuseaction=main.searchresults (update: Continuous (24-hour reporting requirement); history: 2003-01-31 to present)
  - TCEQ emission events RSS feed (offered on the search page for subscription) [UNPROBED] https://www2.tceq.texas.gov/oce/eer/RSSDataFeed.xml (update: Continuous; history: Recent events only; archive daily)
  - Louisiana DEQ Electronic Document Management System (unauthorized-discharge and incident reports) - TESTED HERE: application loads; query path not verified [UNPROBED] https://edms.deq.louisiana.gov/edmsv2 (update: Continuous (REASONED); history: UNKNOWN)
  - EPA ECHO facility service (facility identifiers and locations for site weighting) [DATA] https://echodata.epa.gov/echo/echo_rest_services.get_facilities?output=JSON&p_st=TX&p_act=Y (update: Weekly (REASONED); history: Current snapshot)

- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Kirby runs high utilization on term contracts, so short outages defer rather than cancel volume; many emission events are trivial flaring reports unrelated to output; LDEQ coverage is incomplete; and management often pre-discloses outage impacts at conferences before the quarter ends.
- Red team (reviewer): The informative part of the series is bimodal: the few outages large enough to move a quarter are in the trade press and management's conference commentary within days, so they are priced, while the many small events are flaring reports with no volume consequence; what remains after filtering is a thin, lumpy series on a handful of names, and for Kirby in particular high-utilization term contracts mean short outages shift volume between weeks rather than remove it. (Vendor/literature web check not run: search budget exhausted this turn.)

## N59 FAA aircraft-registry nightly diffs: delivery, sale-leaseback and retirement timing for US airlines and lessors

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: US airlines taking deliveries; aircraft and engine lessors with US-registered assets. Target: capex; EPS surprise (gain on sale-leaseback); guidance (capacity) (both).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: The registry cannot identify the thing the hypothesis trades on: US airlines routinely register owned and debt-financed aircraft through owner trusts (Wells Fargo, Bank of Utah, Wilmington trustees; REASONED), so a registrant-to-trustee transfer does not distinguish a sale-leaseback from ordinary financing, and the spec's own falsifiable test counts exactly those transfers. Deliveries themselves are published monthly by Boeing and Airbus with customer names and in airline fleet guidance, so delivery timing is priced. Registrant history is not retained in the nightly file (only the current registrant and cert date), so the transfer series cannot be backtested until years of snapshots have been archived. OpenSky's free terms are non-commercial (AMBER) and would have to be dropped. Vendor/literature web check not run this turn (search budget exhausted).
- Mechanism: The FAA publishes every US-registered aircraft's record nightly (N-number, serial, model, registrant, certificate issue date, status). A new registration of an airline's serial number marks a delivery; a registrant change to a bank trustee marks a sale-leaseback closing; a cancellation marks a retirement or export sale. Delivery timing within the quarter drives capex, capacity and ownership cost, and sale-leaseback gains are a recurring reported EPS item for Frontier and Sun Country (REASONED from their filings). For lessors with US-registered fleets, registrant transfers reveal trades before the quarterly letter. Applies to US-registered operators; most of Air Lease's fleet is foreign-registered and not covered.
- Industry context: Airlines that finance new aircraft through sale-leasebacks book a gain when each deal closes, and the number closing in a quarter moves reported earnings. Every US civil aircraft must be registered with the FAA, and the registry is public and refreshed nightly.
- Measurement window: Quarter-to-date registrations, transfers and cancellations by serial number, final at quarter end; forward view from reserved N-numbers pending delivery.. Lead time: 20 days.
- Universe coverage: ~0.6%: 6-8 names (ALK, JBLU, ALGT, SNCY, ULCC, SKYW; WLFC partial), several near the $500M cap floor.
- Hypothesis: At least one more registrant-to-trustee transfer of newly delivered aircraft in the quarter than implied by the company's last delivery and financing guidance, measured 10 days before the ULCC or SNCY release, predicts a positive EPS surprise from sale-leaseback gains and a positive T-1 to T+2 abnormal return versus JETS/IYT, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Boeing and Airbus publish monthly orders and deliveries naming customers; ch-aviation and Cirium sell fleet databases; planespotters enthusiasts post deliveries; sell-side models deliveries from company guidance. Delivery schedules are priced; within-quarter slippage and the count of sale-leaseback closings are less so.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): ch-aviation and Cirium (Fleets Analyzer) sell fleet and delivery data; planespotters.net publishes free enthusiast data (REASONED that these exist as described).. Why ours differs: Free nightly registry diffs give exact certificate dates and trustee names that reveal financing structure, with no vendor licence.
- Data sources:
  - FAA Releasable Aircraft Registry zip (MASTER, DEREG files) [DATA] https://registry.faa.gov/database/ReleasableAircraft.zip (update: Nightly; history: Current snapshot; must be archived daily to build diffs)
  - Federal Register API (airworthiness directives affecting specific fleets) [DATA] https://www.federalregister.gov/api/v1/documents.json?per_page=1&conditions[term]=airworthiness%20directive (update: Daily; history: 1994 onward (REASONED))
  - OpenSky anonymous states (confirm first revenue flight of a newly registered tail) [DATA] https://opensky-network.org/api/states/all?lamin=24&lomin=-125&lamax=50&lomax=-66 (update: Live; history: Live only)
- Reviewer source checks: https://registry.faa.gov/database/ReleasableAircraft.zip -> 206 TESTED HERE: range GET returns 206, 73,044,598 bytes, last-modified Wed 11 Nov …; https://www.federalregister.gov/api/v1/documents.json?per_page=1&conditions[ter… -> DATA Listed DATA in the Phase 0 file.; https://opensky-network.org/api/states/all?lamin=24&lomin=-125&lamax=50&lomax=-… -> DATA Listed DATA in the Phase 0 file, but the file flags OpenSky free-account terms …
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 8%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Registration can lag delivery by days to weeks, trust names are ambiguous, airlines disclose fleet changes in monthly updates and 10-Qs, and the EPS effect is small relative to fuel and unit-revenue noise.
- Red team (reviewer): Trustee registration is a financing-structure artifact common to owned, mortgaged and leased aircraft alike, so the transfer count is noise with respect to sale-leaseback gains; the only clean signal in the file, certificate-issue dates for deliveries, duplicates OEM delivery reports that are free, earlier and already in consensus. (Vendor/literature web check not run: search budget exhausted this turn.)

## N60 Fab-cluster equipment trade flows: HS 8486 semiconductor-equipment exports from the US/Japan and imports into Taiwan/Korea/China as a destination-weighted nowcast for mid-cap semi-cap revenue

- Category: Physical. Sector focus: Semiconductor equipment and sub-systems. Target: revenue, guidance (both).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: HS 8486 flows are dominated by Applied Materials, Lam, KLA, ASML and Tokyo Electron, so a destination-weighted index is a large-cap shipment series with the mid-cap contribution buried as a few-percent residual that no weighting scheme can recover; the large caps themselves report and guide before most mid-cap prints (Lam in late October, ASML mid-October), which is a cleaner, earlier and free nowcast of the same information. Half of the named universe does not ship from the US at all. Data access from the container is also poor: Census needs a key, the Taiwan customs portal and both Korean customs hosts are unreachable (000), SEAJ is 403, and the Taiwan/KITA/Japan paths are unverified form-driven queries. Vendor/literature web check not run this turn (search budget exhausted).
- Mechanism: Semi-cap tools are large serialized capital goods that cross a border and are declared under HS heading 8486 (machines for semiconductor and flat-panel manufacture) and 8486.90 (parts). Official customs statistics publish those flows by partner country monthly: Taiwan MOF about 8 days after month end, Korea (KITA/customs) about 15 days, Japan MOF about 20-30 days, US Census about 35-40 days. Mid-cap US equipment makers concentrate in one process step and disclose geographic revenue in XBRL, so a destination-weighted index of HS 8486 flows (US exports to China for Axcelis, Onto, Veeco; flows into Taiwan/Korea for FormFactor, Cohu, Kulicke & Soffa, Ichor, Ultra Clean) nowcasts revenue recognized on shipment or acceptance. ACM Research ships from Shanghai so it is not captured by US exports but is by China imports from Taiwan/Japan of competing tools. Labels: Census endpoint TESTED HERE (returns Missing Key without a key); Taiwan/Korea/Japan portals TESTED HERE for page load only; data queries REASONED.
- Industry context: Nearly all semiconductor tools end up in a handful of fab clusters (Taiwan, Korea, China, Japan, US) and are declared under a single customs heading. Mid-cap equipment makers specialize in one process step and one or two regions, so country-level flows carry a larger share of their revenue signal than for diversified large caps.
- Measurement window: Honest coverage: US Census and Japan data give months 1-2 of the quarter before most late-Oct/early-Nov prints (month 3 arrives ~5 weeks after quarter end, often after the print). Taiwan imports give all three months by about the 8th of the month after quarter end (20-30 days lead); Korea by about the 15th (15-25 days). The 14-day figure is for the two-month US/Japan reading.. Lead time: 14 days.
- Universe coverage: ~0.5%: at most 5-6 US-shipping mid-caps (ACLS, ONTO, VECO, FORM, perhaps CAMT via Israel) are even visible in US export data; UCTT, ICHR, KLIC and COHU ship largely from Singapore, Malaysia and the Philippines (REASONED), and ACM from Shanghai, so they are absent from the US side.
- Hypothesis: A YoY change of more than 15% in two-month cumulative HS 8486 flows for a company's destination mix (weighted by XBRL geographic revenue shares), measured 10 days before the earnings date, predicts the sign of the revenue surprise versus consensus and the T-1 to T+2 abnormal return versus SMH, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Semi-cap sell-side tracks SEAJ billings and SEMI's paid equipment statistics and occasionally cites Korea/Taiwan import data, mostly for large caps (AMAT, LRCX, KLAC). Destination-weighted mapping to mid-caps is uncommon. REASONED: partly priced through the large-cap read-through; the residual for single-step mid-caps is less followed.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): SEMI sells industry equipment billings statistics (REASONED: WWSEMS-type reports); Panjiva and ImportGenius sell bill-of-lading shipment data (seed 22).. Why ours differs: Official customs aggregates are free and faster (Taiwan ~8 days) than bill-of-lading indexing, and they cover the US export side, which is absent from public bill-of-lading products since US export manifests are not public.
- Data sources:
  - US Census international trade API, exports by HS (same API family as the imports endpoint in the working file; TESTED HERE this run: returns 'Missing Key' page without a key) [KEY] https://api.census.gov/data/timeseries/intltrade/exports/hs?get=CTY_CODE,CTY_NAME,ALL_VAL_MO&E_COMMODITY=8486&time=2025-08 (update: Monthly, ~35-40 days after month end; history: REASONED: 2013 onward)
  - Taiwan Ministry of Finance trade statistics query system (TESTED HERE this run: page loads, HTTP 200 frameset; data query not verified) [UNPROBED] https://web02.mof.gov.tw/njswww/WebMain.aspx?sys=100&funid=defjspf2 (update: Monthly, ~7-9 days after month end (REASONED); history: UNKNOWN from this probe (REASONED: multi-decade))
  - KITA K-stat Korea trade by HS and partner (TESTED HERE this run: page loads HTTP 200; form-driven query not verified) [UNPROBED] https://stat.kita.net/stat/kts/ctr/CtrTotalImpExpList.screen (update: Monthly, ~15 days after month end (REASONED); history: UNKNOWN)

- Red team (reviewer): The signal is a proxy for the large-cap semicap cycle, which is already priced through AMAT/LRCX/KLAC/ASML/TEL results and guidance released before the mid-cap prints; any residual correlation with mid-cap surprises is the sector factor, not company-specific information, and the month-3 Census reading arrives after most prints in any case. ## N61 ads.txt and sellers.json supply-path census: Tranco-weighted authorized-seller share of SSPs and ad-tech intermediaries across top publishers

- Category: Digital. Sector focus: Ad-tech (supply-side platforms, intermediaries, native and app monetization). Target: revenue, guidance (both).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: Authorization is not spend. DIRECT lines for the major SSPs are near-universal and sticky across top publishers, so the Tranco-weighted DIRECT share has almost no variance quarter to quarter; the supply-path decisions that actually move SSP revenue happen on the DSP/curation side and are invisible in ads.txt. No usable history exists: web.archive.org is blocked from the container and Common Crawl coverage of /ads.txt could not be verified (every CDX query reset or timed out), so the signal would need 6-8 quarters of forward collection before any test. Ad-tech revenue is dominated by macro ad budgets already in consensus. Vendor/literature search could not be run (WebSearch budget exhausted this turn), so the crowding picture rests on REASONED prior knowledge only.
- Mechanism: Under IAB Tech Lab standards every publisher lists its authorized ad sellers in /ads.txt (apps in /app-ads.txt) and every exchange publishes its seller roster in /sellers.json; both files exist precisely so machines can read them. A weekly crawl of the top publishers (sampled by Tranco rank) counts, per SSP, the number and traffic-weighted share of publishers listing it as DIRECT versus RESELLER, and the net change in the SSP's own sellers.json roster. Supply-path optimization wins and losses (a large publisher dropping or adding PubMatic or Magnite) appear in these files weeks before they change revenue, and the authorized footprint is the base over which an SSP's take rate is earned. Applies to Magnite, PubMatic, Criteo, Taboola, Outbrain/Teads, Perion, Nexxen, Digital Turbine and Unity's LevelPlay via app-ads.txt. Labels: TESTED HERE for the three sample files; link to spend is REASONED.
- Industry context: Programmatic advertising routes each impression through supply-side platforms that publishers choose, and the publisher's ads.txt file is the switch that turns an SSP's access on or off. SSP revenue is roughly take rate times spend flowing through authorized paths, so changes in authorized footprint lead changes in revenue.
- Measurement window: Weekly snapshots through the fiscal quarter; features are the quarter-average and quarter-end DIRECT share, net sellers.json adds/removals, and share of the top-1,000 publishers. Final reading at T-1.. Lead time: 1 days.
- Universe coverage: ~1.5%: 15-20 ad-tech names
- Hypothesis: A QoQ change of more than 3% in an SSP's Tranco-weighted DIRECT-authorization share across the top 5,000 publishers, measured 1 day before the earnings date, predicts the sign of the revenue surprise versus consensus and the T-1 to T+2 abnormal return versus XLC, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Ad-tech specialists and research shops publish supply-path analyses (REASONED: Jounce Media and Adalytics publish such work; Sincera, which analyzed supply paths, was acquired by The Trade Desk in early 2025). Sell-side cites them occasionally but does not run a ticker-mapped time series. REASONED: lightly priced; ad budgets (macro) are what consensus tracks.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Jounce Media and Adalytics (research/consulting, REASONED); no mainstream alternative-data vendor known to sell this as a time series (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: Free weekly census with per-ticker time series and Tranco weighting, rather than periodic research reports; tracks DIRECT versus RESELLER status changes at the publisher level.
- Data sources:
  - Publisher ads.txt files, e.g. New York Times (TESTED HERE this run: 33 non-comment lines, 16 naming major SSPs) [UNPROBED] https://www.nytimes.com/ads.txt (update: Continuous; crawl weekly; history: None (archive); historical copies possibly in Common Crawl (REASONED))
  - PubMatic sellers.json (TESTED HERE this run: 6,525 sellers; 3,577 PUBLISHER, 2,651 INTERMEDIARY, 279 BOTH) [UNPROBED] https://pubmatic.com/sellers.json (update: Continuous; crawl weekly; history: None (archive))
  - Magnite (Rubicon Project) sellers.json (TESTED HERE this run: 3,213 sellers) [UNPROBED] https://rubiconproject.com/sellers.json (update: Continuous; crawl weekly; history: None (archive))
  - Tranco top-1M list for publisher sampling and weighting [DATA] https://tranco-list.eu/top-1m.csv.zip (update: Daily; history: Older lists retrievable by list id)
  - Common Crawl index for historical ads.txt snapshots (REASONED: coverage of /ads.txt paths must be verified in the CDX index) [DATA] https://index.commoncrawl.org/collinfo.json (update: Monthly-ish crawls; history: 2008 to Oct 2026)
- Reviewer source checks: https://www.nytimes.com/ads.txt -> 200 Reachable, plain text.; https://pubmatic.com/sellers.json -> 301 -> 200 Redirects to https://cdn.pubmatic.com/sellers/data/sellers.json which returns 2…; https://rubiconproject.com/sellers.json -> 200 Reachable.; https://tranco-list.eu/top-1m.csv.zip -> DATA Listed DATA in phase0 working file; not re-probed.; https://index.commoncrawl.org/collinfo.json -> 200/000 200 on one retry, connection timeout (000) on two other attempts; host unstable…; https://index.commoncrawl.org/CC-MAIN-2026-43-index?url=www.nytimes.com/ads.txt… -> 000 Connection reset by peer on three attempts across two crawl ids, one 504; Commo…
- Cost: $0 (a few thousand small text fetches per week)
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 3 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 8%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Authorization is not spend: most spend concentrates on a few lines regardless of listing count, DIRECT lines change rarely, and Google and large-publisher consolidation dominate; ad-tech revenue is mostly driven by macro ad budgets that consensus already captures.
- Red team (reviewer): The series is a near-constant: top publishers list every major SSP as DIRECT and rarely remove one, so any measured change is either a crawl artifact (sampling top Tranco sites, most of which are not ad-supported publishers) or a tiny roster shift that does not map to spend share. Revenue for MGNI/PUBM/CRTO is driven by ad-budget macro and DSP-side routing, both already in consensus; with no archive the hypothesis is untestable for about two years. Search for vendors/papers was not possible this turn; REASONED: specialist ad-tech research houses already publish supply-path analyses, and The Trade Desk holds supply-path data internally, so any real roster signal is seen by better-placed participants first.

## N62 FedRAMP Marketplace status transitions and reusing-agency counts, paired with FPDS awards, as a federal-bookings lead for SaaS and cybersecurity vendors

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Application and infrastructure software, cybersecurity with federal exposure. Target: revenue, guidance (both).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: 1) Replace browser automation of the SPA with GSA's public data repo (raw.githubusercontent.com/FedRAMP/marketplace-fedramp-gov-data): data.json daily, status changelog daily; no key needed. 2) Build the reusing-agency series from dated ReuseMapping/AtoMapping events rather than snapshot diffs; use fedramp-status-changelog.json (2012-2026) for status transitions and the repo's git history (daily since 2026-04-14) for point-in-time checks. 3) Drop SAM.gov from the spec until its endpoint and key handling are verified (404 here); it is optional anyway. 4) Add a regime-change control: the 2025-26 FedRAMP 20x re-architecture changed status taxonomy (JAB path retired), so counts must not be pooled naively across the change. 5) Treat the signal primarily as a guidance/next-quarter indicator (ratable SaaS revenue recognition) rather than a reported-quarter nowcast, and test both. 6) Restrict universe to names that disclose public-sector share. API keys needed: none for the repo, FPDS or USAspending; a free api.data.gov key only if SAM.gov is added later. Vendor/literature search not run this turn (budget exhausted); Deltek GovWin and Bloomberg Government as sellers of federal procurement intelligence are REASONED prior knowledge, not verified here.
- Mechanism: Federal agencies may only deploy cloud services that hold a FedRAMP authorization (or are In Process with an agency sponsor). GSA's public FedRAMP Marketplace lists each cloud service offering, its status (In Process, Authorized), impact level (Low/Moderate/High), authorization date and the agencies that have issued or reused the authorization. A transition to Authorized (especially High) unlocks procurement, and a rising count of reusing agencies is a direct trail of agency adoption that precedes contract obligations. Federal buying concentrates in the fiscal fourth quarter (July-September), so marketplace changes in the preceding 90 days, combined with FPDS award records naming the vendor (posted within days of award), nowcast and lead federal revenue. Applies to vendors with meaningful federal exposure: Tenable, Rapid7, Qualys, Varonis, SentinelOne, Okta, Dynatrace, Elastic, GitLab, Appian, Box, DocuSign, PagerDuty. Labels: marketplace TESTED HERE for page load only; FPDS/USAspending DATA; link to revenue REASONED.
- Industry context: The US federal government is the largest single software buyer and may only deploy cloud services that have passed the FedRAMP security authorization, so the marketplace listing is a procurement gate. Federal buying is concentrated in the last fiscal quarter (July-September), which makes authorization timing relative to that window matter.
- Measurement window: Trailing 90 days of status changes and reusing-agency deltas per vendor (final reading at T-1); FPDS award dollars with the vendor's name for the quarter, mostly posted by mid-October for the September quarter, 2-4 weeks before November prints.. Lead time: 1 days.
- Universe coverage: ~2.5-3%: about 30-40 software and cybersecurity names that have at least one Cloud Service Offering in the FedRAMP marketplace and disclose public-sector exposure
- Hypothesis: For software and cybersecurity names with at least one FedRAMP-listed CSO and disclosed public-sector revenue exposure, an increase of at least 2 reuse ATOs (ReuseMapping ato_date) or any status transition to Authorized in the 90 days before the earnings date, combined with FPDS obligations naming the vendor in the fiscal quarter above the trailing-4-quarter mean, measured at T-1 from the daily GSA data repo, predicts a positive next-quarter revenue-guidance surprise and T-1 to T+2 abnormal return versus IGV, controlling for consensus revisions, size and momentum; estimated separately for September quarters versus other quarters and for pre- versus post-FedRAMP-20x taxonomy periods.
- Who already knows: Vendors press-release FedRAMP High authorizations and sell-side federal channel checks discuss the budget flush, so headline authorizations are priced on announcement. The reusing-agency count as a time series and its pairing with FPDS obligations are seldom tracked (REASONED). Seed 16 (USAspending awards) is the lagging complement.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Deltek GovWin IQ and Bloomberg Government sell federal procurement intelligence (opportunity-level, subscription).. Why ours differs: Free, ticker-mapped registry time series focused on the authorization gate rather than opportunity intelligence; hypothesis is seasonal (September quarter only), which is cheap to falsify.
- Data sources:
  - GSA FedRAMP marketplace public data repo: data.json (products, agencies, ATO and reuse mappings with dates) [DATA] https://raw.githubusercontent.com/FedRAMP/marketplace-fedramp-gov-data/main/data.json (update: Daily (2-3 AM ET per README); history: Dated ATO/reuse events back to 2009; daily git snapshots since 2026-04-14 (629 commits))
  - GSA FedRAMP certification status changelog [DATA] https://raw.githubusercontent.com/FedRAMP/marketplace-fedramp-gov-data/main/fedramp-status-changelog.json (update: Daily (3-4 AM ET per README); history: 2,433 transitions, 2012-06-14 to 2026-11-11)
  - FPDS ATOM feed, vendor-name query [DATA] https://www.fpds.gov/ezsearch/FEEDS/ATOM?FEEDNAME=PUBLIC&q=tenable (update: Daily; history: FY2004 onward)
  - USAspending spending_by_award API [DATA] https://api.usaspending.gov/api/v2/search/spending_by_award/ (update: Daily/weekly; history: FY2008 onward (REASONED))
  - SAM.gov Contract Opportunities API (optional; endpoint returned 404 here with and without DEMO_KEY; needs free api.data.gov key) [PROBED_404] https://api.sam.gov/opportunities/v2/search (update: Daily; history: UNKNOWN)
