- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Authorizations are announced and priced immediately; agency-count changes are small and noisy; federal revenue is a minority of revenue for most of these names; and FPDS obligations are often recognized over multiple years, blurring the quarter mapping.
- Red team (reviewer): Reuse ATOs are typically issued after the agency has already decided to buy and often after the contract is awarded, so the series is coincident-to-lagging for bookings, and the sell-side already knows the September budget-flush seasonality; federal revenue is a minority for most listed names, so even a correct federal read is diluted; FPDS obligations are recognized over multi-year periods, blurring quarter mapping; the FedRAMP 20x re-architecture makes pre/post counts non-comparable; and under the September-only hypothesis there is roughly one event per name per year, so a 30-40 name universe gives a very small sample.

## N63 Daily gTLD zone-file counts via ICANN CZDS as a near-exact nowcast of registry and registrar domain bases (Verisign, GoDaddy, Wix)

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Internet infrastructure: domain registries, registrars, website builders. Target: revenue, guidance (nowcast).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: 1) Narrow the primary signal to Verisign; GoDaddy/Wix NS-share counts become exploratory because NS mapping misses third-party DNS (e.g. Cloudflare-hosted zones) and domains are a minority of their revenue. 2) Pull zone files weekly rather than daily to cut bandwidth (.com zone is several GB; REASONED) and compute counts on the fly, discarding files. 3) Add ICANN monthly registry reports (keyless, per-registrar .com/.net totals, net-adds and renewals, about 3.5-month lag) as the free historical backfill and validation set for both the Verisign base and registrar counts; they cannot serve as a T-1 signal because of the lag. 4) Add Verisign's quarterly DNIB page as a cross-check. 5) Legal: read and keep Verisign's CZDS zone-file access agreement; use is internal only, no redistribution; legal risk raised to 3 (AMBER) pending that reading. 6) Verify Verisign's market cap against the $30B ceiling before counting it in the universe. API keys/accounts needed: free ICANN CZDS account plus per-TLD access requests to Verisign for .com and .net (approval can take days to weeks and requires a stated purpose). Vendor/literature search not run this turn (budget exhausted); DomainTools-type zone-derived products are REASONED prior knowledge only.
- Mechanism: ICANN requires gTLD registries to provide daily zone files through the Centralized Zone Data Service to approved users. A zone file lists every delegated domain with its nameservers. Counting .com and .net domains daily nowcasts Verisign's domain name base, whose revenue is essentially base times a fixed, contract-set price, with net adds and renewal rate the only real quarterly variables. Counting domains whose NS records point to a registrar or builder's nameservers (GoDaddy's domaincontrol.com, Wix's wixdns.net, Tucows/Hover) nowcasts those companies' domain and site counts. Net adds equal new names minus deletions, both observable day by day. Labels: CZDS listed as KEY in the working file; NS-share mapping REASONED; Verisign market cap roughly $25-28B so inside the band (REASONED, verify).
- Industry context: Domain registries earn a fixed, contract-regulated price per registered name per year, so revenue is almost purely the count of names in the zone; registrars and site builders similarly bill per domain or per site. ICANN mandates daily zone-file publication, which exposes those counts to anyone who signs the access agreement.
- Measurement window: Full quarter of daily counts; the quarter-end base is known one day after quarter end, about 20 days before Verisign's print and 25-35 days before GoDaddy's and Wix's.. Lead time: 20 days.
- Universe coverage: ~0.2%: 2-3 names (Verisign if its market cap is at or below $30B, GoDaddy, Wix); roughly 10 events per year
- Hypothesis: Primary (Verisign only): a deviation of more than 0.3 percentage points between the CZDS-derived quarterly net change in delegated .com plus .net domains (weekly zone counts, offset-corrected against the last reported base) and the net adds implied by Verisign's latest full-year base guidance midpoint, measured 10 days before the earnings date, predicts the sign of the domain-base surprise and the T-1 to T+2 abnormal return versus XLK, controlling for consensus revisions, size and momentum. Secondary and exploratory only: NS-share-derived GoDaddy and Wix domain counts, validated against ICANN monthly per-registrar .com counts, predict the sign of the revenue surprise.
- Who already knows: Verisign discloses the base quarterly and gives annual base guidance; its few analysts estimate from registrar reports; domain-industry trackers publish daily counts for new gTLDs (REASONED). Whether quant funds use zone files is UNKNOWN. The nowcast is precise but Verisign is a low-volatility name, so the tradable gap may be small.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): DomainTools sells domain intelligence derived from zone data (REASONED); no ticker-mapped product known (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: Direct ICANN access is free; we track daily NS-share counts per registrar/builder and the Verisign base against its guidance midpoint, a mapping not sold as a financial dataset as far as known.
- Data sources:
  - ICANN CZDS zone files (.com, .net; free account, per-TLD approval; internal use, no redistribution) [KEY] https://czds.icann.org/ (update: Daily snapshots while approved; pull weekly; history: None provided; archive counts going forward)
  - ICANN monthly registry reports, .com transactions CSV (per-registrar total-domains, net-adds, renewals) [DATA] https://www.icann.org/sites/default/files/mrr/com/com-transactions-202507-en.csv (update: Monthly, about 3.5-month lag (202607 latest on 2026-11-11); history: Multi-year monthly files by TLD (index at https://www.icann.org/resources/pages/registry-reports))
  - Verisign Domain Name Industry Brief (quarterly cross-check) [DATA] https://www.verisign.com/news-insights/domain-name-industry-brief/ (update: Quarterly; history: UNKNOWN (archive on page not parsed))
  - RDAP for spot checks of registrar/nameserver records [DATA] https://rdap.org/domain/allbirds.com (update: Live; history: Current record only)
- Reviewer source checks: https://czds.icann.org/ -> KEY Listed KEY in phase0 working file: free ICANN account, per-TLD access requests …; https://dnib.com/ -> 403 Bot-blocked from the container.; https://www.verisign.com/en_US/domain-names/dnib/index.xhtml -> 301 -> 200 Lands at https://www.verisign.com/news-insights/domain-name-industry-brief/ (qu…; https://www.icann.org/sites/default/files/mrr/com/com-transactions-202507-en.csv -> 200 TESTED HERE: 343 KB, 2,933 rows; per-registrar total-domains, net-adds and rene…; https://www.icann.org/resources/pages/registry-reports -> 200 Index of monthly registry reports for all gTLDs.; https://rdap.org/domain/allbirds.com -> DATA Listed DATA in phase0 working file.
- Cost: $0 (disk: roughly 1-2 GB/day compressed if all large TLDs are kept; counts can be computed on the fly and the files discarded)
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 3 / 4 / 3. Confidence: 22%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Verisign's earnings reaction is tiny and dominated by pricing and buyback news, so a precise nowcast of a known quantity may not produce abnormal returns after costs; for registrars, NS mapping misses domains using third-party DNS (Cloudflare) and ignores hosting and commerce revenue that drives their stocks; CZDS terms may limit commercial use and must be checked.
- Red team (reviewer): A precise nowcast of a quantity whose consensus is already tight: Verisign guides the annual base, price is contract-fixed, and the stock is low-volatility, so even a correct base read yields a surprise too small to beat spreads and option premium in a 2-10 day trade; zone counts differ from the reported base (names without delegated nameservers and on-hold names are excluded), so the offset must be estimated from only a handful of quarters; with 2-3 names there are about 10 events a year, making any backtest statistically weak for years; and commercial use of CZDS data is terms-ambiguous (AMBER).

## N64 Pre-launch certification cadence: new FCC grants per grantee code (via fccid.io) with confidentiality-expiry dates, cross-checked against Wi-Fi Alliance certifications, as a SKU-slate and sell-in nowcast for consumer electronics

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Consumer electronics, PC and gaming peripherals, home networking and smart-home hardware. Target: revenue, guidance (both).
- Seed item: FDA inspection reports and Form 483s, FCC equipment filings, patent and trademark applications
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: The only usable source is a third-party mirror that throttles exactly the pagination a per-grantee census needs: fccid.io/JNZ page 1 returned 200 but /JNZ/page/2 returned 403 on two attempts, fcc.report returned 403, and the FCC primary (apps.fcc.gov) is blocked as expected, so per-grantee history is capped at the ~100 most recent filings and bulk crawling is likely to be blocked; fccid.io has no published terms. The launch-date claim is unsupported: the filing page shows only Short-Term/Long-Term confidentiality labels and a grant date, no expiry date, and short-term confidentiality is routinely requested for the maximum period rather than the planned launch date (REASONED). Wi-Fi Alliance results are client-rendered behind a Drupal solr form (browser automation, terms unknown) and Bluetooth SIG is a login wall. Grant counts conflate colour/regional variants, miss products filed under ODM grantee codes, and the slate-size-to-guidance mechanism is vague while launch timing is usually pre-guided. Vendor/literature search not run this turn (budget exhausted).
- Mechanism: Every RF-emitting consumer device needs an FCC equipment authorization before sale, and most also obtain Wi-Fi Alliance certification. Grants appear under the company's grantee code weeks to months before launch, often with a short-term confidentiality request whose expiry date is the planned launch date. Counting new grants per grantee per trailing 180 days versus the same window a year earlier, and counting confidentiality expiries that fall inside the reported or next quarter, nowcasts the number of new SKUs shipping; retail sell-in precedes launch by 2-6 weeks and new SKUs carry higher margins. A quarter with no new grants where the prior year had several implies a thinner slate and guidance risk. Applies to Logitech, Sonos, Corsair, Arlo, Netgear, Roku, Garmin (if within the cap band), Peloton, Cricut, Ubiquiti, Digi, Impinj. Differently measured from seed 15: counts per grantee with launch-date extraction rather than filing-level reading. Labels: fccid.io DATA; Wi-Fi Alliance TESTED HERE for page load only; Bluetooth SIG Launch Studio TESTED HERE returns an Auth0 login wall and is excluded.
- Industry context: Consumer hardware revenue is launch-driven: new SKUs trigger retail sell-in and carry higher margins, while aging slates lead to discounting. Regulatory certification is a mandatory, public and dated step between product finalization and launch, so it reveals the slate before management discusses it.
- Measurement window: Trailing 180 days of new grants per grantee code (final count at T-1) and the count of confidentiality expiries dated inside the reported quarter and the next quarter (45-180 days forward view).. Lead time: 1 days.
- Universe coverage: ~2%: 20-25 consumer-electronics and networking names, but only those filing under their own grantee codes would be covered
- Hypothesis: A YoY decline of at least 40% in the count of new FCC grants under a company's grantee codes over the 180 days before quarter end, measured 1 day before the earnings date, predicts next-quarter revenue guidance below consensus and a negative T-1 to T+2 abnormal return versus XLK, controlling for consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Gadget press reports notable FCC filings for marquee products and some hardware analysts note them for Apple or GoPro; the per-grantee count series and confidentiality-expiry calendar are not systematically quantified for mid-caps (REASONED). Lightly priced.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): None known to sell FCC-grant counts as a financial dataset (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: Per-grantee time series with launch-date extraction and YoY slate comparison, mapped to ~20 tickers, rather than filing-by-filing reading.
- Data sources:
  - fccid.io third-party mirror of FCC equipment authorizations (grantee pages at /<GRANTEE_CODE>, REASONED path pattern) [DATA] https://fccid.io/ (update: Daily (mirrors FCC grants, REASONED); history: Multi-year (UNKNOWN exact))
  - Wi-Fi Alliance product finder (TESTED HERE this run: HTTP 200, 320 KB HTML; results render client-side with no dates in static HTML, so browser automation is needed) [UNPROBED] https://www.wi-fi.org/product-finder (update: Continuous; history: UNKNOWN)
  - Bluetooth SIG Launch Studio listings (TESTED HERE this run: HTTP 400 with Auth0 login page; not usable without an account) [UNPROBED] https://launchstudio.bluetooth.com/Listings/Search (update: n/a; history: n/a)
- Reviewer source checks: https://fccid.io/JNZ -> 200 Logitech Far East grantee page, ~100 filings with grant dates 2025-06 to 2026-1…; https://fccid.io/JNZ/page/2 -> 403 403 on two separate attempts; paginated grantee history blocked.; https://fccid.io/JNZA00199 -> 200 Filing page shows Date of Grant 09/24/2025, Final Action Date, and Short-Term/L…; https://fcc.report/company/Logitech -> 403 Blocked.; https://apps.fcc.gov/oetcf/eas/reports/GenericSearch.cfm -> 403 FCC primary blocked, consistent with the known fcc.gov block.; https://www.wi-fi.org/product-finder -> 200 317 KB Drupal page; results via /search/solr form rendered client-side; no cert…; https://launchstudio.bluetooth.com/Listings/Search -> 301 -> 400 Redirects to https://authaz.bluetooth.com/u/login (Auth0 login wall).
- Cost: $0
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 3 / 2 / 2. Confidence: 8%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Grant counts conflate minor variants (colorways, regional versions) with real launches; many brands file under ODM grantee codes, so coverage is incomplete; launch timing is often pre-guided; and revenue depends on sell-through and pricing, not slate size.
- Red team (reviewer): A noisy proxy for a product cycle that management already pre-guides: YoY declines in grant counts mostly reflect ODM/grantee-code migration and variant filings rather than a thinner slate, revenue depends on sell-through and pricing of the existing catalogue, and gadget press already publicizes marquee filings, so whatever is informative is visible before the count is.

## N65 Open-crawl tag census: counting customer sites that embed a vendor's client-side snippet across monthly Common Crawl / HTTP Archive snapshots as a net-customer-adds nowcast for SMB SaaS, martech and commerce platforms

- Category: Digital. Sector focus: Martech, e-commerce platforms, CX software, SMB SaaS, website builders. Target: revenue, guidance (nowcast).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: 1) Make HTTP Archive the primary source (stable monthly origin set and Wappalyzer detections; needs a Google account and Google Cloud project, free BigQuery query tier) and demote Common Crawl to a cross-check because its per-crawl host coverage changes month to month (manufactures adds/churn) and its CDX API was unusable from here. 2) Narrow the universe to vendors with a mandatory customer-site snippet and revenue driven by customer count; drop Semrush, Similarweb, Zeta, Sprinklr and Braze (no mandatory web snippet or enterprise-count names where logos do not drive revenue), and HubSpot/GoDaddy only if inside the cap band. 3) Add a pre-registered control: the free series must add information beyond the publicly visible technographic trend counts that institutions already buy, otherwise the idea is a replica of a sold dataset. 4) Lower signal_noise to 2 because logo counts ignore ARPU and expansion, which drive revenue at Klaviyo and Wix, and snippet presence lags contract start and churn. 5) Keep the 10-day lead but verify per name that the final-month table is published before the print (REASONED: HTTP Archive monthly tables typically appear in the second week of the following month). API keys/accounts needed: Google account and Google Cloud project for HTTP Archive on BigQuery; none for Common Crawl or Tranco. Vendor/literature search not run this turn (budget exhausted); BuiltWith, HG Insights and Datanyze as technographic sellers are REASONED prior knowledge, not verified here.
- Mechanism: Many SaaS platforms require a public JavaScript snippet or hostname on every customer site (Klaviyo static.klaviyo.com, Braze web SDK, Yext pages, LivePerson lpcdn, Sprinklr, Wix static.parastorage.com, BigCommerce cdn11.bigcommerce.com, Global-e checkout, HubSpot js.hs-scripts.com, Amplitude, Zendesk widget). Common Crawl publishes monthly crawls with CDX indexes and byte-range WARC access, and HTTP Archive publishes monthly Wappalyzer technology detections for millions of sites; counting distinct registrable domains carrying a vendor's fingerprint across consecutive months, weighted by Tranco rank, yields gross adds, churn and net adds of live customers, the driver of subscription and GMV-based revenue for SMB-heavy platforms. Applies to Wix, BigCommerce, Klaviyo, Braze, Yext, LivePerson, Sprinklr, Zeta, Global-e, Amplitude, Semrush, Similarweb, GoDaddy. Labels: Common Crawl DATA; HTTP Archive UNPROBED; link to revenue REASONED.
- Industry context: SMB-focused software and commerce platforms grow mainly by adding customer sites, and each live customer leaves a public fingerprint on its web pages. Open web crawls make that fingerprint countable month by month without a proprietary panel.
- Measurement window: Three monthly crawls per fiscal quarter; the crawl covering the last month of the quarter is typically released 2-3 weeks after its window (REASONED), giving a full-quarter reading 10-20 days before most prints.. Lead time: 10 days.
- Universe coverage: ~0.5%: 5-6 names with a mandatory customer-site fingerprint and SMB-count-driven revenue (Wix, Klaviyo, Global-e, LivePerson, Yext, BigCommerce if above the cap floor)
- Hypothesis: For SMB-count-driven platforms whose product requires a client-side fingerprint on every customer site (Wix, Klaviyo, Global-e, LivePerson, Yext, BigCommerce), a QoQ change of more than 2% in rank-weighted HTTP Archive Wappalyzer origin counts, with the quarter's final monthly table available at least 10 days before the print, predicts the sign of the revenue surprise and the T-1 to T+2 abnormal return versus IGV, controlling for consensus revisions, size, momentum and for the publicly visible technographic trend count for the same vendor; Common Crawl counts serve only as a secondary cross-check.
- Who already knows: BuiltWith and HG Insights sell technology-adoption (technographic) data at scale and some funds buy it; Datanyze offers similar (REASONED). Sell-side rarely builds net-adds series from it. REASONED: partly priced through vendor data for the larger names; the open-data version is cheaper and reproducible.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): BuiltWith, HG Insights (technographics, sold at scale); Datanyze (REASONED).. Why ours differs: Free and reproducible from open crawls; monthly cadence aligned to crawl dates; Tranco-weighted adds and domain-level churn; HTTP Archive gives the same class of Wappalyzer detections at no cost with multi-year history.
- Data sources:
  - HTTP Archive monthly Wappalyzer technology detections on BigQuery (httparchive.all.pages technologies column; Google Cloud project required, free query tier) [KEY] https://httparchive.org/ (update: Monthly; history: REASONED: roughly 2016 onward for technology detections)
  - Common Crawl columnar index and WARC byte-range access (secondary cross-check) [DATA] https://data.commoncrawl.org/cc-index/collections/index.html (update: Monthly-ish crawls; history: 2008 to Oct 2026)
  - Common Crawl collection info (CDX API unstable from the container; prefer columnar index) [DATA] https://index.commoncrawl.org/collinfo.json (update: Per crawl; history: 2008 to Oct 2026)
  - Tranco top-1M list for weighting [DATA] https://tranco-list.eu/top-1m.csv.zip (update: Daily; history: Older lists by id)
- Reviewer source checks: https://index.commoncrawl.org/collinfo.json -> 200/000 200 on one retry, connection timeouts on two other attempts; CDX index queries …; https://data.commoncrawl.org/crawl-data/CC-MAIN-2026-43/warc.paths.gz -> 200 Works; use the columnar index on data.commoncrawl.org instead of the CDX API.; https://data.commoncrawl.org/cc-index/collections/index.html -> 200 Columnar index listing reachable.; https://httparchive.org/ -> 200 Site reachable; data lives in BigQuery. bq ls --project_id=httparchive from the…; https://tranco-list.eu/top-1m.csv.zip -> DATA Listed DATA in phase0 working file.
- Cost: $0 (compute: ~1M WARC range fetches per month if restricted to Tranco homepages, or BigQuery free tier for HTTP Archive)
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 18%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Technographic vendors already feed the same counts to institutions; crawl coverage changes between months create artificial adds and churn; logo counts ignore pricing and ARPU, which drive revenue for many of these names; and snippet presence lags contract start and end.
- Red team (reviewer): Technographic customer counts are a dataset institutions already buy at scale (REASONED: BuiltWith, HG Insights, Datanyze), and vendor-level trend charts are publicly visible, so the information is largely priced; the open-crawl version is cheaper but neither faster nor more granular. Logo counts miss ARPU and net revenue retention, which drive revenue for Klaviyo and Wix; snippet presence lags contract start and churn by weeks; and crawl-set changes between months create artificial adds and churn that will look like signal in a small 5-6 name sample.

## G01 Builder-named municipal permit issuances and final inspections / certificates of occupancy as a quarterly starts-and-closings nowcast for mid-cap homebuilders

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Consumer discretionary (homebuilding), building products, manufactured housing. Target: revenue (closings), guidance (starts/backlog), EPS surprise (both).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: 1) Vendor/literature check not performed: WebSearch budget was exhausted this turn, so Zonda, John Burns, BuildFax/ATTOM remain REASONED and unverified. 2) Coverage gate added: build the entity matcher only for builders with at least 25% of closings in covered portals; today's Austin-only evidence (3-8 TMHC finals a month) is far below that, so the first engineering step is adding Maricopa County, Collin/Denton County and the Texas suburban Accela/Tyler portals, not the matcher. 3) Phoenix is now a real source: ArcGIS layer 1 'Permits' has PROFESS_NAME and PER_COMPL_DATE (TESTED HERE) - add it as DATA/UNVERIFIED-for-builder-names. 4) Dallas contractor strings carry address/phone text and MM/DD/YY dates; add a cleaning step. 5) Signal-noise lowered from 3 to 2: final inspection is not a closing, and closings are the most tightly guided line, so the hypothesis is split: closings surprise (primary, likely small) and the T-1/T+1 return (secondary, expected weak). 6) Lead time cut to 18 days to reflect 3-5 week print lags and off-calendar fiscal quarters (KBH, TOL, HOV, LEN). 7) Drop downstream installers/distributors and MHS names from the pilot. 8) Keys to create (all free, optional): Socrata app tokens for data.austintexas.gov and dallasopendata.com (raise rate limits); no key needed for Phoenix ArcGIS, San Antonio CKAN, Wake Hub, Census BPS or EDGAR (declared User-Agent only).
- Mechanism: A homebuilder's quarterly revenue is closings; a closing cannot occur until the municipality records a passed final inspection or issues a certificate of occupancy, so counting final-inspection/CO records whose applicant or contractor field is one of the builder's operating entities (from 10-K Exhibit 21) gives a direct count of homes that became deliverable during the quarter, and permit issuances under the same names count starts that become next quarter's deliveries. The count is lot-level and recorded within days of the event, so the quarter-end tally is final 1-2 weeks after quarter end, before the builder prints 3-5 weeks after quarter end. Applies to production builders with concentrated metro exposure (Texas, Arizona, Florida, Carolinas) and, through them, to installers and distributors billed on closings.
- Industry context: Production homebuilders recognise revenue when a home closes, and a home cannot close until the local building department passes its final inspection or issues a certificate of occupancy, which it records in its permit system the same day. Builders operate through state-specific subsidiaries (for example 'Taylor Morrison of Texas, Inc.'), whose names appear verbatim in the permit applicant and contractor fields, so public permit portals carry a builder-level ledger of starts and completions.
- Measurement window: Full fiscal quarter of final-inspection/CO dates (closings proxy) and permit issue dates (starts proxy), restricted to the city-limit portals actually covered; quarter-end count final about 7 days after quarter end (REASONED from Austin max completed_date = 2026-11-09 on 2026-11-11, TESTED HERE). Census BPS place-level monthly (no builder names) lands ~18 days after month end and covers months 1-2 as a control.. Lead time: 18 days.
- Universe coverage: Pilot only: 4-6 builders whose 10-K metro mix puts at least 25% of closings in the covered portals (DFW city, Phoenix city, Austin, San Antonio, Wake County/Raleigh): candidates MTH, TMHC, LGIH, GRBK, CCS, DFH; roughly 0.4% of the 1,300-name universe until county and suburban portals (Collin, Denton, Maricopa, Williamson, Hays, Fort Bend) are added. Downstream names (IBP, BLD, BLDR) and SKY/CVCO are dropped until the builder leg validates.
- Hypothesis: For builders whose covered-portal share of trailing-4-quarter closings is at least 25%, a deviation of at least 10% between (a) builder-entity-tagged final-inspection/CO counts over the fiscal quarter in the covered portals, scaled by that trailing coverage share, and (b) the builder's closings guidance midpoint, measured 7 days after quarter end and at least 14 days before the print, predicts a same-sign closings surprise; the T-1 to T+1 abnormal return versus XHB/ITB is tested separately and expected to be weak, with permit-tagged starts in the quarter tested as a predictor of next-quarter deliveries guidance. Controls: consensus revisions over 30 days, 30-year mortgage rate change, peer prints already released, sector, size, momentum.
- Who already knows: Zonda and John Burns Research and Consulting sell field-survey starts and closings to the sell side and to builders (named in the brief; REASONED that they are the consensus input). BuildFax (Verisk) and ATTOM sell aggregated permit data (REASONED, not verified for this use). Sell-side models anchor on the builder's own closings guidance and backlog, mortgage applications and Census new-home sales; builder-entity-level permit/CO counts from municipal portals are not in previews (REASONED). Closings guidance is usually within a few percent, so much of the level is priced; the edge is the deviation versus guidance caused by cancellations and cycle-time slips in the last month of the quarter.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Zonda (starts/closings surveys), John Burns Research and Consulting (surveys), BuildFax/Verisk and ATTOM (permit data, REASONED). None of the brief's listed alternative-data vendors (Placer.ai, Earnest, etc.) sell builder-named CO counts.. Why ours differs: Daily, lot-level, free and tagged to the builder's own legal entity, versus monthly or quarterly surveyed or modelled counts sold at scale; ours measures finals/COs (deliverability) rather than starts, which is closer to the revenue line; and it can be re-run the morning after quarter end.
- Data sources:
  - Austin building permits (Socrata 3syk-w9eu) finals/COs with applicant_org, contractor [DATA] https://data.austintexas.gov/resource/3syk-w9eu.json?$where=work_class='New' AND permit_class_mapped='Residential' AND status_current='Final'&$select=applicant_org,completed_date,housing_units (update: Daily (candidate TESTED); history: 1980s+ (issue dates))
  - Dallas building permits (Socrata e7gq-4sah) new single-family, contractor text field [DATA (TESTED HERE 200, rows returned)] https://www.dallasopendata.com/resource/e7gq-4sah.json?$where=permit_type like '%New Construction%' AND land_use='SINGLE FAMILY DWELLING' (update: UNKNOWN; dates stored as MM/DD/YY strings; history: 2018+ (REASONED))
  - City of Phoenix Planning_Permit MapServer layer 1 'Permits' (PROFESS_NAME, PERMIT_NAME, PER_ISSUE_DATE, PER_COMPL_DATE, PER_TYPE_DESC) [DATA (layer metadata TESTED HERE 200; query endpoint UNPROBED)] https://maps.phoenix.gov/pub/rest/services/Public/Planning_Permit/MapServer/1/query?where=1%3D1&outFields=PER_NUM,PERMIT_NAME,PROFESS_NAME,PER_ISSUE_DATE,PER_COMPL_DATE,PER_TYPE_DESC&f=json&resultRecordCount=2000 (update: UNKNOWN; history: UNKNOWN)
  - San Antonio building permits (CKAN, CC-BY) [UNPROBED beyond catalog (TESTED HERE 200)] https://data.sanantonio.gov/api/3/action/package_search?q=building%20permits (update: UNKNOWN; history: UNKNOWN)
  - Wake County 'Building Permits' Hub datasets (ids bdfad82b15344d37beb28d7f90b6c4be, 6a9651b339b7413d8dc264f9ae1b3f6f) [DATA (search TESTED HERE 200; dataset download URLs UNPROBED)] https://data.wake.gov/api/search/v1/collections/dataset/items?q=building%20permits (update: UNKNOWN; history: UNKNOWN)
  - Census Building Permits Survey place-level monthly (control, no builder names) [DATA (TESTED HERE 200)] https://www2.census.gov/econ/bps/Place/South%20Region/so2509y.txt (update: Monthly, ~18 days after month end (REASONED); history: Decades)
  - EDGAR submissions API for 10-K Exhibit 21 builder subsidiary names [DATA] https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000320193.json (update: Annual; history: 2001+)
- Reviewer source checks: https://data.austintexas.gov/resource/3syk-w9eu.json -> DATA (phase0 host; candidate TESTED completed_date max 2026-11-09) Austin city-limit portal only; applicant_org/contractor fields present per cand…; https://www.dallasopendata.com/resource/e7gq-4sah.json?$where=permit_type like … -> 200 DATA (TESTED HERE 2026-11-11) Returns rows; contractor field is a free-text blob with address and phone (e.g.…; https://maps.phoenix.gov/pub/rest/services/Public/Planning_Permit/MapServer/1?f… -> 200 DATA (TESTED HERE) Layer 1 'Permits' fields: PER_TYPE, PER_NUM, PERMIT_NAME, PERMIT_STAT, PER_ISSU…; https://data.sanantonio.gov/api/3/action/package_search?q=building%20permits -> 200 (TESTED HERE) CKAN catalog reachable; resource URLs and fields still not inspected.; https://data.wake.gov/api/search/v1/collections/dataset/items?q=building%20perm… -> 200 (TESTED HERE) Hub search resolves two 'Building Permits' datasets (ids bdfad82b15344d37beb28d…; https://www2.census.gov/econ/bps/Place/South%20Region/so2509y.txt -> 200 (TESTED HERE) Place-level monthly BPS file; control only, no builder names.; https://www.census.gov/data/tables/time-series/econ/mhs/shipments.html -> 301 -> 200 at /mhs/latest-data.html (TESTED HERE) Manufactured housing shipments page moved; ~6-week lag per candidate (REASONED).; https://data.sec.gov/submissions/CIK0000320193.json -> DATA (phase0) Exhibit 21 entity dictionary; declared User-Agent required.; https://www.cclerk.hctx.net/applications/websearch/RP.aspx -> DATA (phase0; viewstate scrape) Deeds as a closings proxy for Houston-area; not yet exercised for builder grant…
- Cost: $0 (public portals; Socrata app tokens free). Engineering cost is the real cost: one adapter per portal (Socrata, CKAN, ArcGIS Hub/REST, Accela, Tyler) and a fuzzy entity matcher for builder LLC names.
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 2 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 18%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Coverage: the tested city portals capture a small slice of each builder's metro volume (suburban and county jurisdictions dominate), so the scaled count may be too noisy to beat the builder's own guidance; final inspection is not a closing (spec homes can pass final and sit unsold for months), and builders already publish backlog and guidance that embed the same cycle-time information, so the residual may be a proxy for regional demand already priced via mortgage rates and peer prints.
- Red team (reviewer): Closings are the least surprising line a builder reports: management guides them within a few percent and routinely meets or beats, while the T-1 to T+1 move is driven by orders, absorptions, incentives and gross margin, none of which a CO count measures. The covered city portals hold a small, unrepresentative slice of each builder's suburban volume, so the scaled count is dominated by coverage noise and entity-matching errors that are themselves correlated with region; any residual that survives is a proxy for regional housing demand already priced through mortgage rates, Census BPS and earlier peer prints. Vendor crowding is unverified today (search budget exhausted), but Zonda/JBREC-style builder-level starts and closings are REASONED to be standard sell-side inputs.

## G02 FDA drug-shortage list status transitions and competitor first-generic/ANDA approvals (openFDA) as a product-level volume and price nowcast for injectable and specialty generic manufacturers

- Category: Regulatory. Sector focus: Healthcare ex-biotech: generic injectables, specialty generics, 503B outsourcing, hospital pharmacy suppliers. Target: revenue, margin, guidance (both).
- Verdict: KEEP_WITH_CHANGES. Changes: 1) Vendor/literature check not performed (WebSearch budget exhausted); IQVIA/Symphony and ASHP remain REASONED. 2) The core defect is history: the shortages endpoint is current-state only, so any backtest built today from initial_posting_date/update_date has look-ahead and survivorship risk (edited or removed records). Change: start a daily snapshot job now (zero cost) and treat the signal as paper-trade-only until four quarters of point-in-time status exist. 3) Replace cumulative shortage-days with NEW entries/exits in the last 8 weeks of the quarter, which is the least-priced piece; persisting shortages are in guidance. 4) Restrict to concentrated-portfolio names (universe cut to 5-7). 5) Share-of-listed-NDC-presentations is a count of labels, not market share; keep only as a crude weight and say so. 6) Drop the accessdata CFM cross-check (bot-blocked). 7) Signal-noise lowered to 2; confidence to 14; lead time to 25 days. 8) Keys to create: optional free openFDA API key (api.fda.gov) to lift the 40 req/min limit for daily snapshots; no key for Drugs@FDA zip, first-generics page or CMS ASP files.
- Mechanism: For a generic injectable, quarterly revenue is set by how many competitors can supply the molecule-presentation: when a rival's presentation enters the FDA drug-shortage list as 'Currently in Shortage' or 'Available / limited', the remaining suppliers absorb hospital demand at higher net prices for the weeks the status persists; when a competitor's ANDA or a first generic is approved against a company's product, share and price fall from the launch date. Both events are dated, product-specific and public the day they happen, so summing shortage-days (weighted by the company's share of listed presentations of that molecule) and launch-days across the quarter gives a nowcast of the quarter's volume and price mix. Applies to companies whose revenue is concentrated in a few dozen hospital injectables or specialty generics.
- Industry context: Hospital injectables are commodity products supplied by two to five manufacturers each, with thin margins and frequent plant-quality disruptions; when one supplier drops out, the others sell every vial they can make at higher spot prices until the FDA lists the shortage as resolved. Generic entry works in reverse: the first additional approval on a specialty generic typically cuts price by 30-60% within two quarters (REASONED), so the dates of approvals and shortage transitions set the quarter's mix.
- Measurement window: Full fiscal quarter of daily shortage-status and approval events; the quarter-end reading is final the day after quarter end, and the companies print 5-6 weeks later. CMS ASP quarterly files (published ~6-8 weeks before the effective quarter, REASONED) give next-quarter Part B reimbursement per HCPCS code as a forward price leg.. Lead time: 25 days.
- Universe coverage: ~0.5%: 5-7 names whose 10-K names individual products at 10% or more of revenue or whose portfolios are concentrated in hospital injectables: AMPH, ANIP, HROW, AMRX (partial), PRGO (weak), plus Hikma/Fresenius/Teva (foreign or above cap) as controls. Hospital operators and distributors dropped as second-order.
- Hypothesis: For companies with at least one product at 10% or more of revenue, a NEW competitor shortage entry (status moving to 'Currently in Shortage') on a molecule-presentation the company supplies, posted in the last 8 weeks of the fiscal quarter and still open at quarter end, measured 5 days after quarter end and at least 20 days before the print, predicts a revenue surprise of at least +2% versus final consensus and a positive T-1 to T+1 abnormal return versus XLV; a competitor ANDA approval or first-generic approval on a product at 10% or more of the company's revenue, with a launch announced at least 30 days before quarter end, predicts the opposite; controls: consensus revisions, management's prior-call shortage commentary (dummy), sector, size, momentum. Backtests using only initial_posting_date and resolution update_date from current records are labelled coarse and are not trade-qualifying; trading waits for at least four quarters of live daily snapshots.
- Who already knows: IQVIA (weekly scripts/NSP) and Symphony Health are the sell-side volume inputs; both cover hospital injectables poorly (REASONED, as the brief states). ASHP/University of Utah maintain a separate shortage list used by hospital pharmacists; Vizient publishes shortage commentary. Specialist analysts watch the headline shortages (e.g. GLP-1, IV fluids) but do not systematically score each company's molecule-level exposure (REASONED). Management often flags shortage-driven upside on the prior call, so part of a persisting shortage is priced; new entries and exits in the last six weeks of a quarter are the least priced.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): IQVIA and Symphony Health (ICON) sell prescription and hospital purchase data at scale; neither sells a shortage-exposure index. No listed alternative-data vendor sells this.. Why ours differs: Free, daily, product- and company-specific regulatory exhaust that is causally upstream of the IQVIA volume number, available the day of the status change rather than weeks later, and buildable as a point-in-time panel by snapshotting.
- Data sources:
  - openFDA drug shortages endpoint (daily snapshot job to build point-in-time status history) [DATA] https://api.fda.gov/drug/shortages.json?limit=1000&skip=0 (update: Daily; history: Current state only; initial_posting_date back to 2012; full status history only from the day snapshots start)
  - openFDA Drugs@FDA endpoint and bulk zip (ANDA approvals by date and sponsor) [DATA (TESTED HERE 206)] https://www.fda.gov/media/89850/download?attachment (update: Weekly-ish; history: Decades)
  - FDA first-generic approvals annual pages [DATA (TESTED HERE 200)] https://www.fda.gov/drugs/drug-and-biologic-approval-and-ind-activity-reports/first-generic-drug-approvals (update: As approvals occur; history: Several years of annual pages (REASONED))
  - CMS Part B ASP quarterly pricing files (forward price leg, Part B codes only) [DATA (TESTED HERE 206)] https://www.cms.gov/files/zip/october-2025-asp-pricing-final-file.zip (update: Quarterly, ~6-8 weeks before effective quarter (REASONED); history: 2005+ (REASONED))
  - openFDA NDC directory (labeler to NDC map; crude presentation-count weight) [DATA] https://api.fda.gov/drug/ndc.json?search=labeler_name:%22Amphastar%22&limit=100 (update: Daily; history: Current)
- Reviewer source checks: https://api.fda.gov/drug/shortages.json?search=company_name:%22amphastar%22&lim… -> DATA (api.fda.gov host is DATA in phase0; candidate TESTED 2026-11-11) Current-state records only; no full status history, so a point-in-time panel mu…; https://api.fda.gov/drug/drugsfda.json?search=submissions.submission_status_dat… -> DATA (candidate TESTED) Sponsor-name phrase search unreliable; filter client-side.; https://www.fda.gov/media/89850/download?attachment -> 206 (TESTED HERE, range request served) Drugs@FDA bulk zip is downloadable unattended.; https://www.fda.gov/drugs/drug-and-biologic-approval-and-ind-activity-reports/f… -> 200 (TESTED HERE) HTML list; parse per year.; https://www.cms.gov/files/zip/october-2025-asp-pricing-final-file.zip -> 206 (TESTED HERE, range request served) Quarterly ASP zip downloadable; forward price leg for Part B codes only.; https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/scripts/drugshortages/default.cfm -> 302 -> /apology_objects/abuse-detection-apology.html (TESTED HERE): BOT-BLOCKED Drop as a cross-check; openFDA endpoint is the usable copy.; https://api.fda.gov/drug/ndc.json?search=labeler_name:%22Amphastar%22&limit=100 -> DATA (candidate TESTED) Labeler-to-NDC map for presentation-share weights; counts NDCs, not market shar…
- Cost: $0 (openFDA key optional and free; raises rate limit from 40 to 240 requests/minute per openFDA docs, REASONED).
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 3 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 14%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): Shortage-driven upside is capacity-limited (the beneficiary may already be running flat out, so status changes add price not volume) and management guides for it; the FDA list lags real supply conditions by weeks and 'Available' entries persist for years, so a naive shortage-days count is noisy; and the companies in the universe each have dozens of products, so one molecule rarely moves the quarter by more than consensus dispersion.
- Red team (reviewer): The quarter of a diversified generic maker is moved by a handful of large products whose shortage status management has already flagged on the prior call and trade press has already covered, so the signal is mostly a proxy for guidance; beneficiaries are capacity-limited, so a competitor's status change adds price on spot volume only, which GPO contracts dampen; and because the FDA endpoint keeps no status history, any in-sample result produced today is contaminated by look-ahead and survivorship, meaning the idea cannot be validated for at least a year.

## G03 State-regulator completion reports, drilling permits and monthly well production (Texas RRC, North Dakota DMR, New Mexico OCD) as an operator-level turn-in-line and volume nowcast for E&Ps and a FracFocus bypass for oilfield services

- Category: Physical. Sector focus: Energy: E&P (Permian, Eagle Ford, Bakken, Haynesville) and pressure pumping / completion services. Target: revenue (volumes), guidance, capex (both).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: Operator-level permits, completions and production are the core product of Enverus and Novi Labs, sold at scale to the same analysts who set consensus (REASONED; vendor search could not run this turn), and the candidate concedes it is 'not more granular, only cheaper'. The timing claim fails: Texas completion reports (W-2/G-1) may be filed up to 30 days after completion under RRC rules (REASONED), so a count taken 7 days after quarter end misses a large share of month-3 turn-in-lines, and ND production lags 6-7 weeks; operators also pre-release volumes mid-quarter. Data acquisition for the largest leg is unverified: the Texas nightly files sit behind a JSF GoDrive page with no direct links (TESTED HERE). No cheaper, faster or more granular version of a vendor-sold panel exists here, so the brief's exclusion applies.
- Mechanism: An E&P's quarterly production surprise is driven mostly by how many wells were turned in line (TIL) and when in the quarter; each completion is filed with the state regulator (Texas RRC Form W-2/G-1 completion data posted nightly, New Mexico OCD C-105 completion filings, North Dakota first-production dates in the monthly well file) under the operator's name, so counting completions per operator by date gives the quarter's TIL count and timing versus the operator's own guidance. Monthly well-level production filed with ND (and TX, with a longer lag) confirms the ramp. Completions per basin also measure the frac-crew work done, which feeds pressure-pumper revenue without needing FracFocus.
- Industry context: Shale wells produce most of their first-year output in the first months after they are turned in line, so a quarter's production is dominated by how many wells came online and how early; operators guide TIL counts but slip them with crew availability and weather. Every completion and every month's production must be filed with the state regulator, which publishes the filings under the operator's legal name.
- Measurement window: TX RRC completion data: nightly, so the quarter's TIL count is final within ~7 days of quarter end (REASONED from the dataset page, TESTED HERE). ND monthly production: ~6-7 week lag (TESTED HERE: 2026_08 file present, 2026_09 absent on 2026-11-11), so before an early-November print it covers months 1-2. NM OCD: completions/permits via ArcGIS REST services; lag UNKNOWN.. Lead time: 15 days.
- Universe coverage: ~3%: about 35-40 names. E&Ps under $30B with TX/NM/ND concentration: Permian Resources (PR), Matador (MTDR), SM Energy (SM), Magnolia (MGY), Vital (VTLE), HighPeak (HPK), Crescent (CRGY), Civitas (CIVI), Chord (CHRD, Bakken), Northern Oil & Gas (NOG, non-op), Comstock (CRK, TX Haynesville), Ovintiv (OVV), Coterra and Devon near the cap. Completion services: Liberty (LBRT), ProPetro (PUMP), ProFrac (ACDC), Nine (NINE), RPC (RES), Select Water (WTTR), Atlas Energy Solutions (AESI), Aris Water (ARIS); drillers via permits: Helmerich & Payne (HP), Patterson-UTI (PTEN), Nabors (NBR). Louisiana (SONRIS) and Pennsylvania portals are blocked or TLS-broken from the container, so Appalachia and Haynesville-LA names are out of scope.
- Hypothesis: A positive deviation of at least 10% in regulator-recorded completions per operator over the fiscal quarter (TX RRC completion dates plus ND first-production months plus NM OCD completion filings) versus the operator's guided TIL count pro-rated to the quarter, measured 7 days after quarter end (at least 20 days before the print), predicts a production beat versus consensus and a positive T-1 to T+1 abnormal return versus XOP, controlling for any mid-quarter operator update, consensus revisions, the WTI/Henry Hub move over the quarter, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Enverus and Novi Labs sell operator-level permits, completions and production (with cleaned operator names and type curves) to most E&P analysts and to the operators themselves; Rystad and Wood Mackenzie also model it (REASONED). Sell-side volume estimates are anchored on company TIL guidance and on mid-quarter updates, and the ND Director's Cut is widely read for Bakken names. So the operator-level TIL count is known to anyone paying Enverus; the free regulator feed matters for a fund without that budget, and the exploitable residual is the last-four-weeks timing of completions versus guided cadence, which the commercial feeds also carry. Edge is modest and must be measured against mid-quarter company updates.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Enverus (permits, completions, production, rig activity), Novi Labs (well-level analytics), S&P Global Commodity Insights and Rystad (basin models, REASONED). FracFocus-derived service-company panels are sold by Enverus and others (REASONED).. Why ours differs: Same-day regulator filings at zero cost, with operator-name matching done in-house from Exhibit 21; the Texas completion file carries the completion date and treatment interval that FracFocus-blocked workflows need. Not more granular than Enverus, only cheaper and uncensored by the container block on FracFocus.
- Data sources:
  - Texas RRC data sets page: 'Completion Information in Data Format' (ASCII, nightly), 'Drilling Permit Master and Trailer - Daily File' (nightly, with lat/long), 'Production Data Query Dump' (CSV, last Saturday each month); files served through mft.rrc.texas.gov/link/<uuid> [UNPROBED] https://www.rrc.texas.gov/resource-center/research/data-sets-available-for-download/ (update: TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: page 200 and lists nightly/monthly cadence; two MFT links returned a JSF download page (HTML), so unattended download needs the page's form POST or a browser; direct file fetch not verified; history: Permit master 'every application since' the 1970s (REASONED from page text); completions and production dumps are cumulative)
  - Texas RRC W-1 drilling permit public query (web form) [UNPROBED] https://webapps.rrc.texas.gov/DP/initializePublicQueryAction.do (update: TESTED HERE: 200 (form page); history: UNKNOWN)
  - North Dakota DMR monthly production by well and operator (xlsx, YYYY_MM.xlsx) [UNPROBED] https://www.dmr.nd.gov/oilgas/mpr/2026_08.xlsx (update: Monthly, ~6-7 week lag (TESTED HERE: 2025_08, 2025_09, 2025_10, 2026_08 all HTTP 200 at ~3.2 MB; 2026_09 404 on 2026-11-11); history: Monthly files back many years (REASONED; 2025 files present))
  - North Dakota DMR Director's Cut and statistics pages (Director's Cut returned 403 with both UAs; statistics page 200) [UNPROBED] https://www.dmr.nd.gov/oilgas/stats/statisticsvw.asp (update: Monthly (TESTED HERE: stats page 200; directorscut 403); history: UNKNOWN)
  - New Mexico OCD ArcGIS REST services directory (OCDView/Wells_Public, OCD_Query_Service, Venting_Flaring, C115B production-report layers) [UNPROBED] https://gis.emnrd.nm.gov/arcgis/rest/services/OCDView?f=pjson (update: TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: services directory 200 listing Wells_Public, OCD_Query_Service, C115B_* (C-115 monthly report) layers; field lists of Wells_Public not yet pulled. OCD Permitting Data pages are behind Cloudflare Turnstile (blocked).; history: UNKNOWN (C115B midstream layer holds 'past 13 months'))
  - EIA API v2 (EIA-914 monthly state production) as an aggregate control [KEY] https://api.eia.gov/v2/petroleum/crd/crpdn/data/?frequency=monthly&data[0]=value (update: Monthly, ~2-month lag; history: Decades)
  - Colorado ECMC data downloads page (DJ Basin operators: CIVI) [UNPROBED] https://ecmc.state.co.us/data.html (update: TESTED HERE: page 200; monthly production zip URL pattern not confirmed (co2025.zip 404); history: UNKNOWN)
  - Operator mid-quarter production updates (8-K Item 7.01 / conference slides) that the gap must be measured against [DATA] https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=8-K&count=100&output=atom (update: Continuous; history: form.idx backfill)
- Reviewer source checks: https://www.rrc.texas.gov/resource-center/research/data-sets-available-for-down… -> 200 (TESTED HERE) Page lists nightly completion/permit files, but the three mft.rrc.texas.gov/lin…; https://webapps.rrc.texas.gov/DP/initializePublicQueryAction.do -> 200 (TESTED HERE) Interactive form only.; https://www.dmr.nd.gov/oilgas/mpr/2026_08.xlsx -> 206 (TESTED HERE, range request served) ND monthly production workbook is downloadable; ~6-7 week lag.; https://www.dmr.nd.gov/oilgas/stats/statisticsvw.asp -> 200 (TESTED HERE) Director's Cut itself returned 403 in the candidate's probe.; https://gis.emnrd.nm.gov/arcgis/rest/services/OCDView?f=pjson -> 200 (TESTED HERE) Lists OCDView/API_Export, Brine_Wells, C115B_* services; completion-date fields…; https://ecmc.state.co.us/data.html -> 200 (TESTED HERE) Download URL pattern unconfirmed.; https://api.eia.gov/v2/petroleum/crd/crpdn/data/?frequency=monthly&data[0]=value -> KEY (phase0; free EIA key) Aggregate control only.; https://www.sec.gov/cgi-bin/browse-edgar?action=getcurrent&type=8-K&count=100&o… -> DATA (phase0) Mid-quarter operator updates to control against.
- Cost: $0; storage for nightly TX files and monthly ND workbooks (tens of MB per month). A browser-automation step may be needed for the RRC MFT download pages.
- Scores (crowdedness / signal-noise / legal): 1 / 2 / 1. Confidence: 8%.
- Strongest reason it is wrong (generator): The information is already sold at scale by Enverus and Novi and the operators pre-release volumes mid-quarter, so by the print the TIL count is largely in consensus; regulator filing lags (TX W-2 may be filed weeks after first production, ND production is two months behind) blur the quarter boundary; and production surprises are increasingly about well productivity and gas/NGL realisations rather than TIL counts.
- Red team (reviewer): By the print the TIL count is already in consensus through Enverus/Novi feeds and the operators' own mid-quarter updates, so any residual is a proxy for well productivity, gas/NGL realisations and commodity moves rather than completion counts; filing lags blur the quarter boundary in a way that is correlated with operator size and basin, creating a spurious sector/size loading.

## G04 Interstate pipeline informational postings: daily scheduled quantities at single-plant delivery meters as a gas-input run-rate nowcast for nitrogen, methanol, gas-fed chemical, gas-processing and LNG names

- Category: Physical. Sector focus: Materials and energy: nitrogen fertilizer, methanol, gas-fed chemicals, midstream processors, LNG export, merchant gas-fired generation. Target: revenue (production volumes), margin, guidance (nowcast).
- Verdict: REJECT. Reject reason: After probing seven EBBs not a single scheduled-quantity row has been retrieved: every reachable host requires reverse-engineering an ASP.NET postback or an Angular SPA, one is blocked (502), one path is invalid, and the Kinder Morgan terms of use PDF is unread (legal AMBER, set to 3). The plant-to-meter mapping is unverified and likely incomplete (Louisiana and Oklahoma plants sit partly on intrastate lines and multiple meters, REASONED), so the output ratio would be unstable. For the target names (CF, LSB, UAN, MEOH) EBITDA surprise is dominated by product price, not volume, and outages are reported by trade press within days, leaving little by the print. Point-level pipeline flow data is sold to gas desks at scale (Wood Mackenzie/Genscape, REASONED; vendor search could not run this turn). For an account under $10k this is a multi-stack scraping project with no retrieved data, unread terms and a second-order economic effect; not worth staking effort on. It could be revisited only if one EBB is found to expose a documented download endpoint with multi-year history for an LSB or MEOH single-plant point.
- Mechanism: Under 18 CFR 284.13 and NAESB WGQ standards every interstate pipeline posts, daily, the scheduled and operationally available quantity at each receipt and delivery point; for a plant fed by a single pipeline meter (an ammonia complex, a methanol plant, a gas processing plant's fuel/residue point, an LNG train's feed-gas meter), the scheduled delivery is the plant's gas consumption that day, which for a nitrogen or methanol plant is proportional to output and for an LNG exporter is proportional to cargoes. Summing the quarter's daily deliveries versus the plant's nameplate and the trailing year gives utilisation, and gaps of several days reveal unplanned outages before management discloses them. Applies to companies whose EBITDA depends on one to three large gas-fed sites.
- Industry context: Ammonia and methanol plants convert pipeline natural gas into product at a nearly fixed ratio (roughly 32-34 MMBtu per tonne of ammonia, REASONED), so gas delivered to the plant is output; unplanned outages at a single complex can remove 10-20% of a quarter's volume for a one- or two-plant company. Interstate pipelines are required by FERC to post scheduled quantities at every point so that shippers can see available capacity, which makes the plant's daily gas intake public.
- Measurement window: Full fiscal quarter of gas days; the reading is final the day after quarter end (postings are made for the next gas day and revised intraday, REASONED); single-plant chemical and fertilizer names print 4-6 weeks after quarter end. History retention per EBB is UNKNOWN; some EBBs allow date-range queries back several years (REASONED).. Lead time: 25 days.
- Universe coverage: ~1.5%: about 15-20 names. Nitrogen: CF Industries (CF; Donaldsonville, Port Neal, Verdigris, Yazoo City), LSB Industries (LSB; Pryor, El Dorado, Cherokee), CVR Partners (UAN; East Dubuque gas-fed). Methanol: Methanex (MEOH; Geismar). Gas-fed chemicals: Huntsman (HUN), AdvanSix (ASIX), Westlake (WLK, partial). Midstream processors with residue/fuel points: Kinetik (KNTK), Western Midstream (WES), Antero Midstream (AM), DT Midstream (DTM), Hess Midstream (HESM). LNG: New Fortress (NFE), Excelerate (EE), NextDecade (NEXT, once Rio Grande flows), Cheniere above the cap. Merchant generators (Talen TLN) are covered better by CEMS/EIA-930 (S07) and should be excluded to avoid overlap.
- Hypothesis: A deviation of at least 5% in the quarter's summed scheduled deliveries at a plant's delivery meter(s) versus the trailing 4-quarter average adjusted for announced turnarounds, measured 3 days after quarter end (at least 20 days before the print), predicts a same-sign production-volume and revenue surprise versus consensus and a same-sign T-1 to T+1 abnormal return versus XLB (or AMLP for processors), controlling for the quarter's benchmark product price change (Tampa ammonia, US Gulf methanol), consensus revisions, sector, size and momentum.
- Who already knows: Wood Mackenzie (Genscape) aggregates pipeline nominations and flows for gas desks, and other energy-data vendors (S&P Global Commodity Insights, BTU Analytics/FactSet; REASONED) sell point-level flow histories; power and gas traders watch LNG feed-gas and generator burns daily, so for LNG and merchant power the gas-flow number is fully priced. Chemicals and fertilizer sell-side analysts model utilisation from company commentary and industry price decks and do not, to my knowledge, pull plant meter flows (REASONED). CF and LSB disclose outages after the fact, so the within-quarter outage days are the least-priced piece for those names.
- Vendors selling similar (generator): Wood Mackenzie Genscape (pipeline flow data). Kpler and Vortexa cover LNG cargoes (the LNG leg is therefore crowded). No vendor is known to sell plant-meter deliveries packaged for chemical/fertilizer equity analysts (UNKNOWN).. Why ours differs: Plant-level daily series mapped to the equity, built from the free regulatory postings rather than a gas-desk subscription, and applied to fertilizer/chemical mid-caps where sell-side does not use flow data; the LNG and power legs are dropped as crowded.
- Data sources:
  - Kinder Morgan pipeline2 EBB: Operationally Available by Point, Capacity Log, Unsubscribed/No-Notice pages per pipeline code (TGP, EPNG, NGPL, SNG, etc.) [UNPROBED] https://pipeline2.kindermorgan.com/Capacity/OpAvailPoint.aspx?code=TGP (update: Daily per gas day with intraday cycles (NAESB); TESTED HERE 2026-11-11: HTTP 200, 176 KB, but the data grid is rendered by ASP.NET postback (viewstate) and the initial HTML carries no rows, so the puller must replay the form POST; history: UNKNOWN (page offers a Gas Day selector))
  - Kinder Morgan PipePortal (DefaultKM.aspx) - alternate entry [UNPROBED] https://pipeportal.kindermorgan.com/PortalUI/DefaultKM.aspx (update: TESTED HERE: 200 (354 KB DevExpress page); history: UNKNOWN)
  - Williams 1Line Transco informational postings (OAC page is an Angular app; JSON endpoint to be found in devtools) [UNPROBED] https://www.1line.williams.com/ebbCode/OAC.html?pipeline=transco (update: TESTED HERE: 200 (Angular shell, 44 KB); data API not identified; history: UNKNOWN)
